This is a stronger statement than QP2303 could make, which printed a complete 16 against two questions. total_marks is therefore carried as 16 per question BY RUBRIC, NOT BY PRINT: instruction 2 states all questions carry equal marks, six answered questions are required, and the paper prints 'Total Marks - 100'. Six equal questions against 100 gives 16.67, and every question in the solved corpus that does print a figure for this format prints (16). Six answered questions at 16 total 96 against the printed 100; that discrepancy is on the source copy and is reproduced, not corrected.

Understand this first

A ship has always produced information about herself - the noon report, the engine log, the sounding book, the survey record. What changed is that the instruments started reporting continuously and automatically instead of once a day by hand, and the quantity that arrives is now far beyond what a person can read. That is the whole idea: not that the information is new, but that there is so much of it, arriving so fast and in so many different forms, that the old way of handling it - a human reading a page and forming a judgement - stops working. So you need machines to do the reading, and the interesting question becomes what you ask them to find. The second idea is that there is a ladder of usefulness. At the bottom you are simply describing what happened, which is what a noon report always did. A step up, you ask why it happened. Higher again, you predict what will happen next - this bearing is running warmer each week and will fail in about two months. At the top, the system recommends what to do about it. Most of the commercial value people talk about lives on the upper rungs, and most of the difficulty in getting there is not clever mathematics but ordinary data that turns out to be wrong. The third idea, and the one that makes this an examination question in 2023 rather than a magazine article, is that the regulator had just started asking for the data too. Once a ship's compliance depends on a number calculated from her own reported consumption, the quality of that reporting stops being a commercial nicety and becomes a legal exposure.

Model written answer

1. The principles of Big Data - the brief introduction the stem asks for

The stem asks for this briefly, and that is an instruction rather than a courtesy. Two things need to be established before either limb can be answered: what makes data big, and what one actually does with it once it is.

Big Data is not simply a large quantity of data. The working definition is that data becomes big when it can no longer be handled by the methods that were adequate for it before - when the volume, the rate of arrival or the mixture of formats defeats a spreadsheet and a human reader. It is conventionally described by a set of properties beginning with V:

  • Volume - a modern main engine and its auxiliaries may carry several thousand instrumented points logged continuously. A single ship generates more data in a day than her noon reports produced in a year.
  • Velocity - the data arrives continuously and in some applications must be acted on immediately, rather than being reviewed at the end of a voyage.
  • Variety - it is not one kind of thing. Structured sensor readings, free-text defect reports, survey photographs, AIS tracks, weather fields and noon reports are all different in form and must be brought together to be useful.
  • Veracity - whether the data is true. This is the property that matters most at sea and is discussed at section 3.
  • Value - whether analysing it changes a decision. Data that changes no decision has no value however much of it there is.

The second half of the introduction is what is done with the data, because the four functions in limb (b) each need a different level of it. Analytics is conventionally described as a ladder, and each rung is harder and worth more than the one below:

RungThe question it answersA shipboard example
DescriptiveWhat happened?This voyage burned 412 tonnes
DiagnosticWhy did it happen?Consumption rose because hull fouling increased shaft power at the same speed
PredictiveWhat will happen next?At the present fouling rate the ship will miss her charter speed warranty within seven weeks
PrescriptiveWhat should be done?Clean the propeller at the next port call rather than waiting for drydock

That ladder is the frame for the rest of the answer. The regulatory limb below lives mostly on the first two rungs, because a regulator wants an accurate account of what happened. The commercial limb lives on the upper two, because an owner wants to know what to do.

2. Limb (a), opportunities - the regulations coming at this sitting had just become data-dependent

The opportunity is best understood by seeing what had changed in the eight weeks before this paper was sat. The revised MARPOL Annex VI adopted by resolution MEPC.328(76) came into force on 1 November 2022, and its chapter 4 requirements on carbon intensity became applicable on 1 January 2023. That is six weeks before this examination. The consequence is precise and it is the hinge of the whole limb:

Compliance itself became a calculation performed on the ship's own reported data. It is no longer a matter of holding a certificate and passing a survey. Three requirements make the point:

  • Regulation 27 requires ships of 5,000 gross tonnage and above to collect and report fuel oil consumption data for each calendar year, which is then aggregated by the Administration and submitted to the Organization. That reporting stream was already running before this sitting.
  • Regulation 28 requires the ship's operational carbon intensity to be determined against a required value, and the ship to be rated. The rating is computed from the consumption and distance data the ship herself reports.
  • Regulation 25 requires the attained EEXI of an existing ship to be not greater than the required EEXI, verified at the first periodical survey on or after 1 January 2023 - so at this sitting the fleet was in the middle of being verified.

The opportunities that follow are then straightforward to state, and each is a scoring point:

  • The same data serves compliance and management. A consumption and distance record accurate enough to be reported is accurate enough to be optimised against. An operator who instruments the ship properly gets the regulatory return and the operational improvement from one investment.
  • Compliance moves from reactive to predictive. Because the carbon intensity metric is computed over a whole calendar year, a ship that waits until December to look at it has lost the year. Analytics allows the year-end position to be forecast in February and the trading pattern adjusted while there is still time to change the outcome. This is the single most valuable opportunity at this particular sitting.
  • Verification becomes cheaper and less disputable. A continuous, timestamped record is easier to present to a verifier and to an Administration than a reconstructed one.
  • Technical decisions acquire evidence. Whether a particular energy-saving device or coating actually delivered what was claimed becomes measurable on the operating fleet, rather than a matter of the maker's brochure.
  • Fleet-level allocation. Where an operator controls several ships, analytics allows the trading pattern to be arranged so that each ship's carbon intensity stays where it is wanted - matching ships to trades rather than accepting the allocation that arises by accident.

3. Limb (a), challenges - and the marks are in the ones peculiar to ships

Generic difficulties - the cost of software, the shortage of data scientists - will be written by every candidate and score little. The marks are in the challenges that arise because this is a ship:

ChallengeWhy it is a shipping problem specifically
ConnectivityBandwidth at sea is limited and charged by volume. Sending every sensor reading ashore continuously is not affordable, so processing has to be split between ship and shore, and the split has to be designed.
Veracity and sensor driftA flow meter, a torsion meter or a draught gauge drifts, fouls or fails, and it does so silently. A fouled log gives a plausible speed that is simply wrong. Bad data that looks reasonable is more dangerous than no data, because it is acted on.
Granularity of the legacy recordThe noon report is one observation a day, manually entered, and it remains the baseline for many performance claims. Reconciling continuous automatic data against a daily manual record produces disagreements that have commercial consequences.
Split ownership of the dataOn one ship the owner, the technical manager, the charterer, the engine maker and the equipment suppliers each generate and claim data. Who owns it, who may see it and who may sell it is frequently unsettled, and it is a contractual question rather than a technical one.
Cyber exposureOnce compliance depends on a data pipeline, an attack on that pipeline is a compliance failure as well as a security incident. Connecting previously isolated machinery systems to a shore network to collect data is exactly what creates the exposure.
StandardisationEquipment from different makers reports different quantities under different names at different intervals. A great deal of the effort in any real project is spent making the data comparable before any analysis begins.
Competence and trustA system that recommends an action is only useful if the engineer believes it. An alert that is wrong three times is ignored the fourth time, when it is right.

Two of these deserve emphasis because they are the ones a well-prepared candidate can develop into real marks. The first is veracity: analytics amplifies whatever it is given, so a systematic error in a sensor becomes a systematic error in every decision taken downstream of it, and the discipline of validating instruments does not disappear with automation - it becomes more important. The second is data ownership, because it is the challenge that no amount of engineering solves. It is settled, if at all, in the charter party and the management agreement.

4. Limb (a), the future transformation of the industry

The stem prints 'future transformation or the shipping industry'. That is a keystroke error for 'of', and it is answered as of.

The transformation to describe is a change in the nature of assurance:

  • From periodic to continuous. Shipping has always assured itself by inspection at intervals - the annual survey, the vetting inspection, the drydocking. Continuous data makes it possible to know the condition of a ship between those events, and in time to let the evidence rather than the calendar determine when intervention is needed.
  • From documents to records. The unit of proof shifts from a certificate asserting a condition on a date to a dataset showing the condition over time.
  • From experience to evidence in technical decisions. Judgements that were made on the basis of what had worked before become measurable across a fleet.
  • New commercial questions. Once a ship's operational efficiency is a published, comparable number, it becomes a term of trade - it affects what she can be chartered for and by whom. The technical department's performance becomes commercially visible in a way it was not.

The honest qualification, which is worth a mark because most candidates omit it: this transformation was at its beginning at this sitting and not complete. The regulatory data stream had just started, no ship yet had a carbon intensity rating, and much of the industry was still assembling the instrumentation. An answer that describes the transformation as accomplished overstates the position at February 2023.

5. Limb (b), chartering

Chartering is where the ship's measured performance meets a contractual promise, and analytics operates on both sides of that meeting.

  • Performance warranties. A time charter typically warrants a speed at a stated consumption in stated weather. Whether the warranty was met, and if not by how much, is a data question that generates real money. Continuous data allows the owner to demonstrate compliance and the charterer to test it.
  • Speed and consumption claims. The classical dispute is whether an underperformance was the ship's fault or the weather's. Analytics settles it by combining the ship's own logged data with independent weather hindcast fields for the same position and time, and the resulting analysis is far harder to dispute than a noon report.
  • Weather routeing and good weather periods. Whether a period qualifies as 'good weather' under the charter definition determines whether it counts in the performance analysis at all. Data decides it.
  • Voyage estimation. Before fixing, the expected consumption and duration for a specific ship on a specific route in a specific season can be estimated from what that ship actually did, rather than from her design figures. This is a direct commercial advantage in setting a rate.
  • Position and market intelligence. AIS data analysed at fleet scale shows where competing tonnage is, how long ships are waiting at load ports, and how much tonnage is effectively out of the market - all of which inform the rate at which one is willing to fix.

The point to make explicitly, because it is the examinable one: the charter party is a contract about performance, and analytics changes the evidence available to prove performance. That is why it matters to chartering rather than merely being interesting.

6. Limb (b), operations

Operations is where the upper rungs of the ladder earn their keep.

  • Voyage optimisation. Speed, trim and route are chosen together against the forecast weather and the arrival requirement. The largest single operational saving available to most ships is avoiding a fast passage followed by waiting at anchor, and identifying that opportunity is a data problem.
  • Hull and propeller performance monitoring. Fouling is a gradual loss that is invisible day to day and large over a docking cycle. Normalising the ship's power-speed relationship for draught, trim, weather and current isolates the fouling component and shows what it is costing. That converts the decision to clean the propeller from a calendar decision into an economic one - clean when the cost of fouling exceeds the cost of cleaning.
  • Machinery condition monitoring. Continuously logged temperatures, pressures, vibration and performance parameters allow a developing fault to be recognised as a trend rather than as an alarm. A bearing running progressively warmer is a maintenance decision; the same bearing at its alarm point is a breakdown.
  • Condition-based maintenance. Overhauls driven by measured condition rather than running hours, which reduces both unnecessary opening-up and unexpected failure. This is the operational form of the same shift from periodic to continuous described at section 4.
  • The regulatory reporting stream. The regulation 27 data collection and the regulation 28 carbon intensity calculation are themselves operational analytics tasks, and doing them well is a compliance requirement rather than an option.

7. Limb (b), vetting

Vetting here means the commercial inspection regimes - the tanker and dry bulk inspection schemes under which a charterer satisfies itself about a ship before fixing her - and not class or statutory survey, which is a different thing.

What analytics changes:

  • The inspection is a snapshot; the data is a record. A vetting inspection observes a ship on one day. Continuous operational and safety-management data lets an operator demonstrate what the ship is like on the other three hundred and sixty-four, which is a stronger case than the snapshot alone.
  • Preparation and self-assessment. An operator can analyse its own observation history across the fleet, identify the deficiency categories that recur, and correct them before an inspector finds them. Recurring observations are the ones that damage a record.
  • Trend analysis across the fleet. Where the same observation appears on several ships it is a management-system failing rather than a ship failing, and only fleet-level analysis distinguishes the two. That distinction is exactly what the management self-assessment element of these regimes is aimed at.
  • Near-miss and incident analysis. Free-text near-miss reports are the classic under-used data source. Analysed at scale they reveal the precursor patterns that precede incidents, which is what a vetting regime is ultimately trying to assess.
  • Screening before the inspection. Charterers increasingly screen candidate ships against port State control history, age, incident record and inspection outcome before an inspection is even sought, so a ship's data record affects whether she is considered at all.

8. Limb (b), design of the vessel

The connection between operating data and design is a feedback loop, and describing that loop is the answer to this limb.

  • Designing for the operating profile actually observed. Ships have traditionally been optimised around a contract speed and design draught that many never operate at. Fleet data shows the speed, draught and trim distribution a ship of that type in that trade genuinely experiences, and the hull and propeller can be optimised for that instead. This is the single largest design consequence.
  • Sizing machinery to real demand. Measured electrical and auxiliary loads across a fleet inform the number and rating of generators, pumps and compressors, instead of accumulated design margins.
  • Evidence on energy-saving devices. Whether a duct, a fin or a coating delivered its predicted saving is measurable across sister ships, and that evidence feeds the next specification.
  • Structural and maintenance feedback. Recurring defect and repair data identifies details that fail in service, which is design information that only the operating fleet can supply.
  • The digital twin. A calibrated model of the ship maintained against her live data allows a proposed modification - a bulbous bow retrofit, a propeller change, a power limitation - to be evaluated numerically before it is bought. At this sitting that was the direct route to demonstrating an EEXI compliance option.
  • Retrofit as the near-term case. With the EEXI requirement biting on the existing fleet from 1 January 2023, the immediate design application was not the newbuilding but deciding which modification to make to a ship already trading, and data determined which option actually met the requirement.

The closing point, which ties the whole answer together: the four functions in limb (b) are the same loop seen at four different timescales. Operations acts on data in days, chartering in weeks, vetting over a year, and design over the life of the next ship. What makes the data valuable is that the loop closes at all.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks and three demands, one of which is unnumbered. The stem says 'with brief introduction on the principles of Big Data explain the following', so the introduction is marked work and not scene-setting. Candidates lose marks at both ends: some omit the introduction entirely and go straight to limb (a), and others write two pages on the four Vs and run out of time before reaching limb (b)'s four functions. Brief means brief.

The larger structural risk is treating the question as one essay about how useful data is. Limb (a) is regulatory and strategic; limb (b) is commercial and technical across four named functions. Four functions are named, so there should be four visible answers. An examiner marking limb (b) is looking for chartering, operations, vetting and design, and will not hunt for them inside a continuous discussion.

The temporal trap, and it is a real one

Limb (a) asks about upcoming regulations. It is February 2023. The regulations that are upcoming are the ones in MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4: the fuel oil consumption data collection under regulation 27, which was already running, and the operational carbon intensity rating under regulation 28, whose first rating year had begun six weeks earlier. Nobody had a rating yet. The EEXI verification under regulation 25 was happening at first annual surveys during that year.

What is not upcoming, and must not appear: the EU Emissions Trading System extension to shipping, which applied from 1 January 2024; FuelEU Maritime; the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, adopted 7 July 2023, five months after this paper; and everything later. A candidate who writes about emissions trading in a February 2023 answer is describing a scheme that had not begun.

The four Vs, and which two actually matter at sea

Volume, velocity and variety are the standard three and can be given in a sentence each. The two worth developing are veracity and value.

Veracity is the marine-specific one. Sensors on a ship drift, foul and fail, and they do it silently. A speed log reading two per cent low gives a plausible number that is wrong, and every efficiency calculation downstream of it is then wrong in the same direction. The engineer's discipline of checking instruments against each other does not disappear when the data becomes automatic - it becomes the foundation of the whole edifice. Plausible bad data is more dangerous than obviously missing data, because it gets used.

Value is the disciplining question: does analysing this change a decision? A great many shipping data projects have collected impressive quantities of information that changed nothing, because no one identified in advance which decision the analysis was supposed to inform.

The ladder, and why limb (b) needs it

Descriptive, diagnostic, predictive, prescriptive. The reason this belongs in the introduction rather than being decoration is that the four functions in limb (b) sit at different rungs, and saying so is a mark:

FunctionRung it mainly needsBecause
CharteringDiagnosticThe question is usually why did the ship underperform - her fault or the weather's
OperationsPredictive and prescriptiveThe value is in acting before the loss occurs, not in recording it
VettingDescriptive and diagnosticThe regime asks what the record actually shows, and whether a fault is a ship fault or a system fault
DesignDescriptive at fleet scaleWhat the operating profile genuinely is, across many ships and years

Hull and propeller performance - the technical core of the operations limb

This is the one place in the answer where an engineer can show real technical understanding, and it is worth doing properly. The problem is that a ship's fuel consumption at a given speed varies with draught, trim, wind, sea state, current and water temperature, and the fouling signal is buried under all of them. Isolating it requires normalising the observed power-speed relationship to a reference condition, so that measurements taken in different conditions become comparable.

Once that is done, the slow upward drift in normalised power at constant speed is the fouling, and it can be costed. The decision to clean the propeller or the hull then stops being a docking-cycle habit and becomes an economic calculation: clean when the accumulated cost of the added power exceeds the cost and off-hire of cleaning. That sentence is worth a mark on its own because it converts a technical measurement into a management decision, which is what this subject is examining.

Data ownership - the challenge that engineering cannot solve

Worth a short paragraph because few candidates raise it. On a single ship the owner, the technical manager, the time charterer, the engine builder and several equipment suppliers all generate or receive data. Who owns it, who may use it, who may sell an aggregated version of it, and whether the charterer may see the owner's machinery data are contractual questions. They are settled in the management agreement and the charter party, or they are not settled at all. This matters practically because performance disputes turn on access to the data, and a party who controls the data controls the argument.

Cyber risk belongs in this answer

The link to make is precise: collecting machinery data means connecting machinery systems that were previously isolated. That connection is the exposure. And because compliance now depends on the data pipeline, a successful attack on it is not only a security incident but a compliance failure - the ship cannot demonstrate what she is required to demonstrate. The ISM Code's safety management system is the vehicle through which cyber risk is managed, which connects this answer to Q5 on the same paper.

Vetting is commercial, not statutory

A common error is to answer the vetting limb with class and statutory survey. Vetting in this context is the charterer's satisfaction that a ship is acceptable - the tanker and dry-bulk inspection regimes and the associated management self-assessment. The distinction to draw is between the ship inspection, which is a snapshot, and the management assessment, which is about systems. Analytics helps most with the second, because a deficiency appearing on one ship is a ship problem and the same deficiency appearing on six is a system problem, and only fleet-level analysis tells them apart.

Design - lead with the retrofit case, not the newbuilding

The instinct is to talk about optimising a new hull. At February 2023 the live design question in the industry was not the newbuilding but the existing ship, because EEXI had just become applicable to the trading fleet. The immediate design application of data was deciding which compliance option - engine or shaft power limitation, a propeller modification, a bulbous bow retrofit, an energy-saving device - would actually bring a particular ship into compliance without destroying her commercial usefulness. Leading with that shows the candidate knows what year it is.

Common mistakes

  • Do not quote a figure for the volume of data a ship generates, the size of the market, or the percentage saving from any measure. Published figures vary enormously with assumptions and an invented one is worse than none.
  • Do not name commercial platforms or vendors. The question is about the technique, not the products.
  • Do not describe artificial intelligence in general terms. The question asks about data and its use, and a general essay on machine learning answers neither limb.
  • Do not write about emissions trading, FuelEU Maritime, or the 2023 GHG Strategy. None of them applied at this sitting.
  • Do not treat the transformation as complete. At February 2023 the regulatory data stream was weeks old and no ship had a rating.

Examiner traps

  • "Which regulations are the upcoming ones at this sitting?" The CII rating cycle whose first year has just begun, and the DCS reporting stream that feeds it. NOT the EU ETS, NOT the 2023 GHG Strategy, NOT any Net-Zero Framework - all three are future here.
  • "Has any ship been rated yet?" No. The first rating year is six weeks old and the rating is determined after the calendar year ends.
  • "Give me the four Vs." Volume, velocity, variety, veracity - and say at once that veracity is the one that decides whether any of it is usable at sea.
  • "Whose data is it?" The question the industry has not settled. Owner, charterer, manager, maker and platform all claim it, and engineering cannot resolve a commercial question.
  • "What does big data have to do with cyber risk?" Everything - the connectivity that moves the data is the attack surface, and it is managed through the ISM safety management system.
  • "Is vetting statutory?" No. It is commercial, and that is exactly why data moves the needle there faster than regulation does.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What data does your ship already send ashore every day, and who reads it?
  • Your noon report and your automated log disagree. Which do you trust, and what do you do?
  • How would you demonstrate that a hull cleaning actually paid for itself?
  • What would you need to measure before you could tell an owner whether to derate the engine?
  • Who owns the engine performance data on a time-chartered ship?
  • What is the first thing you would do if the ship's performance-monitoring PC would not boot?

Memory framework

Principles, then the two limbs. PRINCIPLES (1): the four Vs, and the ladder - descriptive, diagnostic, predictive, prescriptive. LIMB (a) in three moves: OPPORTUNITIES (2) - compliance has just become data-dependent; CHALLENGES (3) - the ones peculiar to ships, bandwidth, sensor drift, crew workload, ownership, cyber; TRANSFORMATION (4). LIMB (b) is four words in the stem's own order: CHARTERING (5), OPERATIONS (6), VETTING (7), DESIGN (8). If nothing else survives, keep four Vs, four rungs, four applications.

Regulation and source map

PointWhere it comes from

Uncertainty and the limits of what is asserted

No figure of any kind is offered for a data or analytics claim. No fuel saving percentage, no sensor accuracy, no bandwidth number, no market size and no adoption rate appears anywhere. Figures of that kind are vendor-sourced, ship-specific and move faster than any examination syllabus, and none could be supported from a held source.

No product, platform, class notation or software is named, and no claim is made about how many ships carry any capability. The answer is written on the mechanism - what the data is, what it is used for, and what limits its usefulness - rather than on the market.

The temporal boundary is the live risk on this question and it is stated rather than assumed. Limb (a) asks about 'upcoming regulations', and what is upcoming depends entirely on the sitting date. At February 2023 the answer is the CII rating cycle and its reporting stream. A donor written in 2025 reaches for the EU ETS, the 2023 GHG Strategy and the Net-Zero Framework, and all three are future here and are excluded by name.

Flashcards 10

Brief intro - the Vs and the analytics ladder. Limb (a): 2 opportunities against the regulations upcoming at this sitting, 3 challenges specific to ships, 4 transformation from periodic to continuous assurance. Limb (b): 5 chartering, 6 operations, 7 vetting, 8 design - in the printed order.

Three demands and the first is unnumbered; four named functions in limb (b) must each be visibly reached.

It can no longer be handled by the methods that were adequate before - volume, velocity, variety, and for shipping especially veracity and value.

A definition that is only 'a lot of data' scores nothing.

MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 - reg 27 fuel oil consumption data collection, reg 28 operational carbon intensity whose first rating year began 1 January 2023, and reg 25 EEXI verified at the first annual survey on or after 1 January 2023.

This is the whole temporal content of limb (a) and it is where a later answer goes wrong.

EU ETS for shipping (from 1 January 2024), FuelEU Maritime, and the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy (adopted 7 July 2023). All future at this sitting.

A twenty-five-month-later donor reaches for exactly these.

Sensors drift, foul and fail silently. A speed log two per cent low gives a plausible number that is wrong, and every calculation downstream inherits the error. Plausible bad data is more dangerous than missing data because it gets acted on.

It is the marine-specific challenge and it is where an engineer can show real understanding.

Normalise the observed power-speed relationship for draught, trim, weather and current so measurements are comparable; the residual upward drift in power at constant speed is the fouling. Then clean when the accumulated cost of the added power exceeds the cost and off-hire of cleaning.

It converts a measurement into an economic decision, which is what this subject examines.

Connectivity and bandwidth cost; sensor veracity and drift; noon-report granularity against continuous data; split data ownership between owner, manager, charterer and makers; cyber exposure created by connecting isolated systems; lack of standardisation between makers; competence and trust in the output.

Generic IT difficulties score little; these are the marks.

The charterer's commercial inspection regimes and management self-assessment - not class or statutory survey.

Answering with class survey answers a different question.

Owner, manager, charterer and equipment makers all generate or receive the data. Who owns, may see and may sell it is settled in the management agreement and charter party, or not at all - and performance disputes turn on who controls it.

Few candidates raise it and it distinguishes an answer.

Not the newbuilding but the existing ship - deciding which EEXI compliance option (power limitation, propeller modification, bulbous bow retrofit, energy-saving device) actually brings a trading ship into compliance without destroying her commercial usefulness.

EEXI became applicable six weeks before this sitting; leading with retrofit shows the candidate knows the year.

Unresolved
  • NO QUANTITY IS ASSERTED ANYWHERE IN THIS ANSWER - no figure for the volume of data a ship generates, no market size, no bandwidth cost, and NO PERCENTAGE SAVING for voyage optimisation, hull cleaning, trim optimisation or any energy-saving device. Published figures for all of these vary by an order of magnitude with the assumptions behind them, no held source fixes one, and this family of questions invites invented numbers. The mechanisms are explained and the magnitudes are not.
  • NO COMMERCIAL PLATFORM, PRODUCT, VENDOR OR CLASS-SOCIETY DIGITAL SERVICE IS NAMED. The question asks about the technique.
  • THE VETTING REGIMES ARE DESCRIBED AT ARCHITECTURE LEVEL ONLY. MIW holds no OCIMF, SIRE, TMSA or dry-bulk inspection scheme document. The distinction between the ship inspection and the management self-assessment is stated as a structural feature of such regimes generally; no scheme's question set, scoring, version or revision date is asserted.
  • NO CHARTER PARTY FORM OR PERFORMANCE WARRANTY CLAUSE IS QUOTED OR CITED BY NUMBER. No standard form is held. The warranty is treated at doctrinal level and the good-weather definition is described as a matter the charter defines, without asserting any particular definition.
  • NO WEATHER HINDCAST PROVIDER OR DATASET IS NAMED, and no claim is made about the accuracy of hindcast against onboard observation. That accuracy is itself the subject of the disputes described and no held source settles it.

Understand this first

A convention is only words until some government makes it real. The Organization that writes the conventions has no ships, no surveyors and no courts; everything it agrees has to be turned into national law and then actually enforced by the individual States that signed it. For most of the twentieth century there was no way of checking whether a State was doing that well, badly or at all. Some States ran serious administrations; others sold the right to fly their flag and did very little else, and the ships suffered for it. This Code is the answer to that problem. It sets out, in ordinary administrative terms, what a government has to have in place if it is going to be taken seriously - laws that actually reproduce what it agreed to, people competent to inspect, a way of dealing with those it delegates work to, a way of investigating when something goes wrong, and a way of knowing whether any of it is working. Crucially, it then became something a State can be audited against, so the question of whether a flag is doing its job stopped being a matter of reputation and became a matter of findings. The second idea is that a State wears three hats and the Code addresses all three. The same country is the flag of the ships on its register, the coastal State off whose shore other people's ships pass, and the port State whose harbours those ships enter. Each hat carries different work: as a flag you are responsible for your own ships wherever they are; as a coastal State you owe services and a response to what happens off your coast; as a port State you check other people's ships when they come to you. Keeping those three apart is most of what this question is testing.

Model written answer

1. What the Code is, and what makes it bite

The IMO Instruments Implementation Code - the III Code - was adopted by the Assembly as resolution A.1070(28) on 4 December 2013, and it revoked its predecessor, the Code for the Implementation of Mandatory IMO Instruments of 2011. It is a statement of what a Member State must have in place in order to give proper effect to the IMO instruments it has accepted.

The point that turns this from an administrative document into an examinable obligation is the next one, and it should be made early because everything else depends on it:

The Code is mandatory, and compliance with it is audited. The Code was made mandatory through amendments to the principal treaty instruments, and it is the standard against which Member States are audited under the IMO Member State Audit Scheme, which became mandatory on 1 January 2016. A State is therefore not merely encouraged to organise its administration in this way - it is measured against the Code, and the findings are real.

Two structural features of the Code should be stated at the outset because they shape every later answer:

  • The Code is organised in three parts by role - common areas, then flag States, then coastal States, then port States - which is precisely the division limb c) asks about.
  • The Code repeatedly requires a State not simply to have arrangements but to evaluate and improve them. That built-in review requirement is what generates the performance indicators in limb b).

2. Limb a), first demand - the objective of the Code

Stated close to the Code's own terms, the objective is to enhance global maritime safety and protection of the marine environment by assisting States to implement the IMO instruments to which they are Party, and by supporting the development of a State's own administrative capability. It is aimed at implementation and enforcement, not at making new technical rules.

Three elements of that objective are worth stating separately because each is a scoring point:

  • It is about giving effect to existing obligations. The Code adds no substantive safety or pollution requirement. It is about whether the requirements already agreed are actually delivered.
  • It applies according to each State's own circumstances. The Code acknowledges that States differ in size, fleet and capability, and asks each to view the Code in the light of its own position.
  • A State is bound only in respect of instruments to which it is a Contracting Government or Party. This is expressly said and it is the point most candidates miss. The Code does not oblige a State to implement a convention it has not accepted; it obliges it to implement properly the ones it has.

The last of these deserves a sentence of its own in an answer, because it disposes of a common misconception that the Code somehow universalises IMO obligations. It does not. It universalises the standard of administration, not the substantive conventions.

3. Limb a), second demand - the strategy recommended to the State

The Code recommends a strategy in three parts, and giving them as three parts rather than as one paraphrase is what earns the marks:

Part of the strategyWhat the State must actually do
1. PromulgateEnact national legislation and administrative instruments that give effect to the instruments it is Party to - and give effect to them fully and completely, not selectively. This means the national law must actually reproduce the obligation, not gesture at it.
2. EnforceEstablish the administrative machinery to implement and enforce what it has enacted - the surveyors, the inspectors, the records, the sanctions, the delegation arrangements and the oversight of anyone the work is delegated to.
3. Evaluate and improveContinuously assess its own performance against measured results, identify shortcomings, and correct them. The Code makes review a standing obligation rather than an occasional exercise.

Two further duties sit alongside the strategy and are worth adding:

  • The State must promulgate laws and regulations and take the other steps necessary to give the instruments full and complete effect, so that safety of life at sea and protection of the environment are assured.
  • The Code carries the no-transfer-of-pollution principle - in giving effect to environmental obligations, a State must not simply move the harm from one medium or place to another.

The Code also sets out what a State should do when a new or amended instrument enters into force: review and update the national law, notify those affected, provide the administrative arrangements to implement it, and train the people who will apply it. That is the strategy in operation rather than in principle, and mentioning it shows the Code has been read.

4. Limb a), third demand - which IMO instruments the Code covers

This is the demand candidates drop, and at this sitting it is also the one where the citation matters.

The Code applies to the mandatory IMO instruments - the treaty instruments that are subject to the audit scheme. In substance they are the following families, together with their Protocols where those are separate instruments:

InstrumentSubject
SOLAS 1974 and the 1988 ProtocolSafety of life at sea - construction, equipment, operation
MARPOL and the 1997 ProtocolPrevention of pollution from ships, all annexes as accepted
STCW 1978 and the STCW CodeTraining, certification and watchkeeping of seafarers
Load Lines 1966 and the 1988 ProtocolAssignment of freeboard and conditions of assignment
COLREG 1972Collision regulations
TONNAGE 1969Measurement of tonnage

The Organization also maintains a non-exhaustive list of the obligations arising under those instruments, so that a State and an auditor can see what is actually being audited. At this sitting that list is the one adopted by the thirty-second Assembly.

A caution on the count. The number of instruments is commonly given as nine, but that figure depends on whether the Protocols are counted as separate instruments from their parent conventions. It is safer to name the families and note the Protocols than to assert a bare number, and an answer that does so is more accurate rather than less.

5. Limb b) - key performance indicators for flag State performance

The Code requires a flag State to evaluate and review its own performance, and it sets out the criteria by which that evaluation is to be made. These are the key performance indicators the question asks for, and they are the Code's own - not general management metrics.

The State should measure itself on:

  • Casualty record - the frequency and severity of casualties involving its ships, so that trends can be detected rather than incidents merely counted.
  • Port State control detention and deficiency record - the rate at which its ships are detained and the deficiencies found on them abroad. This is the most externally visible indicator a flag has, because it is measured by other States.
  • Survey and certification performance - whether statutory surveys are carried out when due and whether certificates are issued properly and on a sound basis.
  • Performance of recognized organizations - the results of the oversight programme over the ROs to which work has been delegated, since a flag remains responsible for what its ROs do in its name.
  • Casualty investigation performance - whether investigations are carried out where required, completed, and reported to the Organization.
  • Reporting to the Organization - whether the State submits the information the instruments require of it, on time and completely.
  • Legislation currency - whether national law has been kept up to date with amendments to the instruments.
  • Resources and training - whether the administration has, and maintains, sufficient qualified people for the size and nature of its fleet.

The Code's point about these indicators is as important as the list: they exist so the State can identify shortcomings and act on them. An indicator that is measured and not acted upon does not satisfy the Code, because the third part of the strategy is improvement and not merely measurement.

6. Limb c), first role - flag State responsibilities

This is the longest part of the Code and the answer should reflect that, but it organises cleanly under six heads:

HeadThe responsibility
ImplementationGive full and complete effect in national law to the instruments the State is Party to, and establish a programme to ensure its ships comply - the minimum safety and environmental protection programme covering survey, certification, manning, casualty investigation and enforcement.
Resources and organisationMaintain an administration with sufficient competent people, defined responsibilities and documented procedures, appropriate to the size and nature of the fleet.
Delegation to recognized organizationsWhere survey and certification work is delegated, the RO must be formally authorised under a written agreement, the scope of the delegation must be defined, and the flag must oversee the RO's performance. Responsibility is not transferred with the work.
EnforcementInvestigate non-compliance, take action against its ships and their operators, provide adequate sanctions of sufficient severity to be a deterrent, and detain a ship where necessary until she can proceed without unreasonable danger.
SurveyorsFlag State surveyors must be qualified, trained, subject to documented procedures, and identifiable - the Code addresses their qualification, their authority and their identification at length.
Casualty investigation and reportingInvestigate marine casualties involving its ships in accordance with the applicable requirements, and report the results to the Organization. Investigation is for the purpose of preventing recurrence, not for apportioning blame.

The delegation head is the one to develop if time allows, because it is where flag States most often fail an audit. The Code's position is unambiguous: a State may delegate the work to a recognized organization, but it retains the responsibility, and it must therefore have an oversight programme capable of telling it whether the delegated work is being done properly.

7. Limb c), second and third roles - coastal State and port State

These are shorter than the flag State part and are best contrasted directly, because the distinction between them is what the limb is testing.

Coastal State. The Code addresses what a coastal State should provide to shipping off its coast - it is a question of services and response, not of jurisdictional zones. A coastal State should:

  • provide and maintain aids to navigation, and vessel traffic services where the traffic warrants them;
  • provide hydrographic services and up-to-date charts, and promulgate navigational warnings;
  • provide meteorological services and weather warnings;
  • provide a search and rescue capability, and arrangements for responding to casualties and to pollution incidents in the waters for which it is responsible;
  • provide places of refuge arrangements and the means to respond to a ship in need of assistance;
  • conduct casualty investigation where a casualty occurs in its waters, and cooperate with other substantially interested States;
  • report to the Organization as required, and evaluate and improve its own performance in these services.

The error to avoid is answering this limb with the UNCLOS maritime zones. The Code is not about how far a coastal State's jurisdiction extends; it is about what the State does with it.

Port State. The Code addresses the State's control over other people's ships entering its ports:

  • establish and maintain a port State control capability, with procedures and trained officers, to verify that visiting ships comply with the applicable instruments;
  • apply the no more favourable treatment principle - a ship whose flag State is not Party to an instrument is not to be treated more favourably than a ship whose flag State is. Without this, non-ratification would be an advantage, and the whole convention system would leak;
  • ensure port State control officers are qualified, trained, authorised and independent - independent of commercial interests in the port and in the ships they inspect, which the Code addresses expressly;
  • take appropriate action where deficiencies are found, up to and including detention where the ship is unfit to proceed to sea without danger, and inform the flag State and the recognized organization concerned;
  • avoid undue delay to the ship, and provide for compensation where a ship is unduly detained;
  • report inspections and detentions, including through the regional port State control arrangements, and evaluate and improve its own performance.

The closing observation that ties limb c) together: the three roles are held by the same State at the same time, and the Code expects each to be discharged independently of the others. A State's failures as a flag are not excused by its diligence as a port State, and the audit looks at all three.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks and five demands hidden inside three printed limbs. Limb a) alone asks three things - objectives, strategy, and which instruments are covered - and the third is dropped by most candidates because it reads like a trailing afterthought. Count the demands before writing: objective, strategy, instruments, KPIs, three state roles. That is five things, and the answer should have five visible places where each is dealt with.

The second structural failure is limb c). Candidates write a page on flag State, half a page on port State, and one line on coastal State. The Code gives coastal State a full part of its own, and an examiner marking that limb has three boxes to tick.

The citation that decides this question at this sitting

This is a February 2023 paper. The Organization maintains a non-exhaustive list of the obligations under the instruments relevant to the III Code, and that list is periodically replaced by a new Assembly resolution.

At this sitting the operative list is the one adopted by the thirty-second Assembly in December 2021. The thirty-third Assembly met and adopted its resolutions on 6 December 2023 - ten months after this paper was sat - and the list it adopted expressly revoked its predecessor. A candidate who cites the 2023 list in a February 2023 answer is citing an instrument that did not yet exist and, worse, has deleted the one that did.

The same trap catches port State control procedures, which belong to the thirty-second Assembly at this sitting and not to the thirty-third. An Assembly boundary is the adoption date, not the meeting month, and December 2023 is after February 2023.

The point about ratification that most candidates miss

The Code says expressly that a State views the Code according to its own circumstances and is bound only in respect of the instruments to which it is a Contracting Government or Party. This is worth a mark on its own because it corrects a natural misunderstanding: the Code does not oblige every State to implement every IMO convention. It obliges a State to implement properly the ones it has actually accepted. The Code standardises the quality of administration, not the list of ratifications.

What 'mandatory' means here, and why it matters

The Code began life as guidance. It became mandatory through amendments to the principal treaty instruments, and it is the standard against which Member States are audited under the IMO Member State Audit Scheme, mandatory from 1 January 2016. Saying this early frames the whole answer, because it explains why any of these administrative arrangements are enforceable at all. Before the audit scheme, a State that ran a poor administration faced reputational consequences at most; after it, there are findings, corrective action plans and a follow-up.

Be careful with the number of instruments

The figure of nine is commonly quoted for the instruments subject to audit, but it depends entirely on whether Protocols are counted separately from their parent conventions - SOLAS and the 1988 SOLAS Protocol, MARPOL and the 1997 Protocol, Load Lines and the 1988 LL Protocol. Name the families and mention the Protocols rather than asserting a bare number. An answer that explains why the count is ambiguous is stronger than one that states a figure and is marked against a different counting convention.

The delegation point - where flags actually fail

If there is time to develop one part of the flag State limb, develop this one. A flag State may authorise a recognized organization to carry out survey and certification on its behalf. The Code requires a formal written agreement, a defined scope of what is delegated, and an oversight programme to verify the RO is doing it properly.

The principle to state is: the work may be delegated; the responsibility may not. A flag State that authorises an RO and then never checks on it has not discharged its obligation, and this is one of the commonest audit findings in practice. It also connects to the port State limb, because when a port State detains a ship it informs both the flag and the RO that certified her.

No more favourable treatment - say why it exists

Most candidates state the principle. The mark is in explaining the purpose: if a ship flying the flag of a non-Party State could claim exemption from port State inspection on the ground that its flag never agreed to the convention, then not ratifying would be a commercial advantage and the whole treaty system would be undermined. No more favourable treatment closes that hole. It is the reason a convention can be effective against ships whose flags have not accepted it.

Coastal State is about services, not zones

The standard error is to answer the coastal State limb with UNCLOS - territorial sea, contiguous zone, EEZ, and the rights that attach to each. That is a different subject. The III Code asks what the coastal State does: aids to navigation, hydrographic and chart services, meteorological warnings, traffic services, search and rescue, pollution response, places of refuge, and casualty investigation in its waters. Provision and response, not jurisdiction.

The KPIs are the Code's, not yours

Limb b) invites invention, and invention scores nothing. The indicators are the Code's own evaluation criteria and they cluster around externally visible outcomes - casualties, detentions, survey and certification performance, RO oversight results, investigation completion, and reporting compliance. The detention record is worth singling out because it is the one measured by other States rather than self-reported, which makes it the hardest for a weak administration to flatter.

A useful closing line

If the answer needs a conclusion, the strongest one is structural: the same State is flag, coastal and port State simultaneously, and the Code expects each role to be discharged on its own terms. Diligence in one does not excuse failure in another, and the audit examines all three. That sentence demonstrates that the three limbs have been understood as one system rather than as three lists.

Common mistakes

  • Citing A.1187(33) for the list of instruments the Code covers. That resolution was adopted on 6 December 2023, ten months after this sitting, and it revokes the one that was operative. The answer here is A.1157(32).
  • Citing A.1185(33) for port State control procedures. At this sitting it is A.1155(32).
  • Saying the III Code is voluntary. It became mandatory through the IMSAS amendments to the instruments themselves, and that is what makes it bite.
  • Confusing the Code with the audit. The Code is the standard; IMSAS is the audit scheme that checks a State against it.
  • Giving a precise count of the instruments the Code covers. State the families and say the list is maintained by Assembly resolution.
  • Treating the coastal State role as a matter of maritime zones. It is about services - aids to navigation, hydrography, meteorology, search and rescue, casualty response.
  • Inventing key performance indicators. The Code has its own; give those.
  • Forgetting that the flag State remains responsible for what it delegates to a recognized organization.

Examiner traps

  • "Which Assembly resolution lists the instruments at this sitting?" A.1157(32), from the 32nd Assembly of December 2021. Anything numbered (33) is ten months in the future.
  • "Is the Code mandatory?" Yes - by amendment to the parent instruments, not by its own force.
  • "What is the objective in one sentence?" To enhance global safety and environmental protection by helping States implement the instruments they have ratified.
  • "Name three flag State KPIs." Casualty and detention rates, the completeness and timeliness of reporting, the currency of implementing legislation, and the effectiveness of oversight of recognized organizations.
  • "Can a flag State delegate to class?" It may authorise a recognized organization - and it remains responsible.
  • "What does the port State role add?" A second line of defence against a flag State that has not done its job.
  • "Which State role is not about ships at all?" The coastal State - it is about the services a State provides to shipping in its waters.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Your flag State audits your company next month. What will they ask to see?
  • What is the difference between a class surveyor and a flag State inspector on your ship?
  • Who issues your Safety Management Certificate, and on whose authority?
  • If your flag has delegated everything to class, who is answerable at IMO?
  • What does a port State inspector do that a flag State inspector does not?
  • Give me one thing a coastal State owes you that has nothing to do with inspecting your ship.

Memory framework

What it is, then a) b) c) in the printed order. WHAT IT IS (1): A.1070(28), made mandatory through IMSAS, audited rather than certified. LIMB a) in three demands: OBJECTIVE (2), STRATEGY (3) - legislation, resources, oversight, review - and INSTRUMENTS (4), listed by A.1157(32) and not by anything numbered (33). LIMB b) KPIs (5). LIMB c) THREE ROLES: FLAG (6), then COASTAL and PORT (7). Anchor phrase: implement, enforce, audit - and the single date that decides the question is the 32nd Assembly, not the 33rd.

Uncertainty and the Assembly boundary

The single largest risk on this question is a citation, and it has been reversed deliberately. All three exact donors for this stem sit after December 2023 and cite A.1187(33). That resolution did not exist at this sitting and revokes the one that did. A.1157(32) is used throughout, and the reversal is recorded in the paper anchor at sections 2.4 and 4.

No number of instruments is asserted and no ratification count is given. The families the Code covers are named; the count is not, because the list is maintained by Assembly resolution and any figure would be a snapshot.

Understand this first

There are two quite different ways of asking whether a ship is efficient, and this question asks about both. The first is a question about the ship as an object: given her size, her engine and her hull, how much carbon does she inherently produce to move a tonne of cargo a mile? That is a property she has when she is built or modified, and you can calculate it from her particulars without her ever leaving the berth. The second is a question about how she is actually run over a year: how much fuel did she really burn, and how much useful work did she really do? Two identical sister ships have the same answer to the first question and can have very different answers to the second, because one is driven hard and the other is not. The first idea gave rise to a design index. For new ships it had been in place for years; the obvious gap was the enormous existing fleet, built before the requirement, which was never going to be caught by a rule aimed at newbuildings. So the same calculation was applied backwards to ships already trading, and each was required to demonstrate that her figure was no worse than a limit set for her type and size. A ship that fails can be modified - and, because the calculation contains the engine power, she can also simply be prevented from using all of it, which is the cheap route and the one most ships took. The second idea gave rise to an annual rating. Each year the ship's actual fuel consumption and distance are used to compute a number, that number is compared with what is expected of her, and she is graded on a five-point scale. Grades slip over time because the expected value tightens each year, so standing still means falling behind. The important thing to see is that the two schemes pull in different directions on one point: limiting a ship's power satisfies the design index without necessarily changing anything about how much fuel she burns, and the annual rating is unmoved by anything that does not actually reduce consumption.

Knowledge map
EEXI Design Compliance Measures, and the CII Rating with AER and EEOI
  1. a)
    1. 1Where EEXI sits in law - and what the printed Phase 2 framing refers to
      • Chapter 4 of Annex VI as revised by MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022, chapter 4 applying from 1 January 2023
      • Reg 23 attained EEXI, reg 25 required EEXI: attained ≤ required
      • The phased Phase 0/1/2/3 scheme belongs to reg 24 Table 1, required EEDI, for NEW ships. EEXI takes its factor from reg 25 Table 3, banded by type and size, not phased
    2. 2Hull and propulsive design measures
      • Bulbous bow retrofit, hull coating, propeller retrofit and polishing, energy saving devices - ducts, fins, PSS
      • Air lubrication; wind assistance and the innovative-technology route
    3. 3Machinery and energy-recovery measures
      • Waste heat recovery, derating and engine tuning, shaft generator, efficient auxiliaries, alternative and low-carbon fuels through the CF term
    4. 4Power limitation - what most existing ships actually did
      • EPL and ShaPoLi - reduce P in the numerator; overridable in an emergency, recorded, verified
      • The honest point: it limits the ship rather than improving her, and it does nothing for the CII unless the speed is actually reduced
  2. b)
    1. 5Limb b) - the concept of the CII rating
      • Reg 27 data in, reg 28 rating out; 5,000 GT and above
      • Attained annual operational CII against a required value from a reference line and a tightening reduction factor; five bands A to E; D three years running, or E once, triggers a corrective action plan in the SEEMP
    2. 6AER and EEOI critically compared - the denominator is the whole argument
      • Both are CO₂ per transport work; the argument is entirely about the denominator
      • AER = deadweight × distance - capacity offered, always available, rewards sailing full-speed empty
      • EEOI = cargo actually carried × distance - real transport work, but needs cargo data and is not comparable between trades
    3. 7Measures to improve the rating - operational
      • Speed reduction - the largest single lever, cubic in power; voyage and weather routeing; just-in-time arrival; trim and draught optimisation; hull and propeller cleaning; reduced auxiliary and boiler load; cargo and ballast utilisation
    4. 8Measures to improve the rating - technical, and the SEEMP as the instrument
      • The same technical measures as limb a) where they reduce real consumption; SEEMP Part III required on board from 1 January 2023, carrying the implementation plan and the corrective action plan
