  4. Measures to improve the rating - technical, and the SEEMP as the instrument

Memory cue. ROUTE: (1) Where EEXI sits in law - and what the printed Phase 2 framing refers to · (2) Hull and propulsive design measures · (3) Machinery and energy-recovery measures · (4) Power limitation - what most existing ships actually did · (5) Limb b) - the concept of the CII rating · (6) AER and EEOI critically compared - the denominator is the whole argument · (7) Measures to improve the rating - operational · (8) Measures to improve the rating - technical, and the SEEMP as the instrument

TWO INDICES, KEEP THEM APART. EEXI = the SHIP (one-off, technical, attained ≤ required, reg 23 and reg 25). CII = the VOYAGE YEAR (annual, operational, rated A-E, reg 28). Limb a) DESIGN on an EXISTING ship: hull/coating/bulb, PROPELLER, ESDs, air lubrication, WHR, derating, shaft generator, fuel via CF - and the one most ships actually used, POWER LIMITATION (EPL/ShaPoLi), which limits her rather than improves her. Watch the stem: 'Phase 2 20-30%' is the EEDI table (reg 24), NOT the EEXI table (reg 25 Table 3, banded not phased). Limb b) AER vs EEOI - it is all about the DENOMINATOR: AER uses deadweight (capacity offered, always available, rewards a fast empty ship); EEOI uses cargo actually carried (real work, but needs cargo data and is not comparable across trades). Improve by SPEED first. Feb 2023: rating year six weeks old, NOBODY is rated yet.

Remember8 route headings
Cover15 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 15 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. Where EEXI sits in law - and what the printed Phase 2 framing refers to
  • Chapter 4 of Annex VI as revised by MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022, chapter 4 applying from 1 January 2023
  • Reg 23 attained EEXI, reg 25 required EEXI: attained ≤ required
  • The phased Phase 0/1/2/3 scheme belongs to reg 24 Table 1, required EEDI, for NEW ships. EEXI takes its factor from reg 25 Table 3, banded by type and size, not phased
2. Hull and propulsive design measures
  • Bulbous bow retrofit, hull coating, propeller retrofit and polishing, energy saving devices - ducts, fins, PSS
  • Air lubrication; wind assistance and the innovative-technology route
3. Machinery and energy-recovery measures
  • Waste heat recovery, derating and engine tuning, shaft generator, efficient auxiliaries, alternative and low-carbon fuels through the CF term
4. Power limitation - what most existing ships actually did
  • EPL and ShaPoLi - reduce P in the numerator; overridable in an emergency, recorded, verified
  • The honest point: it limits the ship rather than improving her, and it does nothing for the CII unless the speed is actually reduced
5. Limb b) - the concept of the CII rating
  • Reg 27 data in, reg 28 rating out; 5,000 GT and above
  • Attained annual operational CII against a required value from a reference line and a tightening reduction factor; five bands A to E; D three years running, or E once, triggers a corrective action plan in the SEEMP
6. AER and EEOI critically compared - the denominator is the whole argument
  • Both are CO₂ per transport work; the argument is entirely about the denominator
  • AER = deadweight × distance - capacity offered, always available, rewards sailing full-speed empty
  • EEOI = cargo actually carried × distance - real transport work, but needs cargo data and is not comparable between trades
7. Measures to improve the rating - operational
  • Speed reduction - the largest single lever, cubic in power; voyage and weather routeing; just-in-time arrival; trim and draught optimisation; hull and propeller cleaning; reduced auxiliary and boiler load; cargo and ballast utilisation
8. Measures to improve the rating - technical, and the SEEMP as the instrument
  • The same technical measures as limb a) where they reduce real consumption; SEEMP Part III required on board from 1 January 2023, carrying the implementation plan and the corrective action plan
Model written answer

1. Where EEXI sits in law - and what the printed Phase 2 framing refers to

The Energy Efficiency Existing Ship Index is part of chapter 4 of MARPOL Annex VI, in the revised Annex adopted by resolution MEPC.328(76). That revised Annex entered into force on 1 November 2022, and its chapter 4 carbon intensity requirements applied from 1 January 2023 - six weeks before this examination.

The structure is the same as the design index for new ships, applied to a ship that already exists:

  • Regulation 23 requires an attained EEXI to be calculated for each ship in the applicable categories. It is a calculated figure in grammes of CO₂ per tonne-mile, derived from the ship's installed power, her specific fuel consumption, her capacity and her reference speed.
  • Regulation 25 sets the required EEXI, and the obligation is simply that attained EEXI shall not exceed required EEXI.
  • Compliance is verified once, at the first periodical survey on or after 1 January 2023, and recorded in the ship's technical file and on her IEE Certificate.

A point of accuracy on the stem, and it is worth a mark. The question prints 'the EEXI framework under Phase 2 ( f 20% - 30% reduction)'. Two things need separating:

  • The phased structure - Phase 0, Phase 1, Phase 2, Phase 3, tightening by delivery date - belongs to the required EEDI at regulation 24 and its Table 1, and applies to new ships.
  • EEXI takes its required value from regulation 25 and Table 3, where the reduction factor is set by ship type and size band. It is not phased: an existing ship has one required EEXI, not a series of them.

The reduction factors in the EEXI table do broadly fall in the range the question quotes for the larger conventional cargo types, which is presumably why the framing was written that way. But the correct statement is that the required reduction is read from the EEXI table by type and size, not that EEXI has phases. An answer that says so is more accurate than the question.

The rest of limb a) then follows from the formula. The attained EEXI is, in essence, emitted CO₂ divided by transport capacity, so anything that reduces the numerator or increases the denominator improves it. Since the denominator is fixed by the ship's capacity, in practice every measure attacks the numerator - and the numerator contains the installed power, the specific fuel consumption, and the carbon factor of the fuel. Those three terms organise the whole of the answer.

2. Hull and propulsive design measures

MeasureMechanismPractical note on an existing ship
Bulbous bow retrofitThe original bulb was optimised for a design speed and draught the ship no longer operates at. Reshaping it for the actual operating profile reduces wave-making resistance and therefore the power needed at the reference speed.A drydock job requiring hull modification. Justified where the ship's real operating speed has fallen well below her design speed - which after years of slow steaming is common.
Advanced hull coatingReduces frictional resistance and, as importantly, resists fouling so the resistance stays low.Applied at a scheduled drydocking. Its EEXI credit is limited because the index uses a reference condition, but its real-world benefit is substantial.
Propeller retrofit or modificationA propeller matched to the current operating point rather than the original design point. Larger diameter and lower rpm, improved blade section, or a new propeller altogether.One of the most effective single retrofits, because the original propeller is frequently mismatched to how the ship is now run.
Propeller boss cap fins and hub vortex devicesRecover rotational energy from the hub vortex downstream of the propeller.Fitted with the propeller, comparatively inexpensive.
Pre-swirl and post-swirl devices - ducts, stators, finsCondition the flow into or out of the propeller so it works in a more uniform wake, improving propulsive efficiency.Effectiveness is strongly hull-form dependent and requires CFD or model verification for the particular ship.
Rudder improvements - bulb, twisted leading edge, finsRecover rotational energy in the propeller slipstream and reduce rudder drag.Often combined with a propeller change as a package.
Air lubricationA layer of air bubbles or a cavity beneath the flat of bottom reduces frictional resistance.Significant installation, needs compressor power which partly offsets the gain, and suits ships with a large flat bottom.
Wind assistance - rotors, rigid sails, kitesProvides thrust independent of the main engine, reducing the power the engine must deliver.Treated in the index through the innovative energy efficient technology provisions. Structural and cargo-access implications are the practical obstacle.

3. Machinery and energy-recovery measures

  • Waste heat recovery. An exhaust gas economiser raising steam, or a power turbine and turbogenerator, converts exhaust heat into useful work and displaces auxiliary generator load. It reduces total fuel burned for the same propulsion.
  • Engine derating and retuning. Re-optimising the engine for the lower power at which it now actually operates improves the specific fuel consumption at that operating point. Slide-fuel valves, adjusted injection timing and turbocharger cut-out are the usual measures.
  • Shaft generator. Taking electrical load from the main engine, which is more efficient per unit of energy than a small auxiliary diesel, reduces total consumption.
  • Efficient auxiliaries. Variable-frequency drives on pumps and fans, and demand-based rather than continuous running, cut electrical load.
  • Low-carbon and alternative fuels. This is the one measure that attacks a different term: the carbon factor CF, the mass of CO₂ per unit mass of fuel. Switching to LNG, LPG, methanol or a biofuel reduces the numerator without touching the machinery's efficiency at all. On an existing ship it usually requires conversion of the fuel system and tanks, which is why it was the least-used route at this sitting.

4. Power limitation - what most existing ships actually did

This deserves its own section because it is the honest answer to what the fleet did, and because it shows understanding of the formula rather than a list of hardware.

The attained EEXI contains the installed main engine power. Reduce the power that may be used and the attained figure falls, without altering the ship physically in any other way. Two forms are recognised:

  • Engine Power Limitation (EPL) - a limitation applied to the engine itself, typically through the control system and mechanical or software stops, so that it cannot exceed a stated output.
  • Shaft Power Limitation (ShaPoLi) - the shaft power is monitored and limited, leaving the engine itself unaltered.

In each case the limitation must be verifiable, recorded in an onboard management manual, and capable of being overridden in an emergency to restore full power where the safety of the ship demands it - with the override recorded. Class and the Administration verify the arrangement.

The critical observation, and it is the one that distinguishes a good answer: power limitation makes the ship compliant without making her efficient. It reduces the maximum speed available to her, and therefore her commercial flexibility, but it does not by itself reduce the fuel she burns on any voyage she was already performing at a lower power. It has no effect on her CII rating unless the limitation actually forces her to sail more slowly than she otherwise would. That is precisely the tension between the two schemes in limb a) and limb b), and stating it links the two halves of the answer.

5. Limb b) - the concept of the CII rating

Where EEXI is a property of the ship, the Carbon Intensity Indicator measures how she is actually operated over a calendar year. The mechanism has four steps:

  1. Data in. Under regulation 27, ships of 5,000 gross tonnage and above collect fuel oil consumption data, together with distance travelled and hours under way, for each calendar year and report it to the Administration, which submits it to the Organization.
  2. Attained CII. Under regulation 28, the ship's attained annual operational CII is calculated from that data - the CO₂ emitted, derived from fuel consumed and its carbon factor, divided by a measure of transport work.
  3. Required CII. The attained value is compared with a required value, obtained from a reference line for the ship's type and size and then tightened by an annual reduction factor relative to a 2019 baseline. The reduction factor increases year on year, so the required value becomes progressively harder to meet.
  4. Rating. The ratio of attained to required places the ship in one of five bands, A to E, where A is the best. The band boundaries are set for each ship type.

The consequence is what gives the scheme teeth: a ship rated D for three consecutive years, or E for a single year, must develop a plan of corrective actions as part of her SEEMP, to be approved and implemented. There is no direct financial penalty in the regulation itself - the pressure is regulatory and, increasingly, commercial, because charterers see the rating.

The state of the scheme at this sitting must be stated correctly. Regulation 28 determines the rating after the end of the calendar year. The first year to which it applies is 2023, which began six weeks before this examination. No ship therefore held a CII rating at the date of this paper. The first ratings could not be assigned until 2024. An answer describing the distribution of ratings across the fleet, or describing ships as having been rated, is describing a state of affairs that did not yet exist.

6. AER and EEOI critically compared - the denominator is the whole argument

Both metrics have the same form - CO₂ emitted divided by transport work - and both are expressed in grammes of CO₂ per tonne-mile. They differ only in what they count as transport work, and the entire critical discussion lives in that difference.

AER - Annual Efficiency RatioEEOI - Energy Efficiency Operational Indicator
DenominatorDeadweight capacity × distance sailedCargo actually carried × distance sailed
What it therefore measuresCarbon per unit of capacity offeredCarbon per unit of transport work actually done
Data neededFuel, distance, and the ship's deadweight - all readily available and already reportedFuel, distance and cargo carried on every leg - which the ship does not always hold reliably
Ballast voyagesCounted in full - distance is distance, and the deadweight denominator does not care whether cargo is aboardA ballast leg has zero transport work, so it worsens the figure sharply and correctly
ComparabilityGood. Two ships of the same type and size are compared on the same basis regardless of tradePoor. A ship in a short-sea trade with frequent port calls cannot be fairly compared with one on long ocean legs
Regulatory roleThe metric used for the CII rating of most ship typesVoluntary, and long-established as a management tool
Principal weaknessIt rewards behaviour that is genuinely wasteful. A ship sailing empty at full speed still divides by her full deadweight, so her figure can look respectable while she does no useful work at allIt is volatile and can be worsened by commercial decisions entirely outside the ship's control - a part cargo, a repositioning leg, a waiting period

The critical judgement to state explicitly is this. EEOI is the better measure of what the industry actually cares about, because carbon per tonne of cargo actually moved is the real environmental performance of a transport operation. AER is the better measure to regulate with, because it can be computed from data that is already reported, verified and hard to manipulate, and because it produces figures that are comparable between ships. The regulator chose administrability over meaning, and did so deliberately.

The cost of that choice is a real perverse incentive, and saying so is what makes the discussion critical rather than descriptive. Because the AER denominator is capacity rather than cargo, a ship can improve her rating by sailing longer distances - including in ballast - since distance appears in the denominator. A ship that sits idle at anchor burning auxiliary fuel accrues emissions with no distance at all and is penalised heavily, whereas the same ship steaming a pointless ballast leg is treated more kindly. That is the opposite of the intended incentive, and it is the standing criticism of the metric.

Two further limitations worth a line each: neither metric accounts for the cargo's own characteristics - a full cargo of a light commodity that fills the ship by volume before reaching her deadweight is invisible to AER - and both are annual, so a single unrepresentative year can determine a rating.

7. Measures to improve the rating - operational

Because the CII is driven by fuel actually burned, only measures that reduce real consumption or increase real transport work will move it. The operational measures are the fastest-acting and the most powerful:

  • Speed reduction. The single largest lever available. Propulsive power varies approximately with the cube of the speed, so a modest reduction in speed produces a disproportionate reduction in consumption. This is why it dominates every other operational measure.
  • Voyage optimisation and weather routeing. Choosing the route and speed profile that minimises consumption against the forecast, rather than steaming the shortest line at full speed.
  • Just-in-time arrival. The classic waste is a fast passage followed by days at anchor. Arriving when the berth is actually available allows the whole passage to be run at lower speed for the same arrival time - the largest easily-available saving on many trades, and it requires cooperation with the port and the charterer rather than any engineering.
  • Trim and draught optimisation. Operating at the trim that minimises resistance for the actual draught and speed, established by trials or by a validated model.
  • Hull and propeller cleaning. In-water cleaning between drydockings, timed on measured performance rather than on the calendar.
  • Reducing auxiliary and boiler load. Shutting down unnecessary machinery, managing cargo heating and tank cleaning, avoiding unnecessary boiler running in port.
  • Improving utilisation. Reducing ballast legs and carrying fuller cargoes improves the real efficiency, and improves EEOI directly - though, as section 6 explains, AER responds to this only weakly.

8. Measures to improve the rating - technical, and the SEEMP as the instrument

The technical measures are the same family as limb a), but the test they must pass is different: they improve the CII only if they reduce fuel actually consumed in service. So hull coating, propeller retrofit, energy-saving devices, waste heat recovery, shaft generators, variable-speed auxiliaries and air lubrication all help. Power limitation, as noted at section 4, does not, unless it forces a genuinely lower operating speed.

A change of fuel to one with a lower carbon factor reduces the attained CII directly, because the emitted CO₂ falls even if the energy consumed does not.

All of this is administered through the SEEMP. Under regulation 26 every ship carries a ship-specific Ship Energy Efficiency Management Plan. With effect from 1 January 2023 - again, six weeks before this sitting - the SEEMP for ships subject to the CII must include an enhanced part containing:

  • the required CII values for the next three years;
  • an implementation plan setting out how the ship intends to achieve them;
  • a procedure for self-evaluation and improvement;
  • and, where the ship has been rated D three years running or E once, the plan of corrective actions.

The operative guidance at this sitting is the 2022 SEEMP Guidelines, resolution MEPC.346(78), together with the CII guideline set covering the reference lines, the reduction factors, the rating boundaries and the correction factors.

The closing point, which answers the question as a whole: limb a) and limb b) can be satisfied by different actions, and only some actions satisfy both. Power limitation satisfies EEXI and does nothing for CII. Just-in-time arrival improves CII and does nothing for EEXI. A propeller retrofit, a hull coating or a waste heat recovery system improves both. Recognising which measures do which is the practical substance of managing a ship under chapter 4.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks and five demands: the design measures; the concept of the CII rating; a critical comparison of AER and EEOI; the improvement measures; and, implicitly, the correct legal framing of EEXI. The single biggest structural failure is blurring the two indices into one essay about decarbonisation. They are different in kind and the question is testing whether the candidate knows that.

EEXICII
What it describesThe shipThe year of operation
When assessedOnce, at the first survey on or after 1 Jan 2023Every year
Calculated fromShip particulars - power, SFC, capacity, reference speedActual fuel burned and distance sailed
ResultPass or fail against a required valueA rating, A to E
Regulation23 attained, 25 required27 data, 28 rating
Improved byAnything reducing the calculated figure - including power limitationAnything reducing real consumption - power limitation does not help

That last row is the most examinable single fact in the whole subject, and it is where the two limbs of this question meet.

The stem is wrong about Phase 2 - handle it, do not adopt it

The question says EEXI has a Phase 2 with a 20 to 30 per cent reduction. The phased structure - Phase 0, 1, 2, 3, tightening by delivery date - belongs to the required EEDI for new ships, at regulation 24 and Table 1. EEXI takes its reduction factor from regulation 25 and Table 3, banded by ship type and size, and is not phased at all. An existing ship has one required EEXI, not a sequence of them.

The factors in the EEXI table do largely fall in that range for the bigger conventional cargo types, so the framing is not absurd - but it is not correct either. Say the framing as printed, then distinguish the two tables. A candidate who adopts the framing uncritically will start describing the wrong table. Do not, incidentally, attempt to reproduce the tables. They are ship-type and size specific and cannot be recalled reliably; describing how the factor is obtained is worth more than a half-remembered number.

Organise limb a) by the formula, not as a shopping list

The attained EEXI is emitted CO₂ over transport capacity, and the numerator contains three things you can attack: installed power, specific fuel consumption, and the carbon factor of the fuel. Every measure in the answer belongs to one of those three, and saying so organises the list into an argument:

  • Reduce the power needed - hull, coating, bulb, propeller, energy-saving devices, air lubrication, wind assistance.
  • Reduce the fuel per unit of power - derating and retuning, waste heat recovery, shaft generator, efficient auxiliaries.
  • Reduce the carbon per unit of fuel - alternative fuels, through CF.
  • Or simply reduce the power term in the formula - power limitation.

Be honest about power limitation

It is what the overwhelming majority of existing ships actually did, because it is cheap, quick and requires no drydocking. An answer that omits it is describing a world that did not happen. But the examinable insight is the criticism: it makes the ship compliant without making her efficient. It removes speed she may have needed commercially, and it does nothing at all for her CII unless she is thereby forced to sail slower than she otherwise would.

Get the safety point in too: the limitation must be overridable to recover full power where the safety of the ship requires it, and the override must be recorded. That detail shows the arrangement has been understood as a real installation rather than as a paper exercise.

AER against EEOI - argue about the denominator

Both are CO₂ per tonne-mile. The only difference is what the tonnes are. AER divides by deadweight - the capacity the ship offers. EEOI divides by the cargo she actually carried. Everything follows from that one distinction and the answer should be built on it rather than on two separate definitions.

The trade-off to state: EEOI is more meaningful, AER is more administrable. EEOI measures what we actually care about - carbon per tonne of cargo genuinely moved - but it needs cargo data the ship may not hold reliably, it is volatile, and it cannot fairly compare a short-sea trader with an ocean voyager. AER needs only fuel, distance and a number from the ship's particulars, all of which are already reported and verified, and it compares like with like. The regulator chose administrability, deliberately.

Then deliver the criticism, because the question says critically: AER rewards a ship for sailing empty. The denominator is deadweight times distance and it does not ask whether there was any cargo aboard. A ship steaming a long ballast leg at speed can post a respectable AER while performing no transport work whatever, and a ship waiting at anchor burning auxiliary fuel accumulates emissions with no distance in the denominator at all and is punished. That is the wrong way round, and it is the standing objection to the metric.

Speed is the answer to the improvement limb

If only one improvement measure could be given, it would be speed reduction, because propulsive power goes approximately as the cube of speed. Nothing else available to a ship's staff comes close. Build the improvement limb outward from that: speed, then the things that let you take speed out without losing the fixture - weather routeing, and above all just-in-time arrival, which converts waiting time at anchor into slower steaming at sea for the same arrival.

Then trim, hull and propeller cleaning, auxiliary and boiler load, and utilisation. Then the technical measures, with the test stated: does it reduce fuel actually burned in service? If not, it does not improve the CII.

Know what year it is

This paper was sat in February 2023. The revised Annex VI had been in force since 1 November 2022 - three months. Chapter 4 had applied since 1 January 2023 - six weeks. The first CII rating year had just begun and no ship anywhere held a rating; the first ratings could not exist until 2024. EEXI verification was happening at first annual surveys through that year, so much of the fleet had not yet been verified either.

Write the CII limb forward-looking - what the mechanism will produce - and not as a description of ratings the fleet already had. And do not import anything later: the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy was adopted on 7 July 2023, five months after this paper, and the EU Emissions Trading System did not reach shipping until 1 January 2024.

Common mistakes

  • Do not quote a percentage saving for any measure. Published figures vary enormously with ship type, baseline and assumptions, and an invented one is worse than none.
  • Do not attempt to reproduce the CII rating boundary values or the reference line coefficients. Explain how they are obtained instead.
  • Do not put slow steaming under limb a). It is operational and belongs in limb b).
  • Do not present AER and EEOI as interchangeable. One is the regulatory metric; the other is voluntary.
  • Do not say ships were rated A to E at this sitting. None was.

Examiner traps

  • "Is the revised Annex VI in force at this sitting?" Yes - since 1 November 2022. The corpus amendment register says 2023-11-01 and it is wrong by a year; the resolution's own operative paragraph 3 settles it.
  • "The stem says Phase 2, 20 to 30 per cent. Is that right?" No, and handling it is the question. The phased Phase 0/1/2/3 structure belongs to the required EEDI table for new ships. EEXI takes its reduction factor from a table banded by ship type and size, and it is not phased.
  • "How many ships hold a CII rating today?" None. The first rating year began six weeks ago and the rating is determined after the year ends.
  • "Which strategy is operative?" The Initial IMO GHG Strategy of 2018. The 2023 Strategy is five months future.
  • "AER or EEOI - which is the regulatory metric?" AER, because its denominator is deadweight × distance and needs no cargo data. EEOI uses actual cargo carried and is the better operational measure - and it is the one that cannot be audited from mandatory reported data.
  • "What did most existing ships actually do for EEXI?" Engine or shaft power limitation - because it is cheap, reversible and verifiable, not because it makes the ship efficient.
  • "Does power limitation improve the CII?" Only if the ship actually slows down. EEXI is a design index; CII is measured in service.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Show me where the EEXI Technical File is kept and what is in it.
  • Your ship has an overridable power limitation. Who may break the seal, and what must you record?
  • Your CII is trending towards D. What would you do first, and what would it cost?
  • Why does a slower ship have a better AER but perhaps a worse EEOI?
  • What is in Part III of your SEEMP that was not in Parts I and II?
  • What would you change on this ship if you had one drydocking and a fixed budget?

Memory framework

Law first, then the design measures, then the rating. LAW (1): revised Annex VI in force 1 November 2022, EEXI and CII applicable 1 January 2023 - and the printed Phase 2 framing belongs to EEDI, not EEXI. DESIGN MEASURES in three groups: HULL AND PROPULSIVE (2), MACHINERY AND ENERGY RECOVERY (3), POWER LIMITATION (4) - the one most ships used. RATING: the CII concept (5), AER against EEOI - the argument is the denominator (6), then improvement measures OPERATIONAL (7) and TECHNICAL with the SEEMP as the instrument (8). Anchor pair: EEXI is design, CII is service.

Regulation and source map

PointWhere it comes from
Entry into force of the revised Annex VIResolution MEPC.328(76), operative paragraph 3, read at source - 1 November 2022. P1
EEXI, the required reduction factor and the CII ratingMARPOL Annex VI regulations 22 to 28, corpus canonical regulation records. P1
The distinction between the phased EEDI table and the unphased EEXI tableThe same regulation records, read against the printed stem
The enhanced SEEMP including Part IIIResolution MEPC.346(78), 2022 Guidelines
The CII guideline layer G1 to G5MEPC.352(78) to MEPC.355(78) and MEPC.338(76)
The operative strategyInitial IMO GHG Strategy, MEPC.304(72) of 2018, recited in MEPC.328(76)'s own preamble

Uncertainty and the corpus correction this question depends on

This is the first MIW paper whose answer depends on an open corpus correction rather than merely referencing one. The corpus amendment register records the entry into force of MEPC.328(76) as 2023-11-01. The correct date, taken from the resolution's own operative paragraph 3 read at source, is 1 November 2022. The correction request TSCR-3 is carried, not re-raised, and the corpus was not modified. Without the correction this question cannot be answered at all: a February 2023 sitting reading the register would conclude the revised Annex VI was not yet in force and would then have to explain EEXI and CII - which the paper states are in force - under a superseded Annex.

No numerical reduction factor, size band, rating boundary or reference-line constant is asserted. The regulation records establish the structure of the tables; the values are not reproduced, and no worked EEXI or CII calculation appears.

No percentage saving is claimed for any design measure, and no manufacturer, product or installed population is named. Published figures are hull-specific and largely vendor-sourced.

The forward-looking treatment of the rating is deliberate and is the paper's own finding. No ship held a CII rating at this sitting, so every statement about ships responding to their ratings has been re-authored as anticipation rather than description.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________a) Where EEXI sits in law - and what the printed Phase 2 framing refers to

  3. __________a) Machinery and energy-recovery measures
  4. __________a) Power limitation - what most existing ships actually did
  5. __________b) Limb b) - the concept of the CII rating
  6. __________b) AER and EEOI critically compared - the denominator is the whole argument
  7. __________b) Measures to improve the rating - operational
  8. __________b) Measures to improve the rating - technical, and the SEEMP as the instrument
Flashcards 13

Limb a): 1 where EEXI sits in law and what the printed Phase 2 framing really refers to; 2 hull and propulsive measures; 3 machinery and energy recovery; 4 power limitation. Limb b): 5 the rating mechanism; 6 AER against EEOI critically; 7 operational improvement; 8 technical improvement and the SEEMP.

Five demands across two limbs, and the two indices must be kept apart.

EEXI is a one-off calculated property of the ship - attained must not exceed required, regs 23 and 25, verified once. CII is an annual measure of how she was actually operated - regs 27 and 28, rated A to E every year.

Blurring them into one essay on decarbonisation is the biggest structural failure on this question.

A candidate who adopts the printed framing describes the wrong table.

By the formula. The numerator holds installed power, specific fuel consumption and the fuel's carbon factor. Reduce power needed (hull, bulb, coating, propeller, ESDs, air lubrication, wind); reduce fuel per unit power (derate, retune, WHR, shaft generator, VFDs); reduce carbon per unit fuel (alternative fuels); or limit the power term directly.

Turns a shopping list into an argument.

It makes the ship compliant without making her efficient. It removes speed she may need commercially and does nothing for her CII unless it actually forces her to sail slower than she otherwise would.

It is what most ships did, and the criticism is where the two limbs of the question meet.

Reg 27 data collected and reported at 5,000 GT and above; reg 28 attained annual operational CII calculated; compared with a required value from a reference line tightened by an annual reduction factor; the ratio places the ship in one of five bands A to E.

The mechanism, not the numbers, is what is examinable.

D for three consecutive years, or E for a single year, requires a plan of corrective actions developed as part of the SEEMP, approved and implemented. No direct financial penalty in the regulation; the pressure is regulatory and commercial.

It is what gives the scheme teeth.

The denominator. AER divides by deadweight capacity times distance - the capacity offered. EEOI divides by cargo actually carried times distance - the work actually done.

Everything else follows from it; build the comparison on it rather than on two definitions.

It rewards sailing empty. Deadweight times distance does not ask whether cargo was aboard, so a fast ballast leg posts a respectable figure for no transport work, while a ship waiting at anchor burning auxiliary fuel accrues emissions with no distance in the denominator and is punished. The incentive runs the wrong way.

The question says 'critically'; this is the criticism.

Administrability over meaning. AER needs only fuel, distance and deadweight - already reported, verified and hard to manipulate - and it compares like with like. EEOI is more meaningful but needs cargo data, is volatile, and is not comparable between trades.

Stating the trade-off as a deliberate choice is the mark of a critical answer.

Speed reduction - propulsive power varies approximately with the cube of speed. Then just-in-time arrival, which converts waiting at anchor into slower steaming for the same arrival time.

Nothing else available to ship's staff comes close.

The first rating year began 1 January 2023, six weeks earlier. Regulation 28 determines the rating after the end of the calendar year, so no ship anywhere held a rating; the first could not be assigned until 2024.

The twenty-month-later donor can describe ships as rated; this sitting cannot.

For ships subject to the CII, an enhanced part carrying the required CII values for the next three years, an implementation plan, a self-evaluation and improvement procedure, and where triggered the plan of corrective actions. Operative guidance: MEPC.346(78), the 2022 SEEMP Guidelines.

It is the instrument through which the whole improvement limb is administered.

Quick revision
15-second recall
EEXI = the ship, once. CII = the year, every year. EEXI: chapter 4, revised Annex VI (MEPC.328(76), in force 1 Nov 2022, ch.4 applies 1 Jan 2023); reg 23 attained, reg 25 required, attained ≤ required, verified at first survey on/after 1 Jan 2023. Printed 'Phase 2' is the EEDI table (reg 24 T1, new ships); EEXI uses reg 25 T3, banded by type and size, NOT phased. Attack the numerator: power (hull, bulb, coating, propeller, ESDs, air lubrication, wind), SFC (derate/retune, WHR, shaft generator, VFDs), CF (alternative fuel) - or just limit the power: EPL/ShaPoLi, overridable, recorded. CII: reg 27 data (≥5,000 GT) → reg 28 rating; attained/required from a reference line with a tightening reduction factor; bands A-E; D×3 or E×1 → corrective action plan in the SEEMP. AER = deadweight×distance (capacity offered - administrable, comparable, but rewards a fast empty ship); EEOI = cargo carried×distance (real work - meaningful, but needs cargo data and is not comparable across trades). Improve: SPEED first (cube law), JIT arrival, routeing, trim, cleaning, utilisation. SEEMP Part III from 1 Jan 2023; guidelines MEPC.346(78). Feb 2023: NOBODY is rated yet.

Understand this first

Someone who saves property at sea from real danger is entitled to be rewarded, even though nobody asked them to and nobody promised them anything. That is unusual - in almost every other area of law you get paid only if somebody agreed to pay you - and the reason for the exception is straightforward policy. Ships in trouble are often a long way from help, the people best placed to assist are strangers with their own commercial interests, and a legal system that offered them nothing would leave a great deal of property, and often lives, unnecessarily lost. So the law creates the reward itself, out of the act of saving. The reward is deliberately generous and it is deliberately conditional. It is conditional in that you get nothing at all if you fail; a heroic and expensive attempt that ends with the ship on the bottom earns no reward. It is generous because the size of the award takes account not just of what you spent but of the skill you showed, the risk you ran, and the value of what you saved. The point of that generosity is to make it worth a professional's while to keep expensive equipment standing by for casualties that may never come. The second idea is that this whole scheme can also be operated by agreement, and usually is. A master with a serious casualty does not want to argue about terms while the ship is going aground, and a salvor does not want to work with no idea whether he will be paid at all. So a standard form exists that settles the question in one signature - the salvor will be paid on the same 'no cure, no pay' basis, and how much will be decided afterwards by an arbitrator. That is the whole of the difference the question asks about: not whether money is involved, but whether the parties agreed anything before the work began. The third idea is the one candidates most often get wrong. The reward is not paid by the person who was rescued in some general sense. It is paid by the property that was saved - and if a laden ship is saved, the cargo was saved too, so the cargo owners pay their share alongside the shipowner.

Model written answer

1. The three preconditions - what makes any claim a salvage claim

Nothing in this question can be explained without these, so they come first. Under the International Convention on Salvage, 1989, a salvage claim requires three things, and all three must be present:

  1. A recognised subject in danger. There must be a vessel or other property in peril. The danger must be real, though it need not be immediate or absolute - a ship that will be in serious trouble if the weather deteriorates is in danger.
  2. A voluntary service. The salvor must be under no pre-existing legal or official duty to render the service. A crew member saving his own ship is doing his job; a tug performing what her towage contract already requires is doing what she was hired for; a public authority acting under a statutory duty is not volunteering.
  3. Success. The service must have contributed to a useful result. This is the 'no cure, no pay' principle - an unsuccessful attempt, however skilful and however costly, earns nothing.

The point to appreciate about 'voluntary' is that it does not mean unpaid or unagreed. A professional salvage company operating commercially under a signed contract is still volunteering in the required sense, because it was under no prior duty to attend this casualty. That is exactly why contractual salvage remains salvage, which is the substance of the first demand.

2. Demand 1 - the difference between maritime salvage and contractual salvage

Maritime salvage - also called pure salvage - is salvage arising by operation of the maritime law itself. No agreement is made before the service, and the salvor's entitlement to a reward arises from the act of successfully saving property in danger. The amount is fixed afterwards, by agreement between the parties or, failing that, by a court.

Contractual salvage is salvage rendered under an agreement made before or during the service. The parties settle in advance the basis on which the salvor will work and how the reward will be determined.

The test is the presence of a prior agreement, and nothing else. That must be said plainly, because the natural but wrong assumption is that the distinction is between an unpaid rescue and a paid one. It is not. Both are paid. The difference is where the obligation to pay comes from:

Maritime (pure) salvageContractual salvage
Source of the entitlementThe general maritime law, operating on the factsThe agreement between the parties
When terms are settledAfterwards, by agreement or by a courtBefore or during the service
Is it still salvage?YesYes - the agreement governs how the reward is fixed, not whether the service is salvage
No cure, no pay?AlwaysUsually - the standard form preserves it, but the parties may agree otherwise, for instance a daily hire rate
How the amount is determinedBy a court applying the Convention's criteriaBy the machinery the contract provides - typically arbitration applying the same criteria
Typical circumstanceAn unplanned rescue by whoever is presentA professional salvor engaged for a serious casualty

Two consequences are worth stating because they show the distinction has been understood rather than memorised:

  • The Convention applies to both. It applies to salvage operations generally, and its criteria are used to fix the award whether or not there was a contract. That is why an arbitrator under the standard form applies the Convention's own criteria - the contract does not displace the law, it provides the machinery for applying it.
  • The parties may contract out of some of it, but not freely. The Convention permits the parties to agree otherwise, but a contract may be annulled or modified where it was entered into under undue influence or the influence of danger and its terms are inequitable, or where the payment is excessive or grossly inadequate. That protection exists precisely because contracts of this kind are signed under pressure.

3. Demand 2 - simple examples

The stem asks for simple examples, and one clear example of each is worth more than a paragraph of qualification.

  • Maritime (pure) salvage. A cargo ship on passage sights a small vessel disabled and drifting towards a lee shore. She closes, passes a line, and tows the casualty clear to a place of safety. Nothing is agreed - there is no time and no need. The salving ship's owners are afterwards entitled to a salvage reward, and if the amount cannot be agreed it is fixed by a court.
  • Contractual salvage. A laden tanker suffers a main engine failure in heavy weather and begins to drift. Her master, on the owners' instruction, signs Lloyd's Open Form with a professional salvage company, which despatches tugs, connects, and tows her to safety. The service is performed under the agreement, and the amount of the reward is settled afterwards by arbitration under that form.
  • By contrast - ordinary towage, which is neither. A tug engaged to move a ship from the anchorage to her berth for an agreed price is performing a contracted service, not salvage. There is no danger and the tug is under a prior duty. But if the tow encounters unforeseen danger beyond the scope of the towage contract - the ship breaks adrift in worsening weather and the tug saves her from grounding - a salvage claim can then arise, because at that point the tug is doing something she was not engaged to do.

That third example is the most useful of the three, because it shows exactly where the boundary lies.

4. Demand 3 - who is responsible for payment of the award

This is the demand most often answered wrongly, and the Convention's answer is precise.

The award is payable by the owners of all the property that was saved, and each interest contributes in proportion to the saved value of its own property.

So where a laden ship is salved, the salvage award is borne by:

Interest savedWho bears its share
The shipThe shipowner - the ship's proportion of the total salved value
The cargoThe cargo owners - the cargo's proportion, divided among the several cargo interests
Bunkers and storesTheir owner, which may be the charterer rather than the shipowner
Freight and hire at riskWhoever stood to lose it
Containers and equipmentTheir owners, if separately owned

Three points follow, and each is a scoring point:

  • The shipowner is not liable for the whole award. He is liable for the ship's share. Where a fully laden ship is saved the cargo's share may be by far the larger part. Saying 'the shipowner pays' is the standard wrong answer.
  • Liability is proportionate, not joint for the whole. Each interest's exposure is limited by the saved value of its own property, and the total award can never exceed the total salved value. A salvor cannot recover more than he saved.
  • No salvage is payable for saving life alone. Life salvage carries no reward against the persons saved. A salvor of life who takes part in the operation is entitled to a fair share of the award payable for saving the property - but the reward still comes out of the property, not out of the people.

How the salvor actually secures payment. Because the award is owed by many parties whose property is aboard one ship, the practical machinery matters:

  • The salvor has a maritime lien on the salved property, and may arrest it. That is his real security.
  • The property is therefore not released until salvage security is provided by or on behalf of each interest - guarantees from the hull underwriters for the ship and from cargo underwriters for the cargo.
  • Who ultimately bears the cost, as distinct from who is liable. The shipowner's share falls on the hull and machinery underwriters under the policy; the cargo's share falls on the cargo underwriters. Where the salvage was incurred for the common safety of the adventure it may also be brought into general average and adjusted between the interests on that basis. These are indemnity arrangements behind the liability, not the liability itself - the question asks who is responsible, and that is the property owners.

5. Demand 4 - the form popularly used

The form is Lloyd's Standard Form of Salvage Agreement, universally known as Lloyd's Open Form or LOF. It is the dominant contract for emergency salvage worldwide, and at this sitting the current edition is LOF 2020. Earlier editions - LOF 1980, 1990, 1995, 2000, 2011 - remain in circulation and appear in reported cases, but the machinery has been substantially stable across editions, so an answer that understands the machinery is not at risk from naming an edition.

Two features explain why it is 'popularly used', and both are worth stating:

  • It is 'open' because the sum to be paid is left open. Nothing has to be negotiated at the casualty. The master can sign in minutes, on the owners' authority, and work can begin.
  • It is short. The whole point of its design is that it can be agreed by radio or by a single signature at a moment when nobody has time to negotiate.

The characterisation that earns the mark: LOF is not a price. It is an agreement to arbitrate on the no cure, no pay principle. What the parties agree is the mechanism - that the salvor will work, that he is paid only on success, that the amount will be fixed later by a Lloyd's arbitrator, and that security will be provided. They agree nothing at all about the figure.

6. Demand 5 - how the amount of the award is calculated under the form

Because LOF fixes no price, the calculation is done afterwards. The route is:

  1. The service is performed on no cure, no pay terms.
  2. Security is provided by the salved interests; the property is then released.
  3. If the amount cannot be agreed, it is determined by arbitration in London under Lloyd's arbitration procedure, by an arbitrator appointed by the Council of Lloyd's, with a right of appeal to an appeal arbitrator.
  4. The arbitrator fixes the award by applying the criteria in Article 13 of the Salvage Convention - which is why an answer to this demand must go back to the Convention.
  5. The award is then apportioned among the salvors, and where more than one salvor was involved between them, on the same criteria.

The Article 13 criteria. The Convention lists the matters the tribunal takes into account in fixing a reward that encourages salvage operations:

  • the salved value of the vessel and other property;
  • the skill and efforts of the salvors in preventing or minimising damage to the environment;
  • the measure of success obtained by the salvor;
  • the nature and degree of the danger;
  • the skill and efforts of the salvors in salving the vessel, other property and life;
  • the time used and expenses and losses incurred by the salvors;
  • the risk of liability and other risks run by the salvors or their equipment;
  • the promptness of the services rendered;
  • the availability and use of vessels or other equipment intended for salvage operations;
  • the state of readiness and efficiency of the salvor's equipment and its value.

Three things must be said about that list, and they are where the marks are:

  • The criteria are not ranked and are not a formula. They are weighed together in the round. There is no percentage of salved value, and an answer that offers one is wrong.
  • The salved value is a ceiling, not the basis. The reward must not exceed the salved value of the vessel and other property. Salved value influences the award strongly, but the award is not a proportion of it.
  • Two of the criteria exist to encourage professional salvors to stay in business - the availability of purpose-built salvage equipment, and its state of readiness. The Convention deliberately rewards a salvor for having maintained an expensive capability that was standing by, not merely for the hours worked on this casualty. That is the policy behind the whole regime and noting it shows the scheme has been understood.

7. The environmental exception - which qualifies the answers above

Pure 'no cure, no pay' produced a perverse result: a salvor attending a laden tanker aground in a sensitive area might work at great expense, prevent an enormous pollution incident, and yet recover nothing if the ship was ultimately lost. The Convention addresses this, and it changes both who pays and how much:

  • Article 14 - special compensation. Where the salvor has carried out salvage operations in respect of a vessel which by herself or her cargo threatened damage to the environment, and has failed to earn a reward under Article 13 at least equivalent to the special compensation, he is entitled to special compensation covering his expenses, which may be increased where he actually prevented or minimised environmental damage.
  • And it is paid by the SHIPOWNER ALONE. This is a genuine and important exception to the answer given at section 4 - special compensation is not shared proportionately among the salved interests, because it is not a reward for saving property. In practice it falls on the shipowner's P&I club.
  • SCOPIC. Article 14 proved difficult and expensive to assess, so the industry produced the Special Compensation P&I Clause, a contractual alternative incorporated into LOF by agreement. The salvor may invoke SCOPIC at any time and regardless of whether there is any threat to the environment, which removes the argument about whether the threat existed. Remuneration is then assessed on an agreed tariff of rates for personnel, tugs and equipment, plus an uplift, and the shipowner provides security through the P&I club. If SCOPIC exceeds the Article 13 award, the shipowner pays the difference; if the Article 13 award is greater, the salvor takes that instead, subject to a discount for having invoked SCOPIC.

The closing observation that ties the answer to the question as asked: the two demands the stem separates - who pays, and how much - are answered differently depending on which limb of the regime is engaged. For an ordinary property salvage the answer is the salved interests, proportionately, on the Article 13 criteria. Where the environment was threatened, a second and quite different payer appears - the shipowner alone - assessed on expenses rather than on salved value.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks and five separate interrogatives printed as one unbroken paragraph with no lettering. That layout is the trap. On a question printed as a), b), c) the candidate has a checklist; here there is nothing to count against, and the commonest failure is to write at length on the first demand, give the examples, and then run out of paragraph before the last three.

Before writing anything, number the demands in the margin: (1) the difference, (2) examples, (3) who pays, (4) which form, (5) how calculated. Then write five visible sections. The whole of the structural risk on this question is disposed of by that one step.

Draw the distinction on the right basis

The natural assumption is that pure salvage is the unpaid rescue and contractual salvage is the paid one. That is wrong and it will cost the whole of the first demand. Both are paid. The distinction is whether the parties agreed anything before the service began.

The corollary is the point that separates a good answer: contractual salvage is still salvage. It is not a different thing that happens to look similar. The Convention applies to both, and an arbitrator under Lloyd's Open Form applies the Convention's own criteria. The contract supplies the machinery for fixing the reward; it does not displace the law that creates the entitlement.

'Voluntary' does not mean unpaid

This is where candidates tie themselves in knots. The precondition of voluntariness means the salvor was under no pre-existing duty to render this service. A commercial salvage company that has signed a contract five minutes ago and expects a large award is still volunteering, because it was under no prior obligation to attend this casualty.

Who is not volunteering: the ship's own crew saving their own ship, because that is their duty; a tug doing what her towage contract already requires; a public body acting under a statutory duty. Getting these three examples out shows the concept is understood.

The towage boundary - the most useful example in the answer

Ordinary towage is a contracted service at an agreed price with no danger and no salvage element. But a towage can turn into a salvage. If the tow encounters danger that was not within the contemplation of the towage contract - the ship breaks adrift, the weather deteriorates beyond anything contracted for, the tug performs a service she was never engaged to perform - then a salvage claim arises from that point.

Use this as the third example. It illustrates the boundary better than either of the clean cases, and it is a real practical question that arises constantly.

Who pays - the answer that separates the scripts

The owners of all the property saved, each in proportion to the saved value of its own interest. Not the shipowner. Not the insurers. Not the master.

So on a laden ship the payers are the shipowner for the ship, the cargo owners for the cargo, the bunker owner - which may be the time charterer, not the shipowner - for the bunkers, and whoever had freight or hire at risk. Where a ship is fully laden with valuable cargo, the cargo's share can dwarf the ship's.

Then draw the distinction between liability and ultimate incidence. The property owners are liable. Behind them, hull underwriters indemnify the shipowner's share and cargo underwriters the cargo's share, and where the operation was for the common safety the whole may be brought into general average. The question asks who is responsible, and the answer is the property owners. Mentioning the insurance behind it shows commercial understanding; putting the insurers forward as the answer is wrong.

Two limits worth stating

  • The award cannot exceed the total salved value. A salvor never recovers more than he saved. This is the natural ceiling on the whole regime.
  • No reward is payable for saving life alone. A life salvor who took part in the operation gets a fair share of the property award - but if nothing was saved but people, there is no fund to pay from. Most candidates find this counter-intuitive and it is worth a mark for stating it correctly.

LOF - describe the machinery, not the edition

Name the form: Lloyd's Open Form, and at this sitting the current edition is LOF 2020. But do not build the answer on the edition. The machinery has been stable across editions, and the mark is for understanding what the form does:

  • It is 'open' - the sum is left open, so nothing is negotiated at the casualty.
  • It preserves no cure, no pay.
  • It is an agreement to arbitrate, in London, under Lloyd's procedure.
  • It provides for security, so the salvor is not left chasing many parties.

The single sentence that earns the mark on demand 4: LOF is not a price - it is an agreement about how the price will later be decided.

The Article 13 criteria - weighed, not scored

Ten criteria. Do not attempt to rank them and do not offer a percentage of salved value, because the Convention offers neither. They are weighed together in the round.

Three groupings help recall: what was achieved (salved value, measure of success, environmental protection); what it cost and risked (time, expenses, losses, risk of liability, danger faced); and what the salvor brought to it (skill, promptness, availability of salvage equipment, its readiness and value).

The last group is worth a sentence of commentary: the Convention deliberately rewards a professional salvor for having maintained an expensive standby capability, not merely for the hours he worked. Without that, nobody would keep salvage tugs available for casualties that may never come. Noting the policy shows the regime has been understood rather than listed.

Article 14 and SCOPIC - short but necessary

It is tempting to leave these out on a question that does not mention the environment. Include them briefly, because they change the answer to two of the five demands.

The problem they solve: a salvor who prevents a major pollution incident but ultimately fails to save the ship gets nothing under pure no cure, no pay. Article 14 special compensation fixes that - and crucially it is paid by the shipowner alone, in practice by his P&I club, not shared among the salved interests. That is a real exception to the answer at demand 3.

SCOPIC is the contractual replacement, incorporated into LOF by agreement, invoked by the salvor at any time whether or not there is an environmental threat, and assessed on an agreed tariff rather than argued about. Its practical virtue is that it removes the litigation about whether a threat to the environment existed at all.

Common mistakes

  • Do not quote any Article of the Convention verbatim, or any LOF clause by number. Give the substance.
  • Do not cite case authority.
  • Do not offer a percentage of salved value as the award, or any monetary figure or SCOPIC tariff rate.
  • Do not say the shipowner pays the award.
  • Do not import the Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 - at this sitting the Indian statute is the Act of 1958.

Examiner traps

  • "Who pays the salvage award?" The owners of ALL the property saved, each in proportion to the saved value of its own interest. Not the shipowner. Not the insurers.
  • "What is the difference between maritime and contractual salvage?" Whether there was a prior agreement - not whether money was paid. Both are paid.
  • "Is contractual salvage still salvage?" Yes. The Convention applies to both, and the arbitrator under LOF applies the Convention's own criteria.
  • "Does 'voluntary' mean unpaid?" No - it means under no pre-existing duty. A commercial salvor under a signed contract is still volunteering.
  • "When does a towage become a salvage?" When the tow meets danger beyond the scope of the towage contract and the tug does what she was never engaged to do.
  • "Is LOF a price?" No - it is an agreement to arbitrate on no cure, no pay.
  • "What percentage of salved value is the award?" There is no percentage. The criteria are weighed in the round, and the salved value is a ceiling.
  • "Who pays special compensation?" The shipowner alone, in practice through the P&I club - not the salved interests proportionately.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Your master has signed LOF. What are the first three things you do in the engine room?
  • Who on board has authority to sign LOF, and on whose instruction?
  • Your ship is saved but the cargo is lost. Who contributes to the award?
  • What is a maritime lien, and why does the salvor care about it?
  • What is the practical difference between Article 14 and SCOPIC to a shipowner?
  • Your tug is towing you and the weather deteriorates badly. When does her claim change character?

Memory framework

Preconditions, then the five printed demands, then the exception. PRECONDITIONS (1): danger · voluntary · success. Then the stem's five, in order: DIFFERENCE (2) - prior agreement or not; EXAMPLES (3) - passing ship, signed LOF, and towage as the boundary; WHO PAYS (4) - all the property saved, in proportion to saved value; THE FORM (5) - LOF, an agreement to arbitrate; HOW CALCULATED (6) - Article 13, unranked, salved value is the ceiling. Then the EXCEPTION (7) - Article 14 special compensation, shipowner alone, or SCOPIC. Anchor: five questions in one paragraph - number them before you write.

Uncertainty and the evidence level on this question

The evidence level here is lower than on most questions in this paper and it is stated rather than blurred. The Salvage Convention text is not held by the corpus, which classifies this treaty family as citation and index only. No Article is quoted verbatim anywhere; article numbers are cited only where the MIW record for QP2408-Q7 establishes them, and all content is given by substance. Every source line is INTERNAL REUSE VERIFIED and none is P1.

No monetary figure, no percentage of salved value and no SCOPIC tariff rate appears anywhere, and the answer says so positively, so that a candidate does not supply one. The Convention provides no formula and any figure would be an invention.

LOF 2020 is named as the edition current at this sitting and the answer deliberately does not depend on it, stating expressly that the machinery has been substantially stable across editions. No LOF edition register is held, so the edition is carried for re-verification.

No case authority is cited, because none is held, and India's party status to the Convention is not asserted - the printed stem says 'as per the salvage convention' and sets no Indian limb.

Five interrogatives are printed in one unlettered paragraph and three of them are routinely dropped.

A recognised subject in danger; a voluntary service, meaning no pre-existing duty; and success - a useful result, on the no cure no pay principle.

Nothing else in the question can be explained without them.

Both are counter-intuitive and both are marks.

Characterising it as a price rather than a mechanism is the error on demand 4.

Quick revision
15-second recall
FIVE demands, no lettering - number them first. Preconditions: DANGER · VOLUNTARY · SUCCESS (no cure no pay). (1) DIFFERENCE = was there a prior agreement? - not 'was there money?'. Pure salvage: entitlement from the act, by maritime law. Contractual: terms agreed first - still salvage, Convention still applies, arbitrator still uses Article 13. (2) EXAMPLES: passing ship tows a drifting vessel clear / master signs LOF with a professional salvor / ordinary towage is neither - but becomes salvage if danger arises beyond the contract. (3) WHO PAYS = owners of ALL property saved, each in proportion to its saved value - ship, cargo, bunkers, freight at risk. Shipowner pays only the ship's share. Secured by maritime lien + salvage security; H&M and cargo underwriters indemnify behind; may go into general average. Award ≤ total salved value. No reward for saving life alone. (4) FORM = Lloyd's Open Form, LOF 2020 current here - an agreement to ARBITRATE, not a price. (5) CALCULATION = London arbitration, Article 13 criteria - salved value, success, environmental efforts, danger, skill, time and expense, risk, promptness, availability and readiness of salvage equipment - unranked, weighed together, salved value is the ceiling. EXCEPTION: environmental threat → Art 14 special compensation, SHIPOWNER ALONE (P&I), or SCOPIC on an agreed tariff.

Major trap
Answering demand 3 with 'the shipowner'. The award is payable by the owners of ALL the property saved, each in proportion to the saved value of its own interest - so on a laden ship the cargo interests pay their share, which may exceed the ship's. Saying 'the insurers' is also wrong: they indemnify behind the liability, they are not the party responsible. The second trap is distinguishing pure from contractual salvage by whether money was paid rather than by whether there was a prior agreement.

  • The Article 13 criteria as listed, the proportionate liability rule, and the Article 14 special compensation regime including that it is payable by the shipowner alone. — Classified INTERNAL REUSE VERIFIED, not primary. The Salvage Convention text is not held by the corpus and was not read at source this session; the material comes from the MIW verification record for QP2408-Q7. If the Convention is ever acquired, the article numbers and the enumeration of the criteria should be checked directly against it. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
  • The description of SCOPIC - that the salvor may invoke it at any time regardless of any threat to the environment, and that remuneration is on an agreed tariff plus an uplift with P&I security. — No SCOPIC text or version is held and successive revisions have been issued. The substance described is the settled market understanding, but no version is asserted and the description should be checked against a held clause if one is acquired. (C_ACCEPTED_LIMITATION)
Understand this first

Most things that go badly wrong at sea are not caused by somebody not knowing what to do. They are caused by two people each holding half of what was needed and never putting the halves together. One man knows the pump has been isolated; the other knows somebody is about to work on it; neither knows what the other knows. The equipment was adequate, the procedures existed, and the accident happened anyway. The insight behind the safety management regime is that this is not a matter of individual carelessness to be solved by telling people to be careful. It is a systems problem, and it can be engineered against. If information has to pass between people for an operation to be safe, then the passing of that information is itself part of the operation and has to be designed, written down, practised and checked - exactly like a lubricating oil system. That is why a safety management code, which one might expect to be about equipment and procedures, spends so much of its attention on who tells whom, in what language, and with what record. The second idea comes from aviation and was adopted at sea. It was noticed that crashes were happening in aircraft where somebody in the cockpit knew perfectly well that something was wrong and did not say so effectively - or said it, and was not heard, because of who they were and who the captain was. The response was to train crews in a set of specific, teachable habits: say things back to confirm you heard them correctly, brief the team before a critical operation so everybody shares the same picture, and make it normal and safe for a junior person to challenge a senior one. Applied to a machinery space rather than a cockpit, that is what resource management means here. The third idea is the one the question closes on. Because communication is a process, it can be measured. If people report the near misses, if the audits look at handovers and permits rather than only at paperwork, and if management actually reads what comes back, then the failures show up as a pattern before they show up as a casualty.
