Model written answer

1. What the ISM Code actually says about communication

A point of accuracy first, because it earns a mark and prevents a fabrication: the ISM Code does not define 'effective communication' as a term of art. There is no definitions entry for it. What the Code does is impose a series of specific communication obligations, spread across several of its elements, and it is those obligations taken together that create the concept the question is asking about.

The obligations are these:

What the Code requiresWhy it is a communication requirement
A working language is established and used. The safety management system documentation is in a working language understood by the personnel, and where the working language is not one they understand, translation is provided.The Code's single most concrete communication provision. It recognises that a multinational crew cannot be assumed to share a language, and it makes the shared language a system requirement rather than a hope.
Personnel are able to communicate effectively in the execution of their duties related to the safety management system.This is the provision the question's phrase comes closest to. The test is functional - not fluency in the abstract, but the ability to do the job safely in the language actually used.
Procedures ensure that new personnel and personnel transferred to new assignments are given proper familiarisation, and that essential instructions are identified, documented and given before sailing.Familiarisation is a communication process, and the Code makes it a documented one.
Non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences are reported to the Company, investigated and analysed, with corrective action taken.It obliges information to travel upwards and ashore, which is precisely the direction it does not travel by itself.
The master's responsibility and authority - to implement the safety policy, motivate the crew, issue appropriate orders and instructions in a clear and simple manner, verify that requirements are observed, and review the system and report deficiencies ashore.The words 'clear and simple manner' are the Code's own, and they are worth quoting because they are the closest it comes to prescribing how to communicate.
The Company designates a person or persons ashore having direct access to the highest level of management, providing a link between the Company and those on board.It creates a communication channel that bypasses the ordinary commercial chain, so that a safety concern cannot be filtered out by the people it might embarrass.
Documents are available, valid, and obsolete documents promptly removed.A superseded procedure in circulation is a communication failure waiting to happen.
Drills and exercises for emergency preparedness, with the response organisation described.Emergency communication has to be practised, because it is the first thing that fails under pressure.

2. The concept those provisions create

Taken together, the Code treats communication not as a personal attribute but as a managed process which is part of the safety management system, and which is therefore documented, trained, audited and improved like any other part of it.

Four consequences follow, and stating them is what turns a list of Code provisions into an explanation:

  • Communication is effective when it produces the correct action, not when it has been sent. An instruction that was given, and was not understood, has failed. This is the operative test and it should be stated in those words.
  • It must work in both directions. The Code obliges information to flow down as orders and up as reports, and it is the upward flow that requires deliberate construction, because it is the one that does not happen naturally.
  • It must work between ship and shore. The designated person exists precisely so that the channel is not dependent on goodwill.
  • It must survive the conditions. Noise, alarms, fatigue, an unfamiliar accent over a handheld radio and the pressure of an emergency all degrade it, which is why the Code requires the arrangements to be documented rather than improvised.

3. The principles of Engine Resource Management

Resource management originated in aviation as crew resource management, was adopted on the bridge as Bridge Resource Management, and is applied in the machinery space as Engine Resource Management. The setting matters: the engine room is noisier, is frequently unattended, involves contractors and riding squads to a degree the bridge does not, and works to a different watch structure. The principles are these:

PrincipleWhat it means in a machinery space
Situational awarenessEvery member of the team holds an accurate and shared picture of the plant's state - what is running, what is isolated, what is being worked on, and what the ship is about to be asked to do. Most communication failures are a divergence between two people's mental models.
Workload and task managementRecognising overload and redistributing tasks. An overloaded engineer stops communicating first, and that is the warning sign.
Closed-loop communicationThe message is sent, read back by the receiver, and the readback confirmed by the sender. Three steps, not one. This alone eliminates a large class of failures.
Challenge and responseAny team member may question an instruction or an observation, and there is an agreed way of escalating if the concern is not resolved. It must be legitimate to challenge, or it will not happen.
Flattening the authority gradientA junior engineer or a rating must be able to say that something looks wrong to the chief. Where the gradient is too steep the information exists on board and never arrives.
Briefing and debriefingBefore a critical operation the team is briefed on the plan, the hazards, each person's role and the abort criteria; afterwards it is debriefed on what actually happened.
Use of all available resourcesPeople, manuals, alarms, trend data, the makers' service line, class, shore technical support. An engineer struggling alone with a problem when shore expertise is a phone call away is not managing his resources.
Error trappingDesigning the operation so that a single mistake is caught before it has an effect - independent verification, cross-checking, a second signature on an isolation.
Decision-making and standard phraseologyAn agreed structure for making and communicating decisions, and agreed words for critical items, so that meaning does not depend on the individual.

4. Applying the principles on board

  • Watch handover. A structured, documented handover against a checklist covering plant status, defects, work in progress, isolations and permits open, and anything expected during the coming watch - with the incoming engineer confirming understanding rather than merely signing. This is the highest-value single application of ERM in the engine room.
  • Standing orders and night orders. Written, specific, and acknowledged, so that the chief engineer's expectations are not a matter of interpretation at three in the morning.
  • Toolbox talks before a task. The briefing principle applied at working level - the plan, the hazards, who does what, what would make us stop.
  • Permit to work and isolation control. The formal, signed embodiment of closed-loop communication and error trapping: the isolation is made, verified independently, tagged, and the permit is not issued until both are confirmed. Nothing else in the engine room depends so completely on communication being right.
  • Closed-loop readback for critical operations. Blackout recovery, changeover of fuels, main engine manoeuvring, tank transfers and bunkering. Orders and confirmations repeated back verbatim.
  • UMS and alarm response protocols. Under unmanned machinery space operation the duty engineer may be alone and asleep when an alarm sounds. Who is called, by whom, and what is expected of them must be agreed in advance, because it will not be negotiated at the time.
  • Bunkering and cargo operations. Agreed signals, an agreed shutdown procedure, continuous communication maintained, and a means of stopping the operation immediately available to both ends.
  • Familiarisation of new joiners. The Code requires it and ERM explains why it matters: a new joiner's mental model of the plant is empty, and every assumption an experienced engineer makes about shared knowledge is false.
  • Debriefing after drills and after incidents. Where the learning actually happens, and the step most often skipped.

5. Applying the principles with outsiders

The stem asks about communication with outsiders as well as on board, and this half of the demand is frequently dropped. 'Outsiders' means everyone outside the engineering team:

OutsiderThe communication that matters, and the ERM principle behind it
Shore technical managementDefect reporting, spares, technical advice, and agreement on repair strategy. Use of all available resources - the ship should not solve alone what the office can help with. Reports must be factual and complete, because the office cannot see the plant.
The designated person ashoreThe Code's dedicated safety channel with direct access to the highest level of management. Its whole purpose is to flatten the authority gradient between ship and Company, so that a safety concern is not filtered by commercial pressure. Every seafarer must know who the DPA is and how to reach them.
Riding squads and contractorsThe single highest-risk interface in the engine room. They may not share the working language, will not know the ship's systems, and are not in the ship's watch structure. Requires induction, a permit-to-work regime that binds them, a named ship's officer responsible for them, and explicit isolation confirmation. Error trapping and closed-loop communication, formally applied.
Terminal and portBunkering and cargo interfaces - agreed signals, agreed rates and shutdown procedure, a common language, and an emergency stop available to both parties.
Bridge, pilot and tugs during manoeuvringThe engine room must know what the bridge intends before it is ordered. A pre-arrival briefing between master and chief engineer, covering expected manoeuvring, machinery limitations, power availability and any restrictions, is briefing and shared situational awareness across two teams - and its absence is a classic cause of engine failure at the worst moment.
Class surveyors and port State control officersAccurate, unevasive presentation of condition, records and defects. Attempting to conceal a defect is a communication failure with regulatory consequences.
Makers' service engineersPrecise symptom description and accurate data - a technical communication in which imprecision leads directly to the wrong part being flown out.

6. Two examples of occurrences caused mainly by communication failure

These two scenarios are constructed illustrations of recognised failure classes, not accounts of named casualties. They are given in that form deliberately, and a candidate should present them the same way rather than half-recalling a real investigation report.

Example 1 — an incomplete watch handover leading to loss of lubricating oil pressure.

  • What happened. During a watch, the duty engineer changed over the main engine lubricating oil filters and left the standby filter isolated pending cleaning of the element. The handover to the oncoming watch was verbal, hurried and taken in the machinery control room during a meal relief. The isolation was mentioned but not recorded and not confirmed. Later in the oncoming watch the running filter differential rose; the engineer attempted the routine changeover to the standby, which was still isolated, and lubricating oil pressure fell to the low-pressure alarm. The engine slowed down automatically and the ship was briefly without propulsion in coastal waters.
  • The communication failure. A change to the plant's configuration existed in one engineer's head and nowhere else. The two men's mental models of the system diverged - a failure of shared situational awareness created by an unstructured handover with no readback and no record.
  • How assessment or monitoring would have caught it. A documented handover checklist with an explicit line for isolations and work in progress, requiring the incoming engineer to confirm rather than sign, converts the omission into a required entry. Tagging of the isolated valve makes the configuration visible without depending on memory. And internal audit of handover records - actually sampling them rather than confirming that the form exists - would have shown handovers being completed with the isolation field blank, which is the pattern that precedes this event.

Example 2 — an isolation not confirmed between ship's staff and a riding squad.

  • What happened. A riding squad was engaged to renew a section of sea water pipework during a coastal passage. The permit to work was raised and the relevant pump was stopped, but the electrical isolation was requested by the squad's supervisor over a handheld radio in a language not fluently shared, and the ship's engineer understood a different pump to be the subject. The requested pump was stopped; the pump actually connected to the pipework being opened was not isolated and was on automatic standby. It started on a falling-pressure signal while the line was open, and a member of the squad was struck by the discharge. On the facts as constructed, the outcome was a near miss with minor injury; the same failure with a different fluid or a different pressure would not have been.
  • The communication failure. A critical instruction crossed a language boundary and an organisational boundary with no readback, no independent verification and no shared identification convention for the equipment. Neither party was careless; each was confident, and they were confident about different pumps.
  • How assessment or monitoring would have caught it. A permit-to-work system that binds contractors and requires the isolation to be physically verified and tagged by a named ship's officer, with the permit signed by both parties at the machine, removes the radio from the critical path altogether. Contractor induction covering the working language, the identification convention for machinery and the stop-work authority addresses the language boundary. Closed-loop readback with the equipment's unique identification repeated back traps the ambiguity. And monitoring: near-miss reporting of earlier occasions when equipment was misidentified over the radio - which almost certainly happened harmlessly before - would have flagged the hazard, and an audit of contractor permits would have shown isolations being recorded without independent verification.

The common feature of both, and worth stating as the analytical point: in neither case was anybody ignorant or negligent. In both cases the information required existed on board and did not reach the person who needed it in a form they acted on correctly. That is what distinguishes a communication failure from a competence failure, and it is why the remedy is a system remedy.

7. The assessment and monitoring machinery that closes the loop

The stem's closing words - 'through assessment or monitoring' - point back at the Code's own mechanisms, and the answer should finish there:

  • Reporting of non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences. The Code requires them to be reported, investigated and analysed with corrective action. Near-miss reporting is the most valuable safety data a ship produces, because near misses are numerous, free, and share their causes with the accidents that have not happened yet.
  • A reporting culture is the precondition for everything else. The Code can require reports; only the Company can make it safe to submit them. Where reporting is punished, the mechanism produces silence and management concludes, wrongly, that nothing is going wrong.
  • Internal audit. Audits must examine whether communication processes are actually working - sampling handover records, permits and isolations - rather than confirming that the forms exist. An audit that verifies the existence of a procedure has assessed nothing.
  • The master's review. The master's periodic review of the system and reporting of its deficiencies to shore management is a designed upward channel and should be used for exactly this class of finding.
  • Management review. The Company evaluates the effectiveness of the system. Trends in near misses, repeated non-conformities in the same area, and audit findings on handovers and permits are the evidence that communication is failing before an accident demonstrates it.
  • Risk assessment. Where a task is identified as depending on communication across a boundary - a contractor, a language, a watch change - the assessment should identify that dependence as a hazard in its own right and control it, which is precisely what a permit-to-work regime does.
  • Training, drills and debriefs. ERM training, and the deliberate debriefing of drills and incidents, are how the principles at section 3 become habits rather than knowledge.

The closing observation: the Code treats communication as an auditable process precisely so that its failures can be detected by monitoring rather than by casualty. That is the connection between the beginning and the end of this question.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The question prints a limb a) and then no b). That is a misprint on the source paper, not a missing question, and it is worth noticing because it means the whole question is one limb containing three separate instructions run together: explain effective communication under the Code, apply ERM on board and with outsiders, and give two worked examples with their avoidance analysis.

As with the salvage question on this paper, the absence of lettering is the hazard. There is nothing to count against, and the commonest failure is a long, comfortable essay on the importance of communication that never reaches the examples - which are worth roughly a third of the marks. Number the three demands in the margin before writing.

Say honestly that the Code defines no such term

The stem says 'as mentioned in the ISM Code', and a candidate naturally starts hunting for a definition. There is none. Saying so is a mark, not an admission. What the Code does is impose communication obligations across several elements, and the concept is what those obligations collectively create.

The provisions to have ready: the working language requirement; that personnel must be able to communicate effectively in the execution of their duties; familiarisation of new and transferred personnel; reporting of non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences; the master's duty to issue orders 'in a clear and simple manner'; the designated person ashore; document control; and drills.

The designated person is the one to develop if time is short. Its purpose is structural: it creates a safety channel with direct access to the highest level of management, deliberately bypassing the ordinary commercial chain, so that a concern cannot be filtered out by the people it might embarrass. That is a communication design decision and explaining it shows the Code has been understood.

The definition of 'effective' worth committing to memory

Communication is effective when it produces the correct action - not when it has been sent. An order given and not understood has failed, and the failure belongs to the system, not to the listener. One sentence, and it frames the whole answer.

ERM is not BRM, and it is not 'talk more'

The stem says Engine Resource Management. The principles are shared with the bridge - both descend from crew resource management in aviation - but write to the machinery space: higher noise, unattended operation, contractors and riding squads, a different watch structure, and an interface with a bridge team that does not see the plant.

And give the principles as named principles, not as encouragement. Situational awareness; workload and task management; closed-loop communication; challenge and response; flattening the authority gradient; briefing and debriefing; use of all available resources; error trapping; standard phraseology. A list of nine named principles is examinable; 'engineers should communicate well' is not.

Closed-loop communication - explain the three steps

It is the single most transferable technique in the answer and most candidates state it in half a line. It has three steps: the sender gives the message; the receiver reads it back; the sender confirms the readback is correct. Only the third step closes the loop. Skipping it leaves the sender assuming, which is exactly the state that produces both examples below.

Do not drop 'with outsiders'

Half the second demand, and routinely lost. Outsiders are not just the office. The list: shore technical management; the DPA; riding squads and contractors; terminal and port during bunkering and cargo; bridge, pilot and tugs during manoeuvring; class surveyors and PSC officers; makers' service engineers.

Two deserve development. Contractors and riding squads are the highest-risk interface in the engine room, because they combine an unfamiliar language, no knowledge of the ship's systems, and no place in the watch structure. And the pre-arrival briefing between master and chief engineer is shared situational awareness across two teams - the engine room needs to know what the bridge intends before it is ordered, and its absence is a classic cause of machinery failure at the worst possible moment.

The examples - construct them, and say that you have

The stem asks for two examples and does not require named casualties. Constructing a clear scenario is safer and scores better than half-remembering a real investigation report, where a misremembered detail is worse than no detail. Say in the answer that the scenarios are illustrative.

Choose failure classes that are recognisable and different from each other. A handover omission and a cross-boundary isolation failure work well because one is internal and one involves outsiders, which lets both halves of the second demand pay off in the examples.

Structure each example in three parts: what happened; where precisely the communication failed; and what assessment or monitoring would have caught it. The third part is not optional - the stem asks for it explicitly - and it is the part candidates omit.

The analytical point that lifts the answer

In a genuine communication failure nobody is ignorant and nobody is careless. The information needed existed on board and did not reach the person who needed it in a form they acted on correctly. Two competent people were each confident, and confident about different things. Stating that distinguishes a communication failure from a competence failure and explains why the remedy has to be systemic.

Close where the question closes - assessment and monitoring

The stem's final words point at the Code's machinery: near-miss and non-conformity reporting, internal audit, the master's review, and management review. Two observations are worth making rather than just listing them:

  • Reporting culture is the precondition. The Code can require reports; only the Company can make it safe to submit them. Where reporting is punished the mechanism produces silence, and management concludes that nothing is going wrong.
  • An audit that confirms a procedure exists has assessed nothing. It must sample the handover records, the permits and the isolations to see whether the process actually operated. That distinction is what makes monitoring capable of catching either of the examples before it happened.

Common mistakes

  • Do not invent a Code definition of 'effective communication'. There is none - say so.
  • Do not cite a named real casualty or attribute an account to an investigation report.
  • Do not name an ERM or BRM training course, provider or syllabus.
  • Do not write general communication theory - sender, channel, receiver, noise. The stem ties the answer to the Code.
  • Do not omit the two examples or their avoidance analysis. They carry roughly a third of the marks.

Examiner traps

  • "Where does the ISM Code define effective communication?" It does not. It requires that the working language be understood, that personnel can communicate effectively in the execution of their duties, and that safety-related instruction be given effectively. Say that honestly - it earns more than a definition that does not exist.
  • "Is ERM the same as BRM?" No. Same principles, different setting - and the engine room's differences matter: noise, watch patterns, dependence on the bridge, and contractors on board.
  • "What is closed-loop communication?" Message, read-back, confirmation. Three steps, and the third is the one that gets dropped.
  • "Give an example of a communication failure with an outsider." A shore contractor, a superintendent, a bunker barge, a repair yard, a tug, or the terminal. The stem says 'and with outsiders' and half of the scripts miss it.
  • "Are your examples real casualties?" Say plainly that they are constructed and illustrative. That is stronger than a half-remembered casualty attributed wrongly.
  • "How does the Code check that communication actually works?" Internal audit, master's review, non-conformity and near-miss reporting, and management review - the assessment and monitoring machinery the stem's last clause asks about.
  • "What is the strongest single barrier to communication in an engine room?" Authority gradient - the person who sees the problem is often the one least able to say so.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What is the working language on this ship, and how do you know everyone understands it?
  • Talk me through a handover you have done between watchkeepers during a critical operation.
  • A rating tells you something is wrong and you disagree. What do you do?
  • How would a near miss involving a misunderstood instruction reach the company?
  • What briefing do you give a shore contractor before he starts work in your engine room?
  • How would your safety management system show that a communication problem had been fixed?

Memory framework

What the Code says, what it means, then ERM applied twice, then the examples, then the loop. CODE (1): working language, communicate effectively in the execution of duties, effective instruction - no defined term, and say so. CONCEPT (2): understood, not merely transmitted. ERM PRINCIPLES (3). APPLIED ON BOARD (4) and WITH OUTSIDERS (5) - do not drop the second. EXAMPLES (6): two, constructed and labelled as such, each with its avoidance analysis. LOOP (7): report, audit, review. Anchor: closed loop - message, read-back, confirmation.

Regulation and source map

PointWhere it comes from
The working-language and communication requirementsISM Code as amended through MSC.353(92), held in the corpus
Reporting of non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrencesThe same Code
Internal audit, master's review and management reviewThe same Code
Engine Resource Management principlesEstablished human-element practice, with the STCW resource-management competences as their training home. No STCW table or column is cited by number
Closed-loop communication and authority gradientEstablished human-factors practice, stated by substance
The two worked examplesCONSTRUCTED AND LABELLED AS ILLUSTRATIVE. No casualty report is held, so no named real casualty is cited
The Indian frameMerchant Shipping Act, 1958. The 2025 Act is thirty-seven months future

Uncertainty and the constructed examples

The two examples the stem demands are constructed, and the answer says so in terms. No casualty report, investigation report or accident database is held by the corpus, so no named real casualty could be cited without inventing or misattributing an account. The scenarios are built as clearly-labelled illustrations of recognised failure classes, and the labelling is inside the answer itself so that a candidate does not repeat them as reported cases.

The ISM Code does not define 'effective communication' and the answer opens by saying so. That is a positive finding rather than a gap: the Code imposes three related requirements and creates the concept by implication, and an answer that supplies a definition the Code does not contain is inventing authority.

No ISM paragraph is quoted verbatim and no STCW table, section or column is cited by number, because the resource-management competences are used as background rather than as the source of any claim. No case authority is cited, because none is held.

Temporal risk on this question is low and structural. The ISM Code as amended through MSC.353(92) is operative and did not move between this sitting and its December 2023 donor, and no ISM amendment falls in the intervening ten months.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  5. __________d2 Applying the principles with outsiders
  6. __________d3 Two examples of occurrences caused mainly by communication failure
  7. __________d3 The assessment and monitoring machinery that closes the loop
Flashcards 14

1 what the Code actually says. 2 the concept those provisions create. 3 the ERM principles named. 4 applying them on board. 5 applying them with outsiders. 6 the two examples, each with its avoidance analysis. 7 the ISM assessment and monitoring machinery.

Three instructions inside one printed limb with no lettering to count against.

No. It imposes communication obligations across several elements - working language, ability to communicate effectively in the execution of duties, familiarisation, reporting, the master's clear and simple orders, and the designated person. The concept is what those collectively create.

Saying so is a mark; inventing a definition and attributing it to the Code is a fabrication.

When it produces the correct action - not when it has been sent. An order given and not understood has failed, and the failure belongs to the system.

One sentence that frames the entire answer.

To create a safety channel with direct access to the highest level of management, deliberately bypassing the ordinary commercial chain, so a concern cannot be filtered out by the people it might embarrass.

It is a communication design decision and explaining it shows the Code understood.

Situational awareness; workload and task management; closed-loop communication; challenge and response; flattening the authority gradient; briefing and debriefing; use of all available resources; error trapping; standard phraseology.

Named principles are examinable; 'communicate well' is not.

The sender gives the message; the receiver reads it back; the sender confirms the readback is correct. Only the third step closes the loop.

Most candidates give it in half a line and omit the confirmation, which is the step that matters.

A structured, documented watch handover against a checklist covering plant status, defects, work in progress, isolations and open permits, with the incoming engineer confirming understanding rather than merely signing.

It is where divergent mental models are created or prevented.

Shore technical management; the DPA; riding squads and contractors; terminal and port during bunkering and cargo; bridge, pilot and tugs during manoeuvring; class surveyors and PSC officers; makers' service engineers.

Half the second demand and routinely dropped; answering only 'the office' loses it.

They may not share the working language, do not know the ship's systems, and sit outside the watch structure. Controls: induction, a permit-to-work regime that binds them, a named responsible ship's officer, physical isolation verification and tagging, and closed-loop readback with unique equipment identification.

It is the setting of the second worked example and it links both halves of demand two.

An isolated standby LO filter was mentioned but not recorded at a hurried handover; the oncoming engineer later attempted the changeover and lost LO pressure. Failure: divergent mental models from an unstructured handover with no readback or record. Caught by: a handover checklist with an explicit isolations line, valve tagging, and internal audit that SAMPLES handover records.

An internal failure, and it pays off the on-board half of demand two.

An isolation was requested by a contractor over a handheld radio across a language boundary; the wrong pump was stopped and the pump on the open line auto-started. Failure: a critical instruction crossed a language and organisational boundary with no readback, no independent verification and no shared equipment identification. Caught by: a permit binding contractors with physical verification and tagging by a named officer, contractor induction, readback of the unique equipment ID, near-miss reporting of earlier misidentifications, and audit of contractor permits.

An external failure, and it pays off the outsiders half of demand two.

Nobody is ignorant and nobody is careless. The information existed on board and did not reach the person who needed it in a form they acted on correctly. Two competent people were each confident, about different things.

The analytical point that explains why the remedy must be systemic.

Reporting of non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences with investigation and corrective action; internal audit; the master's review and report of deficiencies ashore; and management review of the system's effectiveness. Plus risk assessment, training, drills and debriefs.

The stem's closing words point here and the answer must finish there.

Reporting culture is the precondition - the Code can require reports, only the Company can make it safe to submit them, and where reporting is punished the mechanism produces silence. And an audit that confirms a procedure exists has assessed nothing; it must sample handover records, permits and isolations.

These make the closing section an argument rather than a list.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Printed as a lone 'a)' with no 'b)' - but THREE instructions. (1) ISM: no defined term - obligations are: working language; personnel able to communicate effectively in the execution of their duties; familiarisation; reporting of non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences; master to issue orders 'in a clear and simple manner'; DPA with direct access to the highest level of management; document control; drills. Concept = communication is a managed, documented, auditable process. Test = did it produce the correct action. (2) ERM principles: situational awareness · workload management · closed-loop (send-readback-CONFIRM, three steps) · challenge and response · flatten the authority gradient · briefing and debriefing · use all available resources · error trapping · standard phraseology. ON BOARD: handover checklist, night orders, toolbox talk, permit to work and isolation control, readback for blackout/fuel changeover/manoeuvring/bunkering, UMS alarm protocol, familiarisation, debrief. OUTSIDERS: shore management, DPA, riding squads and contractors (highest-risk interface), terminal, master-chief pre-arrival briefing, class and PSC, makers. (3) TWO examples - incomplete handover → isolated standby LO filter → LO pressure loss; unconfirmed isolation across a language boundary with a contractor → pump auto-starts on an open line. Each closed with: handover checklist and tagging, permit binding contractors, readback with unique equipment ID, near-miss reporting, audit that SAMPLES records. Close on reporting · internal audit · master's review · management review, and say reporting culture is the precondition.

Understand this first

A bill of lading exists to solve a problem that is easy to state and was very hard to solve: a ship carrying goods takes weeks to arrive, and during those weeks the goods are worth money to somebody, but nobody can touch them. The seller has parted with his cargo and has not been paid. The buyer is being asked to pay for something he cannot see and which is somewhere in the Indian Ocean. A bank asked to finance the deal has nothing it can hold on to. And the ship's master, when he finally arrives, has to decide which of several strangers claiming the cargo is actually entitled to it. The bill of lading solves all four problems at once by turning the cargo into a piece of paper. Whoever holds the paper controls the goods, because the master will hand the goods over to the person who produces it and to nobody else. That single fact is what makes the document extraordinary. It is not just a receipt saying the goods were loaded, and not just a record of what the carrier agreed to do. It is a thing that stands in the place of the cargo, so that selling the paper sells the cargo, and pledging the paper pledges the cargo, even while the cargo itself is at sea. Once that is understood, the reason for making a bill out 'to order' follows immediately. If the paper names one particular consignee and nobody else, then the cargo can only end up with that person, and all of the flexibility disappears - it cannot be sold on, and a bank cannot take it as security, because a bank does not want to own a cargo of soya beans, it wants the ability to sell one if it is not repaid. A bill made out 'to order' can be passed from hand to hand by signature, so the cargo can be sold three times while the ship is still at sea and the bank has something worth holding. The second limb asks a subtler question. When a shipper books space on a ship, the deal is done at that point - a rate is agreed, a booking is confirmed, and the goods are delivered to the terminal. The bill of lading is printed and signed afterwards, by the carrier alone. So it is not really the contract; it is the carrier's own written record of a contract that already exists, and if it says something different from what was actually agreed, the shipper can prove what was actually agreed. But now the shipper endorses the bill to a buyer in Rotterdam. That buyer knows nothing of any telephone call about freight rates. All he has is the document, and everything he knows about his rights against the ship is written on it. It would be intolerable if the carrier could tell him that the real bargain was something else. So from that moment the document is not evidence of the contract any more. It is the contract. The same piece of paper, unchanged, has changed its legal character because it changed hands.

Memory cue. THREE DEMANDS UNDER TWO LETTERS. Functions: RECEIPT · EVIDENCE OF CONTRACT · DOCUMENT OF TITLE. The MAIN one is document of title - it is the one a sea waybill does not have, and it is what lets cargo be sold and pledged afloat. ORDER BILL = consigned 'to order' or 'to order of', transferred by ENDORSEMENT + DELIVERY, carrier delivers against one original presented. WHY 'to order' rather than a named consignee: (1) sell afloat - string sales, buyer unknown at shipment; (2) finance - the bank takes it as security, a straight bill gives none; (3) retain the right of disposal - endorse only when paid; (4) control delivery - no original, no cargo. Costs: mis-delivery, LOIs, fraud, stale bills. LIMB (b): normally only EVIDENCE because the contract was made BEFORE the bill was issued and the bill is signed by the carrier alone. It BECOMES the contract: (1) once transferred to a consignee or endorsee - the big one, the third party has only the document; (2) charterparty bill in an indorsee's hands - receipt for the charterer, contract for the holder; (3) no antecedent agreement at all; (4) where the parties agree it shall govern; (5) by statute - Indian Bills of Lading Act 1856. STATUTE BOOK HERE = 1856 Act + COGSA 1925 as amended 1993 (Hague-Visby). The 2025 Acts are 31 months FUTURE.

Show the core points to cover
1. Limb (a), first demand - the functions of a bill of lading, and which is the main one
  • Receipt for the goods - quantity, marks, and apparent order and condition
  • Evidence of the contract of carriage - not, normally, the contract itself
  • Document of title - transferable, and the key to delivery of the goods
  • The main function is the document of title function: it is the only one a sea waybill does not share
2. Limb (a), second demand - what an order bill is, and how it operates
  • Consigned 'to order', or 'to order of' the shipper or a named party - never to a named consignee simply
  • Transferred by endorsement and delivery; a bearer bill by delivery alone
  • The carrier delivers against presentation of one original, whereupon the others stand void
3. Limb (a), second demand - the reasons for making a bill out 'to order'
  • To sell the cargo afloat - string sales, and a buyer who is not known at the time of shipment
  • To finance the shipment - the bank takes the bill as security; a straight bill gives it none
  • To retain the right of disposal until payment - the seller endorses only when paid
  • To keep delivery under control - no delivery without an original
  • Costs: mis-delivery exposure, letters of indemnity, fraud, and the stale bill
4. Limb (b) - why the bill is normally only EVIDENCE of the contract
  • The contract is concluded before the bill is issued - at booking, on the fixture, or on shipment
  • The bill is signed by one party only and is issued after the bargain
  • So between carrier and shipper it is strong but not conclusive evidence of terms already agreed
5. Limb (b) - the circumstances in which the bill BECOMES the actual contract
  • THE PRINCIPAL CASE - once transferred to a consignee or endorsee. The third party was not party to the antecedent bargain and can look only at the document, so the bill's terms ARE the contract between him and the carrier
  • The charterparty bill in a third party's hands - a mere receipt for the charterer, the contract for an indorsee
  • Where there was no antecedent agreement at all - the bill issued on shipment is itself the contract
  • Where the parties so agree - a booking note providing that the carrier's bill terms shall govern
  • By statute - the Indian Bills of Lading Act 1856 vests the rights of suit in the transferee as if the contract had been made with him

  • Indian Bills of Lading Act, 1856 - transfer of rights of suit
  • Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925, as amended in 1993 - gives effect to the Hague-Visby Rules in India
  • Indian Sale of Goods Act, 1930 - a bill of lading is a document of title to goods

Model written answer

1. Limb (a), first demand - the functions of a bill of lading, and which is the main one

A bill of lading is a document issued by or on behalf of the carrier, on receipt or on shipment of goods, and signed by the master or by an agent for the master. There is no single statutory definition of it; it is described by what it does. It does three things.

1.1 A receipt for the goods

The bill records what the carrier acknowledges having received. Three things are stated, and each carries commercial consequences:

  • The leading marks necessary for identification of the goods.
  • The number of packages or pieces, or the quantity or weight, as furnished in writing by the shipper.
  • The apparent order and condition of the goods. This is the statement that matters most. A bill that carries no adverse remark is a clean bill, and it tells every subsequent holder that the cargo looked sound when it went aboard. A bill that carries a remark - torn bags, rust-stained coils, wet cartons - is claused or foul.

Two qualifications must be stated because both are examinable. First, the carrier is not obliged to state marks, number, quantity or weight which he has reasonable ground for suspecting do not accurately represent the goods, or which he has had no reasonable means of checking - which is the source of the familiar 'said to contain' and 'shipper's load, stow and count' reservations on container bills. Second, the receipt operates as prima facie evidence of the goods as described in the carrier's own hands, but once the bill has been transferred to a third party acting in good faith, proof to the contrary is not admitted. That escalation from prima facie to conclusive is deliberate: it exists so that a buyer or a bank can rely on the face of the document.

It is also why a letter of indemnity given in exchange for a clean bill on cargo that was not clean is improper. The shipper is asking the carrier to make a statement to the world that is untrue, and to indemnify him for the consequences of the deception. It is common; that does not make it lawful, and it is not enforceable against the party deceived.

1.2 Evidence of the contract of carriage

The bill sets out the terms on which the goods are carried - the parties, the ports, the description, the freight, and on the reverse the carrier's standard conditions, including the clause paramount that applies the Hague-Visby Rules to the contract. It is the written record of the bargain. It is not, in the ordinary case, the contract itself, and the reason for that, and the exceptions to it, are the whole of limb (b) below.

1.3 A document of title to the goods

This is the function that makes the instrument unique. The bill of lading is treated as representing the goods themselves, so that:

  • Delivery of the goods is made against presentation of an original bill, and not otherwise. The master delivers to the person who presents; when one original of the set is accomplished, the others stand void.
  • Transfer of the bill operates as a transfer of the constructive possession of the goods. Endorsing and delivering the document transfers control of the cargo, and it does so while the cargo is at sea and physically untouchable.
  • The bill can therefore be sold, and it can be pledged. A bank advancing money against a shipment takes the bill as security precisely because holding it means being able to obtain and sell the cargo if the borrower defaults.

The Indian Sale of Goods Act, 1930 includes the bill of lading in its definition of a document of title to goods, which is the statutory anchor for this function in Indian law.

1.4 Answering the singular demand - which is the MAIN function?

The stem asks for 'the main function' in the singular while also requiring detail, and the question should be answered rather than avoided:

The main function is the third one - the bill of lading as a document of title. The reasoning is straightforward and it is worth setting out, because the reasoning is what is being marked:

  1. The receipt function is not distinctive. A mate's receipt is a receipt. A sea waybill is a receipt. Any acknowledgment of goods received performs it.
  2. The evidential function is not distinctive either. A sea waybill is also evidence of the contract of carriage, and so is a booking note.
  3. Only the bill of lading is a document of title. The sea waybill - which is the instructive comparison, because it does everything else a bill does - is a receipt and is evidence of the contract, but it is not a document of title, it is not transferable by endorsement, and the goods are delivered to the named consignee on proof of identity without any document being surrendered.
  4. Every commercial use that makes the bill of lading indispensable - the CIF sale, the string sale of a cargo afloat, the documentary credit, the bank's pledge - depends on the third function and on nothing else.

A fair answer therefore says: the bill performs three functions; the receipt function is its historical origin and the evidential function is its everyday one; but the document of title function is the principal one, because it is the only one that could not be performed by some other document, and it is the one on which international trade in goods afloat actually depends.

2. Limb (a), second demand - what an order bill is, and how it operates

The reasons cannot be explained until the mechanism is clear, so the mechanism comes first.

The consignee box of a bill of lading may be completed in one of three ways:

FormHow it is completedHow it transfersNegotiable?
Straight (named) billTo a named consignee - 'Consigned to ABC Traders, Rotterdam'It does not. The named consignee is the only person entitledNo
Order bill'To order', 'To order of shipper', or 'To order of ABC Traders'By endorsement and delivery of the documentYes
Bearer bill'To bearer', or an order bill endorsed in blankBy delivery aloneYes, and the most freely

Three mechanical points follow, and each is a scoring point:

  • 'To order' without more means to the order of the shipper. The shipper remains in control until he signs the reverse of the document.
  • An endorsement may be special or in blank. A special endorsement names the next holder - 'Deliver to the order of XYZ Bank' - and that party must endorse again to pass it on. A blank endorsement is a bare signature, and converts the bill into a bearer bill that passes by delivery. That is convenient and it is dangerous, because a bill endorsed in blank is as good as cash to whoever picks it up.
  • Bills are issued in a set of originals, typically three, and delivery against any one accomplishes the set. That is a practical necessity of the days when originals were posted by different routes, and it is a standing fraud risk.

A point on terminology, because it is often stated loosely: an order bill is called negotiable, but it is not negotiable in the full sense that a bill of exchange is. A transferee cannot obtain a better title than his transferor had - a stolen bill endorsed in blank does not give the thief's transferee good title to the goods. The word is used in the shipping sense of transferable by endorsement and delivery, and an answer that says so shows the distinction has been understood.

3. Limb (a), second demand - the reasons for making a bill out 'to order'

The stem asks for the reasons for choosing an order bill instead of a named consignee, so the comparison must actually be made. There are four principal reasons and they are commercial, not legal.

3.1 To enable the cargo to be sold while it is afloat

In the commodity trades - crude oil, grain, coal, oilseeds, metals - a cargo is routinely bought and sold many times between loading and discharge, in what is called a string. At the moment of shipment the seller frequently does not know who the ultimate receiver will be, because that buyer has not yet bought.

A straight bill makes this impossible: the consignee box names one person, and the cargo can be delivered to that person and to nobody else. An order bill makes it routine: each sale in the string is performed by endorsing and delivering the document, and the final holder presents it at the discharge port. Selling the paper sells the cargo.

3.2 To finance the shipment - the reason banks require it

This is the most important commercial reason and the one most often given incompletely.

Under a documentary credit, the buyer's bank undertakes to pay the seller against presentation of specified documents, and the bill of lading is the principal one. The bank is advancing money against goods it will never see. Its security is that it takes the bill of lading as a pledge: if the buyer does not reimburse it, the bank holds the document, can obtain the cargo on arrival, and can sell it to recover its money.

That security only exists if the bill is transferable. A bill naming the buyer as consignee gives the bank nothing at all - the buyer can claim his cargo from the ship whether or not he has paid his bank. It is for this reason that credits normally call for a full set of clean on board bills of lading made out to order and blank endorsed, or to the order of the issuing bank. The order form is not a preference of the banks; it is the precondition of the financing.

3.3 To retain the right of disposal until payment

A seller who ships goods before he is paid is exposed. If he consigns them to the buyer by name, he has put the cargo beyond his own reach: on arrival the buyer takes delivery, and the seller is left with an action for the price against a party who may be in another jurisdiction and may be insolvent.

If instead he takes the bill to his own order, he has reserved the right of disposal. The goods are shipped, the voyage proceeds, and the seller still controls delivery, because nothing can be delivered without the document he is holding. He endorses it to the buyer, or hands it to the bank for release against payment, only when he is paid or is satisfied he will be. If the buyer defaults he can endorse to somebody else and sell the cargo to a different receiver.

3.4 To keep delivery under control, and to protect the carrier

The order bill also serves the carrier, and this is worth saying because it is the reason the practice is enforced rather than merely permitted. The master's obligation is to deliver to the person entitled, and on a cargo that has been sold three times he has no means of knowing who that is. The rule that delivery is made against surrender of an original bill converts an impossible enquiry into a simple one: the person holding the document is entitled, and the master need look no further.

3.5 The costs of the order bill - because a discussion that reports none is incomplete

  • The document must arrive before the ship. On short voyages it frequently does not, and the cargo cannot be delivered. The market's response is the letter of indemnity - delivery without production of the bill, against a bank-countersigned indemnity from the receiver. It is universal, it is outside the contract, and it removes the carrier's P&I cover for the mis-delivery it is designed to permit.
  • Mis-delivery is a strict exposure. A carrier who delivers to the wrong person, however innocently, is liable to the true holder, and the loss is the full value of the cargo.
  • Fraud. A set of originals in circulation, blank endorsements that pass by hand, and switch bills issued in substitution for an earlier set are all recognised fraud channels.
  • The stale bill. A bill presented too late under a credit is rejected, and the seller loses the benefit of the credit even though the shipment was perfect.

The balanced conclusion for limb (a): the order bill exists because international trade requires the cargo to be transferable and financeable while it is at sea, and the straight bill cannot do either. The price paid is that the document itself becomes a valuable and vulnerable thing, and the whole of the industry's documentary fraud and mis-delivery risk follows from that.

4. Limb (b) - why the bill is normally only EVIDENCE of the contract

The proposition the stem recites is correct and it must be explained before its exceptions can be discussed. There are three reasons, and they are all about timing and signature:

  1. The contract is concluded before the bill exists. Space is booked, a rate is agreed, a booking note or fixture note is exchanged, and the goods are then delivered to the carrier. The bargain is complete at that point. The bill of lading is prepared afterwards and issued on or after shipment.
  2. The bill is signed by one party only. It is signed by the master or by an agent for the carrier. The shipper does not sign it. A document signed by one side and issued after the deal is not naturally the agreement itself.
  3. It is issued as a consequence of the contract, not as its formation. The carrier issues it because he has already undertaken to carry, and because the shipper is entitled to demand it.

The legal consequence, as between carrier and shipper, is that the bill is very strong but not conclusive evidence of the terms. If the shipper can prove that the actual agreement differed from what the bill says - that a particular stowage was promised, or that a term now printed on the reverse was never part of the bargain - that evidence is admissible, because the parties are disputing the terms of a contract they both made and the document is only the record of it.

5. Limb (b) - the circumstances in which the bill BECOMES the actual contract

Five circumstances should be given, and they are set out in descending order of importance. The first is the answer the question is looking for.

5.1 Once the bill is transferred to a consignee or endorsee - the principal case

When the bill of lading is transferred to a third party - a consignee taking under it, or an endorsee - the terms of the bill constitute the contract between the carrier and that holder.

The reasoning is one of necessity and it should be given, because it is what shows the rule has been understood rather than memorised:

  • The transferee was not a party to the antecedent bargain. He was not on the telephone when the rate was agreed. He knows nothing of any booking note.
  • All he has is the document, and he has taken it - and very often paid for it - on the faith of what it says.
  • If the carrier were permitted to prove that the real agreement was something different, the document would be worthless as a trading instrument, because no buyer or bank could rely on it.
  • Therefore, in the transferee's hands, evidence of the antecedent agreement is not admitted to contradict the bill. The bill is not evidence of the contract any longer. It is the contract.

The same escalation was noted at section 1.1 for the statements of quantity and condition, which move from prima facie to conclusive on transfer to a good faith third party. Both movements happen for the same reason and at the same moment, and pointing that out is worth a mark: the document hardens as it moves away from the parties who made the bargain.

5.2 A bill issued under a charterparty, once it reaches a third party

This is the sharpest illustration on the whole question, because here the same document has two different legal characters at the same time, decided only by whose hands it is in:

The holderWhat the bill isWhy
The charterer himselfA receipt onlyThe contract of carriage between the owner and the charterer is the charterparty. The bill cannot displace it
An indorsee or a third-party shipperThe contract between the owner and that holderHe is not a party to the charterparty and may never have seen it; he contracts on the terms of the bill

Two consequences: the charterparty's own terms bind the holder only so far as they are effectively incorporated into the bill by an incorporation clause; and the Rules do not apply to charterparties as such, but they apply to a bill issued under one from the moment that bill regulates the relations between the carrier and a holder of it.

5.3 Where there was no antecedent agreement at all

The general rule assumes a prior bargain. Where there is none - goods delivered to a general ship and shipped without any booking, an ad hoc parcel accepted at the quay, a shipment on a liner service arranged with no prior communication of terms - there is no earlier contract for the bill to be evidence of. The document issued on shipment is the only statement of the terms, and it is therefore the contract itself.

5.4 Where the parties have agreed that it shall be the contract

The parties may simply provide for it, and in liner practice they commonly do. A booking note that states that carriage is subject to the terms and conditions of the carrier's bill of lading incorporates that document into the bargain in advance, so that when the bill is issued its terms are the agreed terms and there is nothing behind it for a shipper to prove. This is the least glamorous of the five circumstances and the most frequently encountered.

5.5 By statute - the transfer of rights of suit

The commercial rule described at 5.1 needs a statutory mechanism to give the holder the right to sue on it, because at common law only a party to a contract could sue on it. In India at this sitting that mechanism is the Indian Bills of Lading Act, 1856, whose first section vests in the consignee named in the bill, or the endorsee to whom property in the goods passes upon or by reason of the consignment or endorsement, all rights of suit, and the same liabilities, as if the contract contained in the bill of lading had been made with himself.

Two points to note. That statutory formula - 'as if the contract contained in the bill of lading had been made with himself' - is the clearest possible statement of the answer to limb (b): the legislature treats the bill as containing the contract in the transferee's hands. And the Act also preserves the shipper's own liability for freight, so the transfer does not release the original party.

6. The statutory frame operative at this sitting

The answer must be written on the statute book that was in force in February 2023, and this is the one question on the paper where that matters most, because the Indian statute book on this subject was replaced entirely some years after this sitting.

InstrumentPosition at February 2023What it supplies
Indian Bills of Lading Act, 1856In forceTransfer of rights of suit to the consignee or endorsee
Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925, as amended in 1993In forceGives effect in India to the Hague-Visby Rules - the carrier's duty of due diligence to make the ship seaworthy before and at the beginning of the voyage, the duty to care properly for the cargo, the contents of the bill as receipt, the one-year time bar, the package and weight limitation, and the nullity of clauses lessening liability
Indian Sale of Goods Act, 1930In forceIncludes the bill of lading within 'document of title to goods'
Merchant Shipping Act, 1958In forceThe governing Indian shipping statute
Bills of Lading Act, 2025 and Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 2025DO NOT EXIST - in force 10 September 2025, thirty-one months after this sittingThirty-one months future. Excluded. They repealed the 1856 and 1925 Acts and re-enacted the Rules with modifications
Merchant Shipping Act, 2025DOES NOT EXIST - commenced 15 March 2026, thirty-seven months after this sittingThirty-seven months future. Excluded

Two further points on the international layer. India is not a party to the Hamburg Rules, and the Rotterdam Rules are not in force; neither governs any Indian bill at this sitting. And the Rules apply of their own force only where a bill of lading or similar document of title covers the contract - which is one more reason the choice of document at section 2 above is a substantive choice and not a clerical one.

The closing observation that ties the two limbs together. Limb (a) asks why a bill is made transferable, and limb (b) asks when it becomes the contract. They have the same answer. Both the transferability and the hardening of the document into the contract exist to serve one person - the stranger who buys or lends against a cargo he cannot see, on the faith of a piece of paper. Everything distinctive about the bill of lading follows from the decision to protect him.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks and three demands under two printed letters. Limb (a) hides two of them - the main function, and the reasons for an order bill - and limb (b) carries the third. Write three visible sections, not two, because the commonest structural failure here is to give the functions in detail and then dispose of the order bill in three lines.

Limb (b) is where the question actually lives. It recites the settled proposition that a bill is evidence of the contract and asks when it becomes the actual contract. That demand has no counterpart anywhere in the solved corpus, and it is answered by explaining the general rule first and the exceptions second.

Read the question properly - there are three demands, not two

The paper prints (a) and (b), but limb (a) contains two separate demands: the main function, and the reasons for an order bill. Answer them as two sections. A script that runs them together almost always gives the functions in detail and then disposes of the order bill in three lines, which loses about a third of the marks.

Note also the bracketed (a) (b) lettering - this is the only question on this paper that brackets its limb labels. It signals nothing; do not read anything into it.

Answer the singular 'main function' - do not dodge it

The stem says 'the main function', in the singular, and also says 'in detail'. The two together mean: give the three functions properly, then commit to an answer on which is principal.

Commit to the document of title function, and give the reason: a sea waybill is a receipt and is evidence of the contract, but it is not a document of title. So the first two functions are not distinctive of a bill of lading, and the third is. Every commercial use that makes the bill indispensable - CIF sales, string sales afloat, documentary credits, bank pledges - rests on the third function alone.

That single comparison with the sea waybill is the most efficient scoring sentence in the answer, because it disposes of the singular demand with a reason instead of an assertion.

Explain the mechanism before the reasons

The reasons for an order bill make no sense until the examiner can see that you know what one is. Three lines will do it: consigned 'to order' or 'to order of' rather than to a named person; transferred by endorsement and delivery, or by delivery alone if endorsed in blank; and the carrier delivers against presentation of an original, one accomplished original voiding the rest.

Then draw the contrast that the stem actually asks for: the straight bill names one consignee, is not transferable by endorsement, and the cargo can go to that person and to nobody else.

The four reasons - and the one that is usually given badly

  • Sale afloat. Commodity cargoes are sold in strings, and at shipment the seller often does not know who the final receiver is. Only a transferable document permits it.
  • Financing. This is the important one and it is usually reduced to 'banks want it'. Say why: the bank advances money against goods it will never see, and its security is that it holds the bill as a pledge and can obtain and sell the cargo if it is not repaid. A bill naming the buyer as consignee gives the bank no security whatever, because the buyer can collect his cargo without it. The order form is the precondition of the credit, not a preference.
  • Retention of the right of disposal. The seller takes the bill to his own order, so the goods are afloat but still under his control, and he endorses only when paid. If the buyer defaults he can sell to somebody else.
  • Control of delivery, which protects the carrier too. The master cannot possibly know who has bought a cargo three times over. The presentation rule converts that impossible enquiry into a simple one.

Then add a short paragraph on the costs - the bill that arrives after the ship, the letter of indemnity that follows and the P&I cover it forfeits, mis-delivery liability, fraud with blank endorsements and switch bills, and the stale bill. An answer that presents the order bill as pure advantage reads as recited; one that prices it reads as understood.

Limb (b) is not a trick - explain the rule before the exception

The stem states that the bill is evidence of the contract. That statement is correct. Do not attack it. The question asks when the position is otherwise, and you cannot answer that without first saying why the general rule exists.

Give the three reasons: the contract was concluded before the bill existed (space booked, rate agreed, goods delivered); the bill is signed by the carrier alone; and it is issued as a consequence of the contract rather than as its formation. The consequence between carrier and shipper is that the bill is strong but not conclusive evidence, and evidence of the actual bargain is admissible.

The answer they want is the transfer to a third party

Once the bill is transferred to a consignee or endorsee, its terms are the contract between the carrier and that holder. Give the reasoning, not just the rule: the transferee was not party to the earlier bargain, has only the document, and has usually paid on the faith of it; if the carrier could prove some different agreement the document would be worthless as a trading instrument.

A high-scoring connection: this is the same movement as the one that makes the statements of quantity and apparent order and condition conclusive rather than prima facie once the bill reaches a good faith third party. Both hardenings happen at the same moment and for the same reason. Say so.

The charterparty bill - the best illustration available

Use it, because it shows the same document holding two characters at once. In the charterer's hands the bill is a mere receipt, because the contract between owner and charterer is the charterparty. In an indorsee's hands the same bill is the contract between owner and holder, since the holder is not party to the charterparty and may never have seen it. Charterparty terms bind him only so far as they are effectively incorporated.

Two more circumstances, quickly

  • No antecedent agreement at all - a parcel shipped with no prior booking. There is no earlier contract for the bill to evidence, so the bill is the contract.
  • The parties agree it shall govern - a booking note stating that carriage is subject to the terms of the carrier's bill of lading. Unglamorous and extremely common.

Then land it on the statute

The Indian Bills of Lading Act, 1856 vests in the consignee or endorsee all rights of suit 'as if the contract contained in the bill of lading had been made with himself'. That statutory phrase is the best single line available for limb (b), because the legislature itself speaks of the contract being contained in the bill.

Get the statute book right - this is the trap on this question

At this sitting the Indian frame is the Indian Bills of Lading Act, 1856, the Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993 giving effect to the Hague-Visby Rules, and the Indian Sale of Goods Act, 1930 for the document-of-title characterisation, with the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 as the governing shipping statute.

Do not write the Bills of Lading Act 2025 or the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 2025 into this answer. Both commenced on 10 September 2025 - thirty-one months after this paper was sat. They repealed the 1856 and 1925 Acts and re-enacted the Rules with modifications and with different article numbering. Material written for a 2025 or 2026 sitting on this subject is entirely wrong for this one, and this is the only question in the batch where the Indian statute book itself moved.

Common mistakes

  • Do not cite any Article or rule of the Hague-Visby Rules by number. Give the substance - no held source fixes the numbering for this sitting, and the later Indian re-enactment renumbers them.
  • Do not cite case authority. None is held, and no case name should appear.
  • Do not import the donor question's limb on the obligations of shipowner, carrier and consignee. It is not asked here.
  • Do not quote any bill of lading clause or condition. No form is held.
  • Do not give any package limitation figure, SDR value or monetary amount.
  • Do not say the order bill is 'negotiable' without qualifying it - a transferee takes no better title than his transferor had.
  • Do not describe the letter of indemnity as a normal or proper commercial practice. It is universal, and it is outside the contract.

Examiner traps

  • "Which Act governs bills of lading in India at this sitting?" The Indian Bills of Lading Act, 1856 and the Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993. Not the 2025 Acts - they commenced 10 September 2025, thirty-one months later.
  • "What is the main function?" The document of title function - because a sea waybill is also a receipt and also evidence of the contract, and is not a document of title.
  • "Why does a bank insist on an order bill?" Because it holds it as a pledge and can obtain and sell the cargo if it is not repaid. A bill naming the buyer gives the bank no security at all.
  • "Is an order bill negotiable in the same sense as a bill of exchange?" No. A transferee takes no better title than his transferor had. 'Negotiable' here means transferable by endorsement and delivery.
  • "When does the bill become the contract?" On transfer to a consignee or endorsee, who was not party to the antecedent bargain and has only the document.
  • "What is a charterparty bill in the charterer's own hands?" A mere receipt - the contract between owner and charterer is the charterparty. In an indorsee's hands the same bill is the contract.
  • "Is a letter of indemnity for a clean bill proper?" No. It is universal, and it asks the carrier to make an untrue statement to the world.
  • "What else changes at the moment of transfer?" The receipt statements harden from prima facie to conclusive - the same movement, at the same moment, for the same reason.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Your cargo has arrived and the receiver has no bill of lading. What happens next?
  • What is a letter of indemnity, and why does your P&I club dislike it?
  • Who signs the bill of lading on your ship, and on whose authority?
  • The mate wants to clause the bill and the shipper objects. What do you do?
  • How many original bills are issued, and what happens when one is presented?
  • What is a sea waybill, and why would a shipper use one instead?

Memory framework

Three demands, six sections. FUNCTIONS (1): receipt · evidence of contract · document of title - and the third is the MAIN one, because a sea waybill has the first two and not the third. MECHANISM (2): 'to order', transferred by endorsement and delivery, delivered against one original presented. REASONS (3): sell afloat · finance · right of disposal · control delivery - plus the costs, LOI and mis-delivery. Then limb (b): WHY ONLY EVIDENCE (4) - the contract was made before the bill existed and the carrier signs alone. WHEN IT BECOMES THE CONTRACT (5) - on transfer, the charterparty bill, no antecedent agreement, agreement that it shall govern, and the statute. STATUTE BOOK (6): 1856 + 1925 as amended 1993, and the 2025 Acts are thirty-one months future.

Regulation and source map
