Uncertainty, and the statute book that moved after this sitting

This is the only question in the 2023 batch so far on which the Indian statute book itself was replaced between the sitting and a corpus donor, and that is the live risk. The Bills of Lading Act 2025 and the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 2025 commenced on 10 September 2025 - thirty-one months after this paper - repealing the 1856 and 1925 Acts and re-enacting the Rules with modifications. The corpus's October 2025 answer to the same subject is written entirely on them, and its whole statutory layer is excluded here.

No article or rule of the Hague-Visby Rules is asserted by number anywhere, and the prohibition is substantive rather than formal. No carriage-convention text is held for this sitting - India Code publishes current law only and removed both Acts on repeal - and the two corpus donors cite the same provisions under two different numbering schemes, because the 2025 re-enactment renumbers. No held source fixes the numbering a February 2023 candidate would have found, so the substance is stated and the addresses are not.

The text of the 1856 Act is likewise not held. Its first section and the effect of that section are carried at INTERNAL REUSE VERIFIED provenance, and no wording of the Act is quoted - the statutory formula given in the answer is presented as substance.

One genuinely contested practical question is recorded as unresolved rather than decided: whether a carrier may lawfully deliver against a straight bill without production of the document. The answer states only that a prudent carrier requires production in any event, and states that as practice rather than as law.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 6 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________(a) Limb (a), first demand - the functions of a bill of lading, and which is the main one
  2. __________(a) Limb (a), second demand - what an order bill is, and how it operates
  3. __________(a) Limb (a), second demand - the reasons for making a bill out 'to order'
  4. __________(b) Limb (b) - why the bill is normally only EVIDENCE of the contract
  5. __________(b) Limb (b) - the circumstances in which the bill BECOMES the actual contract
  6. __________framing The statutory frame operative at this sitting
Flashcards 15

1 the three functions and which is main. 2 what an order bill is and how it is negotiated. 3 the four reasons for choosing it over a named consignee, plus its costs. 4 why the bill is normally only evidence. 5 the five circumstances in which it becomes the contract. 6 the Indian statute book operative at this sitting.

Two printed letters conceal three demands, and the second demand in limb (a) is the one habitually compressed.

A receipt for the goods - marks, quantity and apparent order and condition. Evidence of the contract of carriage. A document of title, transferable and the key to delivery.

Everything else on the question is built on these three.

The document of title function. A sea waybill is also a receipt and also evidence of the contract, but it is not a document of title - so only the third function is distinctive of a bill of lading, and CIF sales, string sales afloat, documentary credits and bank pledges all depend on it alone.

The stem asks in the singular; the sea waybill comparison answers it with a reason rather than an assertion.

By endorsement and delivery. A special endorsement names the next holder, who must endorse again; a blank endorsement is a bare signature and converts the bill into a bearer bill passing by delivery alone. Bills issue in a set of originals and delivery against any one accomplishes the set.

The reasons for using an order bill cannot be explained without the mechanism.

Because it advances money against goods it will never see, and its only security is holding the bill as a pledge so that it can obtain and sell the cargo if it is not repaid. A bill naming the buyer as consignee gives the bank no security at all - the buyer can collect the cargo whether or not he has paid.

The financing reason is the most important and is usually reduced to 'banks want it'.

The seller's continuing control over goods already shipped. By taking the bill to his own order he keeps the key to delivery, endorses it only when paid or secured, and can sell the cargo to a different buyer if the first defaults.

It is the reason a seller, as distinct from a bank, wants an order bill.

Documents that arrive after the ship, and the letters of indemnity that follow - outside the contract and forfeiting P&I cover; strict mis-delivery exposure for the full cargo value; fraud through blank endorsements, sets of originals and switch bills; and the stale bill rejected under a credit.

A discussion of why the order bill is used that reports no cost reads as recited.

Because the contract was concluded before the bill existed - space booked, rate agreed, goods delivered; because the bill is signed by the carrier alone; and because it is issued as a consequence of the contract rather than as its formation. Between carrier and shipper it is strong but not conclusive evidence.

Limb (b) cannot be answered without the general rule being explained first.

Principally on transfer to a consignee or endorsee. He was not party to the antecedent bargain, has only the document, and has usually paid on the faith of it - so evidence of any different agreement is not admitted against him, and the bill's terms are the contract between him and the carrier.

This is the answer the question is looking for and it is the demand with no donor in the corpus.

The receipt statements. The description of quantity and of apparent order and condition is prima facie evidence in the carrier's hands but becomes conclusive once the bill is transferred to a third party in good faith. The document hardens as it moves away from the parties who made the bargain.

Linking the two escalations shows the principle understood rather than two rules memorised.

In the charterer's own hands it is a mere receipt, because the contract of carriage between owner and charterer is the charterparty. In an indorsee's hands the same bill is the contract between the owner and that holder, and charterparty terms bind him only so far as effectively incorporated.

The sharpest available illustration that the character of the document depends on whose hands it is in.

Where there was no antecedent agreement at all, so the bill issued on shipment is the only statement of terms. Where the parties agree it shall govern - a booking note subject to the carrier's bill of lading terms. And by statute, through the vesting of rights of suit in the transferee.

Five circumstances make a complete limb (b); most scripts give one.

Its first section vests in the consignee named in the bill, or in the endorsee to whom property passes upon or by reason of the consignment or endorsement, all rights of suit and the same liabilities as if the contract contained in the bill of lading had been made with himself. It also preserves the shipper's own liability for freight.

The statutory phrase 'the contract contained in the bill of lading' is the best single line available for limb (b).

The Indian Bills of Lading Act 1856, the Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 1925 as amended in 1993 giving effect to the Hague-Visby Rules, and the Indian Sale of Goods Act 1930, under the Merchant Shipping Act 1958. NOT the Bills of Lading Act 2025 or the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 2025, which commenced 10 September 2025.

The only question in the batch where the Indian statute book itself was replaced between the sitting and a corpus donor.

Both are receipts and both are evidence of the contract of carriage. The waybill is not a document of title, is not transferable by endorsement, and the goods are released to the named consignee on proof of identity with no document surrendered. It is quick and safe, and it cannot be sold or pledged.

It isolates the one distinctive function and answers the singular demand.

Quick revision
15-second recall
THREE demands under two letters. FUNCTIONS: RECEIPT (marks, quantity, apparent order and condition - clean vs claused; prima facie, but CONCLUSIVE once transferred to a good faith third party) · EVIDENCE OF THE CONTRACT · DOCUMENT OF TITLE. MAIN = document of title, because a sea waybill is a receipt and is evidence of the contract but is NOT a document of title - so only the third function is distinctive, and CIF sales, string sales afloat, credits and bank pledges all rest on it alone. ORDER BILL: 'to order' / 'to order of', transferred by ENDORSEMENT + DELIVERY (blank endorsement makes it a bearer bill), carrier delivers against one original presented, others void. WHY, not a named consignee: (1) SELL AFLOAT - strings, buyer unknown at shipment; (2) FINANCE - bank holds it as a PLEDGE and can sell the cargo; a straight bill gives the bank nothing; (3) RIGHT OF DISPOSAL - endorse only when paid; (4) CONTROL DELIVERY - protects the master too. COSTS: bill arrives after the ship → LOI (outside the contract, forfeits P&I), mis-delivery, fraud, stale bill. LIMB (b): normally only EVIDENCE because the contract was concluded BEFORE the bill was issued, the bill is signed by the carrier alone, and it follows from the contract rather than forming it - so between carrier and shipper it is strong but not conclusive. IT BECOMES THE CONTRACT: (1) ON TRANSFER to a consignee or endorsee - he was not party to the bargain, has only the document, paid on the faith of it (same moment the receipt statements harden); (2) CHARTERPARTY BILL - receipt in the charterer's hands, contract in an indorsee's; (3) NO antecedent agreement at all; (4) parties AGREE it shall govern - booking note subject to carrier's bill terms; (5) STATUTE - Indian Bills of Lading Act 1856, rights of suit 'as if the contract contained in the bill of lading had been made with himself'. STATUTE BOOK: 1856 Act + COGSA 1925 as amended 1993 (Hague-Visby) + Sale of Goods Act 1930 + MS Act 1958. 2025 Acts = 31 months FUTURE. MS Act 2025 = 37 months FUTURE.

Critical numbers
  • THREE functions - receipt, evidence of the contract, document of title. The MAIN one is document of title
  • THREE ways of completing the consignee box - straight, order, bearer
  • FIVE circumstances in which the bill becomes the actual contract - transfer to a third party, the charterparty bill in an indorsee's hands, no antecedent agreement, agreement that it shall govern, and the statutory vesting of rights of suit

Critical regulation
The Hague-Visby Rules as given effect in India by the Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993 - the carrier's duty of due diligence before and at the beginning of the voyage, the duty to care properly for the cargo, the required contents of the bill as receipt including the apparent order and condition of the goods, the escalation of that statement from prima facie to conclusive on transfer to a good faith third party, and the nullity of any clause relieving or lessening the carrier's liability. With the Indian Bills of Lading Act, 1856, whose first section vests in the consignee or endorsee all rights of suit as if the contract contained in the bill of lading had been made with himself, and the Indian Sale of Goods Act, 1930, which includes the bill within documents of title to goods.
Major trap
Writing the answer on the Bills of Lading Act 2025 and the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 2025. Both commenced 10 September 2025 - thirty-one months after this sitting - and the corpus's own October 2025 bill of lading answer is written entirely on them, with renumbered Schedule articles. At February 2023 the operative statutes are the Indian Bills of Lading Act, 1856 and the Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993. The second trap is answering limb (b) by repeating the three functions: the question asks when the bill stops being evidence and becomes the contract, and the answer is on transfer to a third party who was never part of the original bargain. The third is treating the printed proposition in limb (b) as a statement to be refuted rather than a general rule whose exceptions are wanted.

Understand this first

A ship's propeller is the last step in a long chain. Fuel becomes heat, heat becomes shaft power, and shaft power becomes thrust. Each step loses something, but the last step loses the most that can still be recovered, and that is why so much design effort is spent on a piece of bronze that looks unchanged since the nineteenth century. To see where the losses are, it helps to think about what a propeller actually does. It pushes the ship forward by throwing water backwards. That is Newton's third law and there is no way round it - but it means that when the ship has passed, there is a column of water behind it moving astern that was not moving before. Somebody paid for the energy in that moving water, and it is doing nothing useful. That is the axial loss, and the only way to reduce it is to throw a larger mass of water backwards a little more slowly rather than a small mass very fast. That is why large slow-turning propellers are more efficient than small fast ones. But a propeller does not only throw water backwards. It is a rotating device, so it also leaves the water spinning. That spin is pure waste - it contributes nothing to driving the ship - and on a normal installation it is the biggest single loss that could in principle be recovered. Then there is simple friction on the blade surfaces, and finally, because there is high pressure on one side of a blade and low pressure on the other, at the open tip the two sides meet and the water rolls up into a vortex that trails away behind, taking energy with it. Those four losses are the whole story, and once you have them, every device in this question falls into place. The duct is an attack on the axial loss. The Kappel blade is an attack on the tip vortex. Contra-rotating propellers are an attack on the swirl, and they are the most direct attack available - a second propeller turning the other way straightens out the water the first one spun. Azimuth propulsion is different in kind: the propeller itself is not better, but it works in clean undisturbed water instead of in the disturbed wake behind a hull, and all the shafts, struts and rudders that used to drag along behind the ship have been deleted. One warning underlies the whole answer. None of these devices is simply better. Each buys a reduction in one loss by paying something somewhere else - drag, complexity, cost, reliability, or a benefit that only appears if the ship is operated the way the designer assumed. The examiner asks for merits and demerits because that trade is the subject.

Knowledge map
High-Efficiency Propellers - Ducted, Kappel, Contra-Rotating and Azimuth, with Merits and Demerits
  1. framing
    1. 1Why propeller efficiency is being pursued - the framing the stem supplies
      • The propeller is the last conversion in the chain and among the least efficient
      • Revised MARPOL Annex VI in force 1 November 2022; EEXI and CII applicable from 1 January 2023 - six weeks old
      • A propeller improves both the design index and the operational rating - few measures do both
    2. 2Where the losses occur - the map the rest of the answer hangs on
      • Propulsive efficiency = hull efficiency × open-water efficiency × relative rotative efficiency
      • AXIAL loss - kinetic energy left in the accelerated slipstream
      • ROTATIONAL loss - the race leaves the disc spinning; that swirl is wasted
  2. a)
    1. 3(a) Ducted propellers - an attack on the axial loss
      • An annular aerofoil round the disc; an accelerating duct raises velocity through it and cuts the thrust each blade must make
      • The duct is itself a lifting surface, so it generates thrust; it also closes the tip gap
      • Benefit rises with THRUST LOADING - tugs, trawlers, slow full-form ships
  3. b)
    1. 4(b) Kappel propellers - an attack on the tip vortex loss
      • Blade tips curved smoothly towards the suction side as an integrated continuous winglet - not a bolted-on device
      • Load is carried further outboard while the tip vortex is weakened and displaced; the analogy is the aircraft winglet
      • MERITS - tip loss cut at the same diameter; no appendage, so no added drag or maintenance item; smoother wake into the rudder, so less vibration; straight replacement retrofit
  4. c)
    1. 5(c) Contra-rotating propellers - an attack on the rotational loss
      • Two coaxial propellers turning in opposite directions; the after one recovers the swirl left by the forward one
      • Swirl is the largest single recoverable loss in a conventional installation
      • MERITS - highest theoretical gain; thrust split, so lower loading per propeller, less cavitation and vibration; torque reaction largely cancelled; smaller diameter for a given thrust where draught limits it
  5. d)
    1. 6(d) Azimuth propulsion - an attack on hull-side and appendage losses
      • A propeller on a unit that rotates through 360°; mechanical Z-drive, or a podded electric unit with the motor in the pod
      • Efficiency comes from the clean, uniform inflow and the deletion of shafting, struts, brackets and rudder - not from the blade
      • A pulling unit works in undisturbed water, which is the main gain
  6. closing
    1. 7Comparative judgement, and the instruction printed in the stem
      • The choice follows thrust loading and operating profile, not fashion
      • Heavily loaded and slow → the duct earns its drag. Lightly loaded and fast → the duct makes it worse
      • Moderately loaded single screw, clean wake → Kappel. Large swirl and complexity acceptable → contra-rotating. Manoeuvring-dominated or DP → azimuth

ANSWER THREE, NOT FOUR. MERITS AND DEMERITS FOR EACH. Framing at this sitting: revised Annex VI in force 1 Nov 2022, EEXI + CII applicable 1 Jan 2023 (six weeks old, no ship rated yet), Initial GHG Strategy 2018 - NOT the 2023 Strategy, NOT EU ETS. LOSS MAP: η = hull × open-water × relative rotative; losses are AXIAL · ROTATIONAL (swirl) · FRICTIONAL · TIP VORTEX; cavitation caps blade loading. DUCT → axial. Annular aerofoil, accelerating nozzle, duct itself makes thrust, closes tip gap. Merit: big gain at HIGH THRUST LOADING - tugs, trawlers, slow full ships; bollard pull; protection; steering nozzle. Demerit: NET PENALTY AT LIGHT LOADING; fouling, ice, debris; internal cavitation erosion. KAPPEL → tip vortex. Tip curved to the suction side, INTEGRAL geometry not an add-on, winglet analogy. Merit: gain at same diameter, no appendage, smoother wake so less vibration, drop-in retrofit. Demerit: modest, wake-specific, costly design and casting, falls away off design point. CONTRA-ROTATING → swirl (paper prints 'Cortra'). Two coaxial props opposite ways; aft one recovers swirl - the largest recoverable loss. Merit: highest theoretical gain; lower loading per prop; torque reaction cancelled; smaller diameter. Demerit: limited by MECHANICS not hydrodynamics - coaxial shafts, epicyclic gearing, the concentric SHAFT SEAL. AZIMUTH → clean inflow + appendage deletion. 360° unit, Z-drive or podded electric; the gain is NOT in the blade. Merit: undisturbed inflow, no shaft/strut/rudder, thrust any direction, no tunnel thrusters. Demerit: electrical conversion losses, cost, cannot open the pod at sea, sliprings and seals. CHOICE = thrust loading + operating profile. NO PERCENTAGES ANYWHERE.

Remember7 route headings
Cover35 core points beneath them

Memorise the 7 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 35 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. Why propeller efficiency is being pursued - the framing the stem supplies
  • The propeller is the last conversion in the chain and among the least efficient
  • Revised MARPOL Annex VI in force 1 November 2022; EEXI and CII applicable from 1 January 2023 - six weeks old
  • A propeller improves both the design index and the operational rating - few measures do both
  • And unlike slow steaming it costs the operator no time
  • Operative strategy is the Initial IMO GHG Strategy of 2018
2. Where the losses occur - the map the rest of the answer hangs on
  • Propulsive efficiency = hull efficiency × open-water efficiency × relative rotative efficiency
  • AXIAL loss - kinetic energy left in the accelerated slipstream
  • ROTATIONAL loss - the race leaves the disc spinning; that swirl is wasted
  • FRICTIONAL loss - viscous drag on the blade surfaces
  • TIP VORTEX loss - pressure equalises round the tip and sheds a vortex
  • Cavitation sets the ceiling on how hard a blade may be loaded
  • Each device attacks a different loss - that is the organising idea
3. (a) Ducted propellers - an attack on the axial loss
  • An annular aerofoil round the disc; an accelerating duct raises velocity through it and cuts the thrust each blade must make
  • The duct is itself a lifting surface, so it generates thrust; it also closes the tip gap
  • Benefit rises with THRUST LOADING - tugs, trawlers, slow full-form ships
  • MERITS - large thrust augmentation and bollard pull; propeller protection; course stability, or steering with a nozzle
  • DEMERITS - a net penalty at light loading; fouling, corrosion, debris and ice; internal cavitation erosion; weight, survey and maintenance
4. (b) Kappel propellers - an attack on the tip vortex loss
  • Blade tips curved smoothly towards the suction side as an integrated continuous winglet - not a bolted-on device
  • Load is carried further outboard while the tip vortex is weakened and displaced; the analogy is the aircraft winglet
  • MERITS - tip loss cut at the same diameter; no appendage, so no added drag or maintenance item; smoother wake into the rudder, so less vibration; straight replacement retrofit
  • DEMERITS - modest gain, highly wake-dependent; hull-specific design, CFD and model testing; complex geometry harder to cast, repair and re-pitch; benefit falls away off the design condition
5. (c) Contra-rotating propellers - an attack on the rotational loss
  • Two coaxial propellers turning in opposite directions; the after one recovers the swirl left by the forward one
  • Swirl is the largest single recoverable loss in a conventional installation
  • MERITS - highest theoretical gain; thrust split, so lower loading per propeller, less cavitation and vibration; torque reaction largely cancelled; smaller diameter for a given thrust where draught limits it
  • DEMERITS - limited by mechanics, not hydrodynamics: coaxial shafting and epicyclic gearing, and above all the concentric shaft seal; harder alignment, maintenance and drydocking; little large-merchant service experience
6. (d) Azimuth propulsion - an attack on hull-side and appendage losses
  • A propeller on a unit that rotates through 360°; mechanical Z-drive, or a podded electric unit with the motor in the pod
  • Efficiency comes from the clean, uniform inflow and the deletion of shafting, struts, brackets and rudder - not from the blade
  • A pulling unit works in undisturbed water, which is the main gain
  • MERITS - clean inflow and no appendage drag; full thrust in any direction, so no tunnel thrusters and no rudder; diesel-electric plant flexibility; aft-body freed for cargo
  • DEMERITS - electrical conversion losses offset part of the gain; high capital cost and complexity; the pod cannot be opened at sea and needs drydocking; slipring, bearing and seal reliability; appendage and structural drag of the pod at high speed
7. Comparative judgement, and the instruction printed in the stem
  • The choice follows thrust loading and operating profile, not fashion
  • Heavily loaded and slow → the duct earns its drag. Lightly loaded and fast → the duct makes it worse
  • Moderately loaded single screw, clean wake → Kappel. Large swirl and complexity acceptable → contra-rotating. Manoeuvring-dominated or DP → azimuth
  • ANSWER THREE, NOT FOUR, and give merits AND demerits for each
  • Give no percentage figures - they are vendor claims and hull-specific
Model written answer

1. Why propeller efficiency is being pursued - the framing the stem supplies

The stem opens by saying that improving propeller efficiency is 'a growing means to improve energy efficiency of ships'. That sentence is the examiner's premise and should be answered before the devices are described, because it explains why any of this is being done.

The engineering reason. The propeller is the last energy conversion in the chain and one of the least efficient. Resistance rises steeply with speed, so a small improvement in propulsive efficiency returns real fuel over a voyage - and unlike slow steaming, which is the other obvious lever, it costs the operator no time.

The regulatory reason at this sitting, which is six weeks old. The position in February 2023 is precise and it is worth stating precisely:

  • The revised MARPOL Annex VI adopted by resolution MEPC.328(76) entered into force on 1 November 2022 - three months before this sitting.
  • Its chapter 4 requirements apply from 1 January 2023 - six weeks before this sitting. Existing ships must have an attained EEXI not exceeding the required value under regulations 23 and 25, and ships above the applicable size must determine an annual operational carbon intensity and be rated A to E under regulation 28.
  • The enhanced SEEMP, including the Part III carbon intensity plan, has been required on board from the same date.
  • The operative strategy behind all of it is the Initial IMO Strategy on reduction of GHG emissions from ships, of 2018.

Why this makes propeller work unusually attractive. Most efficiency measures improve one thing or the other. A propeller improves both: it lowers the attained EEXI, which is a design index calculated once, and it lowers fuel per tonne-mile in service, which improves the annual carbon intensity and therefore the rating. Very few retrofits do both, and fewer still do both without costing speed.

An important qualification about the rating, which fixes the answer to this sitting. The first CII reporting year began on 1 January 2023 and the rating is determined only after the calendar year has ended. At the date of this paper no ship holds a CII rating. The pressure is therefore anticipatory - owners are acting on what they expect their rating to be and on the requirement that a ship rated D for three consecutive years or E in any year must submit a corrective action plan - and the answer is written that way rather than as a description of ratings already received.

The EEXI reduction-factor framework itself is treated at Q3 of this paper and is not repeated here.

2. Where the losses occur - the map the rest of the answer hangs on

The devices cannot be compared unless it is clear what each of them attacks. Overall propulsive efficiency is conventionally decomposed as:

Propulsive efficiency = hull efficiency × open-water (propeller) efficiency × relative rotative efficiency

Three of the four devices in this question attack the middle term; the fourth attacks the first. The open-water efficiency is limited by four loss mechanisms:

LossWhat it isAttacked by
AxialKinetic energy left in the slipstream, which is accelerated astern and does no further useful work. Reduced by moving a larger mass of water more slowly - hence large, slow-turning propellersThe duct
Rotational (swirl)The race leaves the disc spinning. That rotation contributes nothing to thrust and is the largest single recoverable loss in a conventional installationContra-rotating propellers
FrictionalViscous drag over the blade surfaces. Reduced by blade area no larger than needed, good section design and a clean, smooth, unfouled surfaceBlade design and maintenance
Tip vortexAt the open blade tip the pressure difference between face and back equalises, rolling up into a trailing vortex that carries energy awayThe Kappel blade

Two constraints sit over all of them. Cavitation sets the ceiling on how hard a blade may be loaded - push a section too hard and the low-pressure side vaporises, which destroys thrust, erodes the blade and generates noise and vibration. And the propeller does not work in clean water: it works in the ship's wake, which is non-uniform, so each blade passes through a varying inflow once per revolution. That is the source of the pressure pulses felt in the aft accommodation, and it is why a device that works on one hull may do nothing on another.

3. (a) Ducted propellers - an attack on the axial loss

3.1 The principle

A ducted propeller runs inside an annular aerofoil - a nozzle, commonly called a Kort nozzle - fitted concentrically around the propeller disc with a small tip clearance.

  • In the usual accelerating form, the duct's internal section converges towards the propeller plane, so the water velocity through the disc is increased. The propeller therefore has to produce less thrust for the same total, because it is working on a faster stream.
  • The duct is itself a lifting surface. The flow round its aerofoil section generates a force with a forward component, so the duct contributes thrust of its own - typically a substantial share of the total on a heavily loaded installation.
  • It also closes the tip gap, so the tip vortex is largely suppressed as a secondary benefit.
  • A decelerating duct - diverging towards the disc - is used instead where the object is to raise pressure at the propeller and suppress cavitation and radiated noise, at some cost in efficiency. Naval and research vessels use it.

The governing variable is THRUST LOADING - thrust per unit of disc area. The higher it is, the more the duct earns.

3.2 Merits

  • Large thrust augmentation at high loading. On tugs, trawlers, pushers and slow full-form ships the gain is substantial, and bollard pull in particular improves markedly.
  • Thrust at low speed and when towing - precisely the conditions in which an open propeller is least efficient.
  • Protection of the propeller from ropes, nets, floating debris and quay contact.
  • Course stability with a fixed nozzle; and with a steerable nozzle, a large gain in manoeuvring capability, since the whole jet is directed.
  • Suppression of the tip vortex, and often reduced propeller-excited hull vibration as a consequence.

3.3 Demerits

  • It is a net penalty at light loading, and this is the essential qualification. The duct has its own frictional and form drag, which it carries all the time. On a fast, fine-form, lightly loaded ship that drag exceeds the thrust the duct generates, and fitting one makes the ship worse. A duct is not an improvement in itself; it is an improvement in the right application.
  • Fouling, corrosion and mechanical damage. The duct is a large wetted surface that fouls, and its internal surface is exposed to debris and ice. Tip clearance is small and critical; a distorted duct can foul the propeller.
  • Cavitation erosion on the internal surface, in the high-velocity region near the blade tips, is a recognised maintenance problem.
  • Added weight, structure and support, with the associated survey burden and drydock work.
  • Poor astern performance in some configurations, since the duct is shaped for one flow direction.

Fit it when: the ship is slow, full-formed and heavily loaded, or does towing or trawling work. Do not fit it when: the ship is fast and lightly loaded.

4. (b) Kappel propellers - an attack on the tip vortex loss

4.1 The principle

The Kappel propeller is a conventional propeller whose blade tips are curved smoothly towards the suction side, forming a continuous integrated winglet.

  • The curvature is part of the blade geometry, cast in. It is not an end plate, fin or cap bolted to a standard blade, and the distinction carries the whole of its merit case.
  • The aeronautical analogy is exact enough to be worth stating: an aircraft winglet reduces induced drag by weakening and displacing the wing-tip vortex, and the curved propeller tip does the same to the blade-tip vortex.
  • The effect is to allow load to be carried further outboard - where a blade element is most effective, because it moves fastest - while the vortex that would normally result is weakened and shed further from the blade.

4.2 Merits

  • Reduced tip vortex loss at the same diameter. This matters because diameter is very often fixed by draught, aperture or ground clearance, and this device improves efficiency without needing any more of it.
  • No additional appendage. There is nothing extra in the water, so no added drag, no added structure, no additional item to survey and nothing extra to foul or lose.
  • A smoother, more uniform wake into the rudder, which reduces hull pressure pulses, vibration and radiated noise - a real benefit on ships where aft accommodation vibration is a complaint.
  • Retrofittable as a straight replacement. The ship takes a new propeller at a scheduled docking; no hull modification, no shafting change and no change to the machinery.

4.3 Demerits

  • The gain is modest compared with the duct at high loading or with contra-rotation, and it is highly dependent on the wake field of the particular hull. It is not a general-purpose improvement.
  • It requires hull-specific design - wake measurement or computation, CFD and normally model testing - so the design cost is high and cannot be amortised across a class unless the ships are truly identical.
  • The curved geometry is harder and more expensive to cast, to inspect, to repair after damage and to re-pitch.
  • The benefit falls away when the ship operates away from the design condition - a different draught, trim or speed than the one the blade was optimised for. A ship whose trading pattern changes may not keep the gain it paid for.

Fit it when: a moderately loaded single-screw ship with a reasonably clean wake needs a gain without a hull change, and the trading pattern is stable.

5. (c) Contra-rotating propellers - an attack on the rotational loss

A note on the printed word. The paper prints 'Cortra rotating Propellers'. There is no such device; the intended device is the contra-rotating propeller, and it is answered as such. The misprint is noted once and not laboured.

5.1 The principle

  • Two propellers on the same axis, one behind the other, turning in opposite directions.
  • The forward propeller leaves its race spinning. The after propeller, turning the other way, is set in that swirling flow and recovers the rotational energy directly, converting it into additional thrust instead of allowing it to be shed astern.
  • This is the most direct attack available on any of the losses, because rotational loss is the largest single recoverable loss in a conventional installation - typically the biggest item after the unavoidable axial loss.
  • Mechanically it requires coaxial shafting, an inner shaft running inside a hollow outer shaft, driven through epicyclic gearing that reverses one output; or, in the modern arrangement, a conventional propeller forward with an azimuthing podded unit aft carrying the second.

5.2 Merits

  • The highest theoretical gain of the devices in this question, and gained by direct recovery rather than by redistribution.
  • The total thrust is divided between two propellers, so the loading per propeller falls for the same total thrust.
  • Lower loading means a reduced cavitation risk, lower blade stresses and reduced propeller-excited vibration.
  • Torque reaction is very largely cancelled between the two units, which improves course keeping and removes the steady helm bias a single screw induces.
  • A smaller diameter can deliver a given thrust, which is valuable where draught or aperture limits diameter - exactly the constraint that limits the conventional route to efficiency.

5.3 Demerits

The limitation is mechanical, not hydrodynamic, and saying so is the point that shows the device is understood. The hydrodynamic case has never been in doubt; what has kept the arrangement rare in large merchant ships is engineering:

  • Coaxial shafting and the associated epicyclic gearing are complex, heavy and expensive, and they occupy space aft.
  • The concentric shaft sealing arrangement is a recognised reliability weak point. Two shafts through one gland, one rotating inside the other, is a hard sealing problem and it is the failure that operators fear.
  • Alignment, bearing loads, maintenance and drydock work are all harder, and a defect on the inner shaft is not a small job.
  • Limited service experience in large merchant ships, so owner and class confidence is low and residual value is uncertain - a commercial demerit that is as real as the technical ones.
  • The podded variant substitutes electrical conversion losses and pod maintenance for the gearing problem; it does not remove the complexity, it relocates it.

Fit it when: the rotational loss is large, the diameter is constrained, and the owner can accept the mechanical complexity and the maintenance exposure.

6. (d) Azimuth propulsion - an attack on hull-side and appendage losses

Only three of the four devices are to be answered. This one is included so that a candidate who chooses it is served, and because the reason it is the least natural choice of the four is itself instructive.

6.1 The principle

  • A propeller mounted on a unit that rotates through 360° about a vertical axis, so that thrust can be directed in any horizontal direction. There is no rudder.
  • Two forms: the mechanical Z-drive, in which engine power is taken down through bevel gearing into the rotating unit; and the podded electric drive, in which an electric motor sits inside the submerged pod and drives the propeller directly, with power supplied through sliprings.
  • The efficiency gain is not in the blade. It comes from two other places, and a candidate who locates it in the propeller has misunderstood the device. First, a pulling unit ahead of its own strut works in clean, undisturbed, largely uniform inflow, instead of in the disturbed and non-uniform wake behind a hull - which raises open-water efficiency and reduces cavitation and vibration. Second, the arrangement deletes the shafting, stern tube, shaft brackets, struts and rudder from the water altogether, and with them their appendage drag.
  • A secondary and often decisive gain is arrangement freedom: with diesel-electric machinery the prime movers can be sited anywhere and run at their most efficient loading, and the aft body can be shaped for flow rather than for a shaft line.

6.2 Merits

  • Clean inflow to the propeller, giving higher open-water efficiency, lower cavitation risk and markedly lower vibration and radiated noise.
  • No shafting, struts or rudder in the water - a direct reduction in appendage resistance.
  • Full thrust in any direction. Manoeuvrability is transformed: no rudder is needed, stern thrusters become unnecessary and bow thruster requirements are reduced, and the arrangement is the enabling technology for dynamic positioning.
  • Machinery arrangement freedom with an electric plant, and the ability to run generators at efficient load and shut down surplus sets.
  • Hull-form and cargo benefits - the aft body is freed from the shaft line and can be shaped hydrodynamically or used for capacity.

6.3 Demerits

  • Electrical conversion losses offset part of the gain. Generator, switchboard, transformer, converter, motor and sliprings each take their share, and on a ship that runs at steady sea speed a direct-drive shaft line remains hard to beat on pure transmission efficiency.
  • High capital cost and system complexity, and a plant that demands electrical competence on board that a conventional engine room does not.
  • The pod cannot be opened at sea. A motor, bearing or seal defect inside the pod is a drydocking, not a repair. This is the single most serious operational demerit and it dominates the risk assessment.
  • Sliprings, steering-gear bearings, thrust bearings and shaft seals in the pod are known maintenance and reliability items, and the sealing of a rotating shaft in a submerged unit is unforgiving.
  • The pod and its strut are themselves appendages. On a fast, fine ship their drag can consume much of the benefit, which is why the arrangement is common on cruise ships, ferries, icebreakers and offshore vessels and rare on fast cargo liners.
  • Vulnerability to grounding and ice contact, and a structurally demanding mounting.

Fit it when: manoeuvring, station-keeping, low noise or arrangement flexibility dominate - passenger ships, ferries, icebreakers, offshore and DP vessels. It is the weakest of the four choices for a straight 'high-efficiency propeller' answer, because its gain is a system gain rather than a propeller gain; a candidate choosing it must be able to say so.

7. Comparative judgement, and the instruction printed in the stem

DeviceLoss attackedBest applied toPrincipal demerit
DuctedAxialSlow, full-form, heavily loaded; tugs and trawlersA net penalty at light loading
KappelTip vortexModerately loaded single screw, diameter constrained, stable trading patternModest and wake-specific gain; costly design and casting
Contra-rotatingRotational (swirl)Large swirl loss, diameter constrained, complexity acceptableMechanical - coaxial shafting, gearing and the concentric seal
AzimuthHull-side and appendage; clean inflowManoeuvring, DP, low noise, arrangement flexibilityConversion losses; the pod cannot be opened at sea

The governing principle is that the choice follows the thrust loading and the operating profile, not fashion. The single best test of whether a candidate has understood the subject is whether they can say that a duct on a fast, lightly loaded ship makes the ship worse.

Two instructions printed in the stem must be obeyed. It asks for any three of the four - three treated properly will always outscore four treated superficially, and the natural three are the duct, the Kappel and the contra-rotating arrangement, because each attacks a different named loss and the answer then has a spine. And it asks for merits and demerits: a device presented with advantages only forfeits about half of its marks however well the principle is explained.

Finally, offer no percentage figures. Published gains for ducts, tip-modified blades, contra-rotating arrangements and pods vary widely, depend entirely on hull form, wake field, loading and operating profile, and originate very largely from the manufacturers. A quantified claim cannot be defended in an examination answer, and a mechanistic explanation of why a device gains is worth more than a number that cannot be supported.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks, and the structure is dictated by three instructions printed in the stem that candidates routinely break: answer any three of the four devices, give merits and demerits for each, and take seriously the opening sentence about improving the energy efficiency of ships, which is the examiner's own framing and not decoration.

The organising idea that turns a list into an answer is the loss map. Give propulsive efficiency and the four loss mechanisms first, then locate each device against the loss it attacks. Two short paragraphs at the front make every later paragraph land somewhere.

Three instructions are hidden in the stem, and candidates break all three

  • 'Any three of the following.' Four are printed; write three. Writing four is the commonest error on this question and it converts three good treatments into four thin ones.
  • 'With its merits and demerits.' Both. A device described with advantages only loses roughly half its marks no matter how well the principle is explained.
  • The opening sentence is not decoration. 'Improving efficiency of propellers is recognized as a growing means to improve energy efficiency of ships' is the examiner's premise, and two short paragraphs answering it are cheap marks that most scripts leave on the table.

Get the framing right for THIS sitting - it is six weeks old

In February 2023 the position is precise and stating it precisely is worth real credit: the revised MARPOL Annex VI entered into force on 1 November 2022; EEXI and the CII rating requirements apply from 1 January 2023; the enhanced SEEMP including Part III has been required on board since the same date; and the operative strategy is the Initial IMO GHG Strategy of 2018.

Do not write that ships have been rated. The first rating year began six weeks before this paper and the rating is determined only after the calendar year ends. No ship holds a CII rating at this sitting. Write the driver as anticipatory - owners acting on the rating they expect, and on the corrective-action-plan consequence of a D-for-three-years or a single E.

Do not write in the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy (five months future), the EU ETS extension to shipping (eleven months future), or anything from the Net-Zero Framework (thirty-two months future). Material written for a 2025 or 2026 sitting on this same question reaches for all three.

Learn the loss map first - it is the spine of the whole answer

Propulsive efficiency = hull efficiency × open-water efficiency × relative rotative efficiency. Then four losses: axial (energy left in the accelerated slipstream), rotational or swirl (the race leaves the disc spinning - the largest single recoverable loss), frictional (viscous drag on the blades), and tip vortex (pressure equalising round the open tip). Cavitation caps how hard a blade may be loaded.

Then say which device attacks which: duct → axial. Kappel → tip vortex. Contra-rotating → swirl. Azimuth → hull-side and appendage losses, plus clean inflow. Once that sentence is on the page the answer has a structure and every later paragraph has somewhere to attach.

The duct - and the qualification that carries the marks

An annular aerofoil round the disc. The accelerating form raises velocity through the propeller so each blade produces less thrust; the duct is a lifting surface so it generates thrust itself; and it closes the tip gap. The decelerating form is for cavitation and noise suppression at some cost in efficiency.

The mark is in the qualification: the duct's benefit rises with THRUST LOADING, and on a fast, lightly loaded, fine-form ship it is a net penalty because its own drag exceeds the thrust it makes. If you write one qualifying sentence in this whole answer, write that one.

Kappel - insist that the curvature is integral

Tips curved smoothly towards the suction side as a continuous integrated winglet, cast into the blade. It is not a bolted-on end plate, and the whole merit case depends on that: no appendage, no added drag, nothing extra to survey, and a drop-in retrofit at a scheduled docking.

Its honest weaknesses: the gain is modest, it is specific to the hull's wake field, it needs CFD and model testing so the design cost is high, the geometry is dearer to cast and repair, and the benefit falls away if the ship trades away from its design draught and speed.

Contra-rotating - say why a better device is rare

Two coaxial propellers turning opposite ways; the after one recovers the swirl left by the forward one. Since swirl is the largest recoverable loss, this is the most direct attack available, and it also splits the thrust between two propellers - so lower loading, less cavitation, less vibration, cancelled torque reaction, and a smaller diameter for a given thrust.

Then make the point that separates the scripts: the constraint is mechanical, not hydrodynamic. Coaxial shafting, epicyclic gearing, and above all the concentric shaft seal, which is the recognised reliability weak point. Add the commercial demerit - little large-merchant service experience, so owners and class are cautious. That is why a theoretically superior arrangement is uncommon.

The paper prints 'Cortra'. It is a misprint for contra. Name the device correctly, note the misprint in one clause, and move on - it shows you know the subject rather than the page.

Azimuth - if you choose it, locate the gain correctly

A propeller on a unit rotating through 360°, either a mechanical Z-drive or a podded electric unit with the motor in the pod. The gain is not in the blade. It comes from the clean undisturbed inflow to a pulling unit and from deleting the shafting, struts and rudder from the water. Say that explicitly; a candidate who claims the propeller itself is more efficient has misplaced the mechanism and it shows.

Demerits worth having: electrical conversion losses eat part of the gain; capital cost and complexity; the pod is an appendage in its own right and its drag can consume the benefit on a fast ship; and the operational one that dominates every risk assessment - a defect inside the pod cannot be repaired at sea and means drydocking.

This is the weakest of the four for a 'high-efficiency propeller' answer, because its gain is a system gain. Prefer the duct, the Kappel and the contra-rotating arrangement as your three - they attack three different named losses, which gives the answer a spine.

Close with a judgement, not a summary

The choice follows thrust loading and operating profile, not fashion. Heavily loaded and slow, the duct earns its drag; lightly loaded and fast, it makes matters worse. Moderately loaded single screw with a clean wake, a tip-modified blade. Large swirl and complexity acceptable, contra-rotating. Manoeuvring or station-keeping dominant, azimuth. A short closing paragraph of that kind is worth more than a restatement of what has already been said.

Common mistakes

  • Do not answer all four devices. Three, properly.
  • Do not give a percentage efficiency gain for anything. The published figures are hull-specific and largely vendor-sourced, and none can be defended in an examination.
  • Do not name a manufacturer's product, or claim how many ships carry a device.
  • Do not present the duct as an unqualified improvement.
  • Do not describe the Kappel tip as a fitted or bolted-on winglet.
  • Do not attribute contra-rotation's rarity to hydrodynamic doubt. It is mechanical.
  • Do not import the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, EU ETS or any Net-Zero Framework content - all are future at this sitting.
  • Do not say ships have been rated A to E. None had been at this date.
  • Do not repeat the EEXI reduction-factor detail from Q3 of this paper; refer to it and get on with the propellers.

Examiner traps

  • "Does a duct always improve efficiency?" No. Its benefit rises with thrust loading; on a fast, fine, lightly loaded ship its own drag exceeds the thrust it makes and the ship is worse.
  • "Is the Kappel tip a fitted winglet?" No - it is integral cast blade geometry. The whole merit case depends on it: no appendage, no added drag, nothing extra to survey, drop-in retrofit.
  • "Why are contra-rotating propellers rare?" Mechanics, not hydrodynamics - coaxial shafting, epicyclic gearing, and above all the concentric shaft seal.
  • "Where does a pod's efficiency gain come from?" Not from the blade - from clean, undisturbed inflow to a pulling unit and from deleting shafting, struts and rudder.
  • "What is the worst operational demerit of a pod?" It cannot be opened at sea. A defect inside means drydocking.
  • "Which loss is the largest recoverable one?" Rotational - the swirl left in the race.
  • "How much will a duct save?" Give no percentage. Published figures are hull-specific and largely vendor-sourced.
  • "What is the regulatory driver at this sitting?" EEXI and the CII rating, applicable six weeks ago, under the revised Annex VI in force since 1 November 2022 and the Initial GHG Strategy of 2018 - and no ship has been rated yet.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Your ship has a fixed-pitch propeller and a fouled hull. Which do you clean first, and why?
  • How would you know, from the ship's own data, whether a propeller polish had paid for itself?
  • What would you inspect on a nozzle at drydocking?
  • Why is cavitation erosion worse on the inside of a duct than on the blade?
  • What would you tell an owner asking whether to fit a pod to an existing cargo ship?
  • How does a propeller change affect the EEXI Technical File?

Memory framework

Framing, loss map, then the devices, then the judgement. FRAMING (1): revised Annex VI in force 1 Nov 2022, EEXI and CII applicable 1 Jan 2023, Initial GHG Strategy 2018, no ship rated yet. LOSS MAP (2): axial · rotational (largest recoverable) · frictional · tip vortex, with cavitation capping blade loading. Then one device per loss: DUCT (3) → axial; KAPPEL (4) → tip vortex; CONTRA-ROTATING (5) → swirl; AZIMUTH (6) → hull-side and appendage. JUDGEMENT (7): the choice follows thrust loading and operating profile. Anchor: one loss each - and answer three, not four.

Regulation and source map

PointWhere it comes from

EEXI under regulations 23 and 25, and the CII rating under regulation 28Corpus canonical MARPOL Annex VI regulation records, regulations 22 to 28. P1

The operative strategyInitial IMO GHG Strategy, MEPC.304(72) of 2018
Propulsive efficiency, the four loss mechanisms, and the ducted, Kappel and contra-rotating devicesMIW INTERNAL REUSE VERIFIED from the verification records for QP2510-Q4 and QP2403-Q4, which print this identical stem
Azimuth propulsionENGINEERING JUDGEMENT. Neither donor treats the fourth printed device; it is authored here from established marine engineering
That no instrument prescribes a propeller typeStated expressly, so that the regulatory framing is not mistaken for a design requirement

Uncertainty, and why the donors say less than this answer does

The donor audit on this question produced a result that runs the opposite way from the usual one, and it is recorded rather than smoothed: the donors do not over-claim, they UNDER-claim. Both exact instances of this stem carry an empty regulations list, and each records expressly that MARPOL Annex VI was not read in a licensed consolidated edition, so neither asserts any regulation number or any date for EEXI or CII. That was correct on the evidence those sessions held. It does not bind this paper, because this paper holds the primary material for a different question - Q3 reads MEPC.328(76) at source and the paper consumes the corpus's canonical Annex VI regulation records - so the framing here is stated at P1 provenance.

No percentage efficiency gain is given for any device anywhere, and the reason is stated positively in the answer so that a candidate does not supply one. Published figures vary widely, depend entirely on hull form, wake field, thrust loading and operating profile, and originate very largely from manufacturers. No manufacturer, product or installed population is named; 'Kort nozzle' and 'Kappel' are used as the established generic terms the stem itself invokes.

No quantified loss budget is asserted. That rotational loss is the largest single recoverable loss is stated as a settled qualitative proposition with no percentage attached, because no held source supports one for any particular hull. No naval architecture text, model test report or CFD study is held.

The framing depends on the open corpus correction TSCR-3 - the amendment register records the entry into force of MEPC.328(76) a year late. The correction is carried, not re-raised, and the corpus was not modified.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________framing Why propeller efficiency is being pursued - the framing the stem supplies
  2. __________framing Where the losses occur - the map the rest of the answer hangs on
  3. __________a) (a) Ducted propellers - an attack on the axial loss
  4. __________b) (b) Kappel propellers - an attack on the tip vortex loss
  5. __________c) (c) Contra-rotating propellers - an attack on the rotational loss
  6. __________d) (d) Azimuth propulsion - an attack on hull-side and appendage losses
  7. __________closing Comparative judgement, and the instruction printed in the stem

The pod cannot be opened at sea. A motor, bearing, slipring or seal defect inside the submerged unit means drydocking, not a repair. Electrical conversion losses through generator, converter and motor also offset part of the hydrodynamic gain.

It is the operator's real objection, and it is more convincing than a list of components.

Slow, full-formed, heavily loaded, or towing - a duct. Moderately loaded single screw with a clean wake and a stable trade - Kappel. Large swirl loss, constrained diameter, complexity acceptable - contra-rotating. Manoeuvring, DP, low noise or arrangement flexibility - azimuth.

The closing judgement is worth more than a summary of what has already been said.

Revised MARPOL Annex VI in force since 1 November 2022; EEXI and the CII rating applicable from 1 January 2023, six weeks earlier; enhanced SEEMP including Part III required on board from the same date; and the Initial IMO GHG Strategy of 2018 as the operative strategy. No ship yet holds a CII rating.

Precise dating earns credit and keeps later strategy and EU ETS material out.

Answer ANY THREE of the four printed devices, not all four; and give MERITS AND DEMERITS for each, not advantages alone. Three treated properly outscores four treated superficially, and a device given without demerits loses about half its marks.

Both are printed instructions and both are routinely broken.

A misprint for CONTRA-ROTATING propellers. No device of that name exists, and both other corpus instances of this identical stem print 'Contra'. Name the device correctly, note the misprint once, and answer it.

Naming it correctly shows the subject is known rather than the page copied.

Quick revision
15-second recall
ANY THREE OF FOUR. MERITS AND DEMERITS FOR EACH. FRAMING AT THIS SITTING: revised Annex VI MEPC.328(76) in force 1 Nov 2022; EEXI (regs 23, 25) and CII rating (reg 28) apply from 1 Jan 2023 - six weeks old; enhanced SEEMP Part III required on board from the same date; operative strategy = Initial IMO GHG Strategy 2018. NO SHIP HOLDS A CII RATING YET - first rating year is six weeks old and the rating comes after the year ends. EXCLUDED: 2023 GHG Strategy (+5 mo), EU ETS (+11 mo), Net-Zero Framework (+32 mo). A propeller improves BOTH the design index and the operational rating, and costs no time. LOSS MAP: ηD = ηH × ηO × ηR. Losses: AXIAL (slipstream KE) · ROTATIONAL/SWIRL (largest recoverable) · FRICTIONAL · TIP VORTEX. Cavitation caps blade loading; the propeller works in a non-uniform wake. DUCT → AXIAL. Annular aerofoil; accelerating duct raises velocity through the disc; duct itself makes thrust; closes tip gap. Decelerating duct = cavitation/noise. Benefit rises with THRUST LOADING. M: bollard pull, low-speed thrust, protection, course stability, steerable nozzle. D: NET PENALTY AT LIGHT LOADING, fouling/ice/debris, internal cavitation erosion, weight and survey. KAPPEL → TIP VORTEX. Tip curved to suction side, INTEGRAL cast geometry, winglet analogy, load carried further outboard. M: gain at same diameter, no appendage, smoother wake so less vibration, drop-in retrofit. D: modest, wake-specific, CFD + model testing, hard to cast/repair/re-pitch, lost off design condition. CONTRA-ROTATING → SWIRL (paper prints 'Cortra'). Two coaxial props opposite directions, aft one recovers swirl directly. M: highest theoretical gain, thrust split so lower loading, less cavitation and vibration, torque reaction cancelled, smaller diameter for given thrust. D: MECHANICAL not hydrodynamic - coaxial shafts, epicyclic gearing, the concentric SHAFT SEAL, hard alignment and docking, little merchant experience. AZIMUTH → HULL-SIDE + APPENDAGE. 360° unit; Z-drive or podded electric. Gain is NOT in the blade - it is clean inflow to a pulling unit + deletion of shafting, struts and rudder. M: clean inflow, no appendages, thrust any direction, no rudder, DP enabler, plant flexibility, aft body freed. D: conversion losses, cost and complexity, pod cannot be opened at sea, sliprings/bearings/seals, pod drag on fast ships. CHOICE = thrust loading + operating profile. NO PERCENTAGES. NO MANUFACTURERS.

Critical regulation
MARPOL Annex VI as revised by resolution MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022. Chapter 4 applies from 1 January 2023: regulations 23 and 25 require an existing ship's attained EEXI not to exceed the required EEXI, and regulation 28 requires the annual operational carbon intensity indicator to be determined and rated A to E, with a corrective action plan where a ship is rated D for three consecutive years or E in any year. The enhanced SEEMP, including the Part III carbon intensity plan, is required on board from the same date. The operative policy instrument is the Initial IMO Strategy on reduction of GHG emissions from ships, 2018 - NOT the 2023 Strategy, which is five months future at this sitting. No instrument prescribes any propeller type or design concept; the regulations create the driver, not the design.
Major trap
Answering all four devices when the stem asks for any three, and giving merits only when it asks for merits and demerits. On the engineering, the trap is presenting the ducted propeller as an unqualified improvement: its benefit rises with thrust loading and on a fast, lightly loaded ship the duct's own drag makes the ship worse. On the framing, the trap is importing later law - the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy (five months future), the EU ETS extension to shipping (eleven months future) and any Net-Zero Framework material (thirty-two months future) - and, subtler, writing that ships have been rated A to E when at this sitting the first rating year was six weeks old and no ship held a rating at all.

Understand this first

Almost every other maritime convention protects something other than the people on board. SOLAS protects the ship and the lives in an emergency, MARPOL protects the sea, STCW makes sure the crew are competent. The Maritime Labour Convention is the one that protects the seafarer as a worker - his wages, his hours, his food, his accommodation, his right to go home - and that difference in subject creates a difference in enforcement that is the whole point of this question. The difficulty is obvious once stated. A safety defect can be seen: a corroded hatch cover is a corroded hatch cover whoever looks at it. A labour breach usually cannot. Whether a seafarer has been paid, whether his rest hours are genuine or manufactured, whether he signed his employment agreement freely or was told to sign it - none of that is visible to an inspector walking round the deck, and the only person who knows is the one with the most to lose by saying so. So the Convention builds enforcement in three layers, and the four limbs of this question are three of those layers plus India. The first layer is the flag State, which certifies the ship, inspects her regularly, and issues a certificate saying that conditions on board meet the standards. The second is the port State, which cannot inspect every foreign ship from scratch - it would be unworkable - so it starts by accepting the flag State's certificate at face value and looks no further unless something gives it a reason to. The third layer exists because the first two can both be satisfied on paper while the seafarer is still unpaid: the complaint procedures, which give the seafarer himself a route, first on board and then ashore. That third layer is why the misprinted limb of this question matters so much. The paper prints 'Compliant Procedures'. The word is Complaint, and it is not a detail - the complaint procedures are the mechanism by which the person who actually knows what is happening can make it visible. Everything else in Title 5 depends on somebody telling somebody. And that also explains the last limb. An Indian seafarer's problem is usually not that no rule exists; it is that the ship is under a foreign flag, the owner is a company he has never met in a country he has never visited, and the person who hired him was an agent in Mumbai. So the useful question is not 'what does the Convention say' but 'who, in practice, can he go to' - and the answer runs through India not as the flag State, which it usually is not, but as the country that supplied the labour and licensed the agent.

Knowledge map
MLC 2006 - Flag and Port State Responsibilities, Complaint Procedures, Detainable Deficiencies, and Indian Grievance Redressal
  1. framing
    1. 1Which edition applies, and how the Convention is built
      • ILO instrument, in force 20 August 2013 - the 'fourth pillar' alongside SOLAS, MARPOL and STCW
      • Articles · Regulations · Code Part A Standards (mandatory) · Code Part B Guidelines (guidance)
      • Five Titles; Title 5 is compliance and enforcement and splits into flag State, port State and labour-supplying responsibilities
  2. a)
    1. 2Limb a) - Flag State and Port State responsibilities
      • FLAG: an effective system of inspection and certification; may authorise recognized organizations but remains fully responsible
      • Maritime Labour Certificate + DMLC at 500 GT on international voyages; 5 years, intermediate inspection between the 2nd and 3rd anniversaries; interim 6 months
      • DMLC Part I by the competent authority, Part II by the SHIPOWNER - the classic trap
  3. b)
    1. 3Limb b) - the on-board and onshore COMPLAINT procedures
      • ON-BOARD - Regulation 5.1.5. Fair, effective and expeditious; resolution at the lowest level, but direct access to the master and to external authorities preserved
      • A copy of the procedure to every seafarer, with contacts and the name of an impartial adviser on board
      • Victimization is PROHIBITED AND PENALIZED - the provision that makes the rest usable
  4. c)
    1. 4Limb c) - detainable deficiencies
      • The Convention publishes no list. It gives TWO GROUNDS
      • (1) Conditions clearly hazardous to the safety, health or security of seafarers
      • (2) A serious or repeated breach of the requirements, including seafarers' rights
  5. d)
    1. 5Limb d) - grievance redressal mechanisms for Indian seafarers
      • Layer 1 - the Convention's own route, which every Indian seafarer has: on board → master → flag State → authorized officer in port → Director-General of the ILO
      • Layer 2 - India's capacity. Flag State for Indian ships; but for most Indian seafarers, who serve on foreign flags, the operative head is LABOUR-SUPPLYING responsibility, Regulation 5.3, plus Regulation 1.4 licensing of recruitment and placement services and the no-fees rule
      • Layer 3 - the domestic frame. Merchant Shipping Act, 1958; the shipping master for engagement, discharge and wage disputes; the Directorate General of Shipping as the maritime administration
  6. closing
    1. 6Closing - the thread that joins limbs b) and d)
      • Flag State certifies and port State verifies - and both can be satisfied while a seafarer sits unpaid on a certified ship
      • What closes that gap is the complaint - which is why a complaint is also one of the four triggers
      • Limb d) is the same thread followed to its Indian end: regulate the agent who is within reach, because the foreign owner is not
