Memory cue. FOUR LIMBS, FOUR MARKS EACH - COMPRESS LIMB a), IT IS A WHOLE QUESTION ELSEWHERE. EDITION: MLC 2006 in force 20 Aug 2013, as amended through the 2018 set. 2022 amendments = ADOPTED BUT NOT IN FORCE (23 Dec 2024, +22 months). Structure: Articles · Regulations · Part A mandatory · Part B guidance; Title 5 = flag / port / labour-supplying. a) FLAG: effective inspection and certification system; may authorise ROs but stays fully responsible; MLC + DMLC at 500 GT international, 5 yrs, intermediate between 2nd and 3rd anniversary, interim 6 months; DMLC Part I = authority, Part II = SHIPOWNER; inspection interval never more than 3 years; power to prohibit sailing. PORT: certificate + DMLC = PRIMA FACIE EVIDENCE, so ordinary inspection = document check; limited to Articles, Regulations, Part A only; Art V(7) no more favourable treatment. b) COMPLAINT (paper prints 'Compliant'): ON-BOARD reg 5.1.5 - fair, effective, expeditious; lowest level first but direct access to master and to external authorities; copy to every seafarer + contacts + impartial adviser on board; VICTIMIZATION PROHIBITED AND PENALIZED. ONSHORE reg 5.2.2 - authorized officer in port; try on board first → notify flag State for a corrective plan → still unresolved → Director-General of the ILO; source kept confidential. c) DETAINABLE: the Convention has NO list - it has TWO GROUNDS: (1) conditions clearly hazardous to safety, health or security; (2) serious or repeated breach incl. seafarers' rights. Reached via a more detailed inspection on four triggers: bad/missing documents · clear grounds · flag changed to avoid compliance · a complaint. Then rectify or accepted plan of action, flag State notified forthwith, compensation for undue detention. d) INDIA - THREE LAYERS: (1) the Convention's own route above; (2) India is usually NOT the flag State - most Indian seafarers are on foreign flags, so the head is reg 5.3 LABOUR-SUPPLYING + reg 1.4 licensing of recruitment and placement services and the NO-FEES rule; (3) MS Act 1958, the shipping master, the Directorate General of Shipping. Unions and ITF = practical, not law. NO circular, NO named office, NO named forum, NO section number. Administration is DG Shipping - DGMA is 37 months future.

Remember6 route headings
Cover30 core points beneath them

Memorise the 6 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 30 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. Which edition applies, and how the Convention is built
  • ILO instrument, in force 20 August 2013 - the 'fourth pillar' alongside SOLAS, MARPOL and STCW
  • Articles · Regulations · Code Part A Standards (mandatory) · Code Part B Guidelines (guidance)
  • Five Titles; Title 5 is compliance and enforcement and splits into flag State, port State and labour-supplying responsibilities
  • At this sitting: as amended through the 2018 set. The 2022 amendments are ADOPTED BUT NOT IN FORCE - 23 December 2024, twenty-two months away
2. Limb a) - Flag State and Port State responsibilities
  • FLAG: an effective system of inspection and certification; may authorise recognized organizations but remains fully responsible
  • Maritime Labour Certificate + DMLC at 500 GT on international voyages; 5 years, intermediate inspection between the 2nd and 3rd anniversaries; interim 6 months
  • DMLC Part I by the competent authority, Part II by the SHIPOWNER - the classic trap
  • Inspection interval shall in no case exceed three years; inspectors may prohibit sailing
  • PORT: any foreign ship may be inspected; the certificate and DMLC must be accepted as prima facie evidence, so the ordinary inspection is a document review
  • Inspection limited to the Articles, Regulations and Part A only of the Code
  • Article V(7) no more favourable treatment - a non-ratifying flag gains no advantage
3. Limb b) - the on-board and onshore COMPLAINT procedures
  • ON-BOARD - Regulation 5.1.5. Fair, effective and expeditious; resolution at the lowest level, but direct access to the master and to external authorities preserved
  • A copy of the procedure to every seafarer, with contacts and the name of an impartial adviser on board
  • Victimization is PROHIBITED AND PENALIZED - the provision that makes the rest usable
  • ONSHORE - Regulation 5.2.2. Complaint to an authorized officer in port; seek resolution on board first
  • Unresolved → notify the flag State for advice and a corrective plan; still unresolved → report to the Director-General of the ILO
  • Confidentiality of the source is safeguarded throughout
4. Limb c) - detainable deficiencies
  • The Convention publishes no list. It gives TWO GROUNDS
  • (1) Conditions clearly hazardous to the safety, health or security of seafarers
  • (2) A serious or repeated breach of the requirements, including seafarers' rights
  • Reached only through a more detailed inspection, opened on one of four triggers: documents missing or invalid; clear grounds; flag changed to avoid compliance; or a complaint
  • Then: rectification, or an accepted plan of action; the flag State is notified forthwith; compensation for undue detention
5. Limb d) - grievance redressal mechanisms for Indian seafarers
  • Layer 1 - the Convention's own route, which every Indian seafarer has: on board → master → flag State → authorized officer in port → Director-General of the ILO
  • Layer 2 - India's capacity. Flag State for Indian ships; but for most Indian seafarers, who serve on foreign flags, the operative head is LABOUR-SUPPLYING responsibility, Regulation 5.3, plus Regulation 1.4 licensing of recruitment and placement services and the no-fees rule
  • Layer 3 - the domestic frame. Merchant Shipping Act, 1958; the shipping master for engagement, discharge and wage disputes; the Directorate General of Shipping as the maritime administration
  • Practical actors, not law: seafarers' unions and the ITF inspectorate
  • NOT ASSERTED: no circular number, no named office or cell, no named forum or tribunal, no section number
6. Closing - the thread that joins limbs b) and d)
  • Flag State certifies and port State verifies - and both can be satisfied while a seafarer sits unpaid on a certified ship
  • What closes that gap is the complaint - which is why a complaint is also one of the four triggers
  • Limb d) is the same thread followed to its Indian end: regulate the agent who is within reach, because the foreign owner is not
Model written answer

1. Which edition applies, and how the Convention is built

Nothing in this question can be asserted safely until the edition is fixed, so it is fixed first and briefly.

The Maritime Labour Convention, 2006 is an International Labour Organization instrument, not an IMO one, adopted at the International Labour Conference in 2006 and in force since 20 August 2013. It is commonly called the fourth pillar of international maritime regulation, alongside SOLAS, MARPOL and STCW.

  • It is built in three levels: the Articles, the Regulations, and the Code, which is in two parts - Part A Standards, which are mandatory, and Part B Guidelines, which are guidance.
  • It has five Titles: minimum requirements; conditions of employment; accommodation, recreation, food and catering; health protection, medical care, welfare and social security; and Title 5, compliance and enforcement, which is where the whole of this question lives.
  • Title 5 divides into three, and the third is the one candidates forget: flag State responsibilities, port State responsibilities, and labour-supplying responsibilities. Limb d) of this question turns on the third.
  • The Code is amended not by IMO tacit acceptance but by the Special Tripartite Committee under Article XIII.
  • One structural rule is worth stating because it stiffens the whole Title: the substantial equivalence permitted by Article VI, by which a Member may implement Part A by substantially equivalent measures, does not apply to Part A of the Code within Title 5. The compliance machinery itself must be implemented as written.

The edition operative at this sitting is MLC 2006 as amended through the 2018 amendments. The 2014 set, which introduced the abandonment and financial security provisions, the 2016 set on harassment and bullying, and the 2018 set on wages while held captive are all in force. The amendments approved by the International Labour Conference at its 110th Session in 2022 are ADOPTED BUT NOT IN FORCE: they enter into force on 23 December 2024, twenty-two months after this paper, and nothing from that set may be presented as an obligation here.

2. Limb a) - Flag State and Port State responsibilities

This limb is printed as one, and it is worth about four marks - but it is a complete sixteen-mark question elsewhere. It must therefore be compressed to its structure. The single sentence that organises it: the flag State builds and certifies the system; the port State checks the certificate, and looks behind it only when it has a reason to.

2.1 Flag State responsibilities

ProvisionThe obligation
Reg 5.1.1 general principlesResponsible for implementation on ships that fly its flag, and for maintaining an effective system of inspection and certification so that conditions meet and continue to meet the standards. A copy of the Convention is kept on board
Reg 5.1.2 recognized organizationsIt may authorise ROs to inspect and certify on its behalf, having satisfied itself of their competence and independence - but it remains fully responsible. The current list is provided to the ILO and made public
Reg 5.1.3 certificationA Maritime Labour Certificate and a Declaration of Maritime Labour Compliance for ships of 500 gross tonnage and over on international voyages or operating from or between foreign ports; others on the shipowner's request. Valid for five years, with an intermediate inspection between the second and third anniversary dates; an interim certificate runs for six months
The DMLCPart I is drawn up by the competent authority and lists the national requirements; Part II is drawn up by the SHIPOWNER and sets out the measures adopted to ensure ongoing compliance. This split is the classic examination trap
Reg 5.1.4 inspection and enforcementRegular inspections at an interval that shall in no case exceed three years, by qualified and independent inspectors with power to board, examine, require rectification and prohibit the ship from sailing. Rights of appeal, confidentiality of the complaint source, no conflict of interest, reports to the authority and the master and posted on board, an annual report, and adequate penalties
Reg 5.1.6 casualtiesAn official inquiry into any serious marine casualty causing injury or loss of life

2.2 Port State responsibilities

The port State's role is different in kind. It is not certifying anything; it is verifying, on a foreign ship, and its powers are deliberately bounded:

  1. Any foreign ship calling at a port of a Member may be inspected, on the basis provided by Article V(4).
  2. The Maritime Labour Certificate and the DMLC must be ACCEPTED as prima facie evidence of compliance. The ordinary inspection is therefore a review of those documents and nothing more. This is what makes the system workable at the scale of world shipping.
  3. Any inspection is limited to the Articles, the Regulations and Part A only of the Code. Part B, being guidance, is not an enforcement standard.
  4. A more detailed inspection may be carried out on any of four triggers: the required documents are not produced or are not maintained or are falsely maintained; there are clear grounds for believing conditions do not conform; there are grounds to believe the ship has changed flag to avoid compliance; or there is a complaint. It is mandatory where conditions could be clearly hazardous or where there is a serious or repeated breach.
  5. Article V(7) - no more favourable treatment. Ships of States that have not ratified receive no advantage from that fact. Without this the Convention could be escaped by choosing a flag, and with it the port State's inspection reaches every ship.

The third leg of Title 5, labour-supplying responsibilities under Regulation 5.3, should be named here in a sentence even though the printed limb does not ask for it, because limb d) depends on it.

3. Limb b) - the on-board and onshore COMPLAINT procedures

A necessary correction before the limb can be answered. The paper prints 'On-board & On-shore Compliant Procedures'. The word is COMPLAINT. There is no instrument, procedure or defined term in MLC 2006 called a compliant procedure; what the Convention provides is an on-board complaint procedure and an onshore seafarer complaint-handling procedure, and the printed pairing of 'on-board' with 'on-shore' maps onto that pair and onto nothing else. The limb is answered accordingly, and this is noted once and not laboured.

These are two distinct procedures with different owners, different audiences and different escalation routes. Treating them as one is the commonest error on this limb.

3.1 The on-board complaint procedure - Regulation 5.1.5

Every ship must have procedures for the fair, effective and expeditious handling of seafarer complaints alleging breaches of the Convention, including seafarers' rights.

  • The complaint should be resolved at the lowest level possible. That is the design intention - most matters are best settled by the head of department or the master before they become anything else.
  • But the seafarer's right to complain directly to the master is preserved, and so is his right to complain directly to the appropriate external authorities - the flag State administration or an authority in the port - without first exhausting the on-board route. The lowest-level principle is a preference, not a precondition, and stating that distinction correctly is where the mark is.
  • Every seafarer is given a copy of the on-board complaint procedures applicable on the ship, in a language he understands, together with the contact information for the competent authority in the flag State and in his country of residence, and the name of a person on board who can advise him impartially on the procedure.
  • The seafarer may be accompanied or represented during the procedure.

The provision that makes all of it usable: victimization is prohibited and penalized. Any form of victimization of a seafarer for having filed a complaint is forbidden and must be penalized under national law. Without that protection the procedure would be worthless, because the person best placed to complain is also the person most easily dismissed at the next port, and an answer that omits it has missed the point of the mechanism.

3.2 The onshore complaint-handling procedure - Regulation 5.2.2

This is the port State's procedure, and it exists so that a seafarer on a foreign ship in a foreign port has somewhere to go that is not his own employer.

  1. A seafarer - or a representative, or an organisation acting for him - may make a complaint to an authorized officer in the port at which the ship has called, alleging a breach of the Convention.
  2. The officer undertakes an initial investigation to establish whether the complaint is well-founded.
  3. Resolution is sought at ship level first. Where appropriate the officer promotes settlement on board, which is a deliberate mirror of the on-board principle.
  4. If it cannot be resolved on board, the flag State is notified and asked for advice and a corrective plan of action, with a deadline.
  5. If it still cannot be resolved, a copy of the report is sent to the Director-General of the ILO, and the appropriate seafarers' and shipowners' organisations in the port State are informed.
  6. The confidentiality of the source of the complaint is safeguarded throughout, and steps are taken to avoid the seafarer being identified. This mirrors the anti-victimization rule on the on-board side.

The connection worth drawing, because it is the reason both exist: a complaint is also one of the four triggers for a more detailed port State inspection. So the complaint procedure is not only a remedy for the individual seafarer; it is one of the principal ways the enforcement machinery of limb c) is set in motion at all.

4. Limb c) - detainable deficiencies

An important preliminary: the Convention publishes no list of detainable deficiencies. Lists of that kind circulate, and they are useful, but they belong to regional port State control regimes and to industry guidance rather than to the Convention. What the Convention provides is TWO GROUNDS for detention, and an answer built on the two grounds is both correct and more durable than one built on a recited list.

4.1 How the ship gets there

Detention is the end of a sequence, not a first step. The ordinary port State inspection is the document review described at section 2.2. It becomes a more detailed inspection only on one of the four triggers - documents not produced, not maintained or falsely maintained; clear grounds for believing conditions do not conform; grounds to believe the flag was changed to avoid compliance; or a complaint. The scope of that more detailed inspection is drawn from the Convention's own appendix listing the areas to be examined, which are essentially the subject matter of Titles 1 to 4.

4.2 The two grounds

GroundWhat it means
1. Conditions on board are clearly hazardous to the safety, health or security of seafarersThe defect creates a real and present danger to the people on board - as distinct from a paperwork failure or a breach that is serious but not dangerous
2. There is a serious or repeated breach of the requirements of the Convention, including seafarers' rightsThe breach need not endanger anyone. Its gravity, or its repetition, is what justifies detention - and 'including seafarers' rights' makes clear that a purely contractual or financial wrong can detain a ship

Those two grounds are the answer to this limb. The second is the one most often missed, and it is the more remarkable of the two: this is a Convention under which a ship may be detained because seafarers have not been paid, with nothing whatever wrong with the ship.

4.3 What in practice falls under them - given as illustration, not as a list from the Convention

  • Documentary: no valid Maritime Labour Certificate or DMLC, or a certificate that no longer reflects the conditions found.
  • Employment: seafarers serving without a signed seafarers' employment agreement, or on terms they had no opportunity to examine.
  • Wages: substantial wages long unpaid - which also touches the abandonment provisions.
  • Age and fitness: a seafarer below the minimum age, or serving without a valid medical certificate.
  • Hours: serious or systematic breach of the hours of work and rest limits, particularly where the records appear to have been manufactured.
  • Food and water: insufficient or unfit food, or insufficient drinking water.
  • Accommodation and health: grossly unsanitary or unsafe accommodation, or an absence of the required medical care.
  • Repatriation and abandonment: failure to repatriate, or the absence of the financial security certificate that the 2014 amendments require - and here the ship may be detained precisely because the seafarers cannot get home.

These are illustrations of the two grounds and are identified as such, because presenting them as the Convention's own list would be wrong.

4.4 What follows a detention

  • The ship is detained until the deficiencies are rectified, or until the officer has accepted a plan of action to rectify them and is satisfied it will be carried out promptly.
  • The flag State is notified forthwith, and is invited to be present. The seafarers' and shipowners' organisations in the port State may be informed.
  • Compensation is payable for any loss or damage suffered as a result of a ship being unduly detained or delayed. The power is real and so is the check on it; an answer that gives the power without the check has given half of it.
  • Every effort is made to avoid the ship being unduly detained or delayed in the first place - the object is rectification, not punishment.

5. Limb d) - grievance redressal mechanisms for Indian seafarers

This limb leaves the Convention and asks about India, and it should be answered in three layers, in this order. What is deliberately not done here is as important as what is: no circular, no named office or cell, and no named forum or tribunal is asserted, because no held source supports one. The limb is answered to architecture.

5.1 Layer one - the route the Convention itself gives every Indian seafarer

The first and most practically useful answer is that an Indian seafarer already has the escalation described at section 3, and it is available to him wherever he is and whatever flag he serves under:

  1. The on-board complaint procedure, with the right to go directly to the master, the right to an impartial adviser on board, and protection against victimization.
  2. Direct complaint to the flag State administration, without exhausting the on-board route.
  3. Complaint to an authorized officer in the port of call, wherever in the world that is, under the onshore procedure - with the source kept confidential.
  4. Escalation by that officer to the flag State for a corrective plan of action, and ultimately to the Director-General of the ILO.
  5. And where the complaint is about repatriation, unpaid wages or abandonment, direct access to the financial security provider under the 2014 amendments - a claim that does not go through the shipowner, who is by definition the party who has failed.

5.2 Layer two - India's capacity, which is usually not the one candidates assume

This is the structural insight that makes the limb answerable, and it is Convention architecture rather than an assertion about India.

India's capacityWhen it appliesWhat it engages
Flag StateIndian-flagged shipsThe whole of the flag State machinery at section 2.1 - certification, inspection, the power to prohibit sailing, and the obligation to respond to an onshore complaint referred by a foreign port State
Port StateAny foreign ship in an Indian portThe onshore complaint-handling procedure and the inspection and detention powers at sections 3.2 and 4
Labour-supplying StateThe great majority of Indian seafarers, who serve on foreign-flag shipsRegulation 5.3 labour-supplying responsibilities, and Regulation 1.4 - the licensing and regulation of recruitment and placement services operating in the territory, the requirement that they keep registers and verify employment agreements, the prohibition on charging fees to the seafarer, and the requirement that they operate a system of protection to compensate the seafarer for monetary loss arising from the service's failure to meet its obligations

The point to make explicitly: for most Indian seafarers the flag State is not India, so the operative Indian head is the labour-supplying one. It is also the most useful in practice, because the recruitment and placement service is in India, is licensed in India, and can be reached in India - which the foreign shipowner very often cannot. The regulation of the agent is the enforceable end of the chain.

5.3 Layer three - the Indian statutory and institutional architecture

  • The governing statute at this sitting is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958. It is the vehicle through which India's obligations as flag State, port State and labour-supplying State are given domestic effect.
  • The maritime administration is the Directorate General of Shipping, which acts as the competent authority for the purposes of the Convention - drawing up DMLC Part I, authorising recognized organizations, appointing inspectors and authorized officers, and licensing recruitment and placement services.
  • The shipping master is the statutory officer through whom seafarers are engaged and discharged and before whom wage and discharge disputes have historically been brought. That office is the traditional first domestic point of redress for a seafarer's claim against a ship.
  • The ordinary courts remain available for a contractual claim for wages, and a maritime claim may found an action in rem against the ship in the admiralty jurisdiction of the High Courts - which is often the only effective remedy against a foreign owner, because it proceeds against an asset within the jurisdiction rather than against a person outside it.
  • Practical actors that are not law but matter enormously: the seafarers' unions, and the inspectorate of the International Transport Workers' Federation, which boards ships, pursues wage claims and arranges food and repatriation. They are named as industry mechanisms and not as legal machinery.

What is expressly not asserted. No administrative circular is cited by number; no grievance cell, committee or office is named; no tribunal or forum is named; and no section of the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 is cited by number. No held source supports any of them for this date, and inventing one would be worse than the gap. And the Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 does not exist at this sitting - it commenced on 15 March 2026, thirty-seven months later - so the administration is the Directorate General of Shipping and not the renamed body that followed it.

6. Closing - the thread that joins limbs b) and d)

The four limbs look like four separate topics and they are not. The flag State certifies, the port State verifies, and both can be entirely satisfied while a seafarer sits unpaid on a certified ship. What closes that gap is the complaint - which is why the Convention gives the seafarer two procedures, protects him from victimization for using them, keeps his identity confidential, and makes a complaint one of the four triggers that opens a detailed inspection and can end in detention.

Limb d) is the same thread followed to its Indian end. An Indian seafarer's practical difficulty is not the absence of a rule but the distance to the person who owes him the money. The Convention's answer is to give him a route that runs through whichever port he is in rather than through the owner's country; India's answer is to regulate, licence and hold responsible the agent who recruited him, because that is the link in the chain that is physically within reach.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks across four printed limbs of very unequal natural size, and the structure decides the mark more than the content does. Limb a) is a complete sixteen-mark question elsewhere in this corpus; here it is worth about four. Limbs c) and d) are smaller subjects than their allocation and will be hurried if the candidate follows instinct.

So the discipline is: compress limb a) deliberately, and protect the time for c) and d). The commonest way to fail this question is to write a superb account of Title 5 that consumes the paper and leaves three limbs unanswered. Four adequate limbs beat one excellent one by a wide margin.

First: the misprint, because the limb is unanswerable without resolving it

The paper prints 'On-board & On-shore Compliant Procedures'. It means COMPLAINT. There is no such thing in MLC 2006 as a 'compliant procedure'; the Convention has an on-board complaint procedure (Regulation 5.1.5) and an onshore seafarer complaint-handling procedure (Regulation 5.2.2), and the printed pairing of on-board with on-shore matches that pair exactly.

Say so in one clause - 'the printed word appears to be a misprint for complaint procedures, which are dealt with at Regulations 5.1.5 and 5.2.2' - and then answer the Convention. Do not spend a paragraph on it and do not silently ignore it.

Second: manage the time, because the limb sizes are wildly uneven

Limb a) - flag State and port State responsibilities - is a complete sixteen-mark question in other papers of this corpus. Here it is one of four limbs and worth about four. The single commonest way to fail this question is to write a superb limb a) and leave b), c) and d) unwritten.

Compress limb a) to its skeleton and spend the time you save on limbs c) and d), whose natural size is smaller than their allocation. Your instinct will be exactly the wrong way round.

Fix the edition in one sentence and get a mark for it

MLC 2006, in force 20 August 2013, as amended through the 2018 amendments. The 2014 (abandonment and financial security), 2016 (harassment and bullying) and 2018 (wages while held captive) sets are all in force.

The 2022 amendments are adopted but NOT in force - they come into force on 23 December 2024, nearly two years after this paper. Do not write in appropriately sized PPE, free drinking water as an amendment obligation, social connectivity and internet access, or repatriation of the remains of a deceased seafarer. Consolidated texts carrying the 2022 set are in circulation and read as current; using one is the specific way this question is failed.

Limb a) - the skeleton that carries the marks

Flag State: build and certify. An effective system of inspection and certification; recognized organizations may be authorised but the flag State remains fully responsible; the Maritime Labour Certificate and DMLC at 500 GT on international voyages, five years, intermediate inspection between the second and third anniversaries; DMLC Part I by the competent authority, Part II by the shipowner - get this the right way round, it is the classic trap; inspection interval never more than three years; inspectors may prohibit sailing.

Port State: check, and look behind only with a reason. The certificate and DMLC must be accepted as prima facie evidence, so the ordinary inspection is a document review; inspection is limited to the Articles, Regulations and Part A only; a more detailed inspection follows on four triggers - bad or missing documents, clear grounds, flag changed to avoid compliance, or a complaint; and Article V(7) no more favourable treatment stops a ship escaping by choosing a flag.

Name the third leg - Regulation 5.3 labour-supplying responsibilities - in one sentence. It is not asked in limb a), but limb d) depends on it and naming it early makes the last limb look planned.

Limb b) - two procedures, not one, and one provision that carries the mark

On board (5.1.5): fair, effective and expeditious; resolution at the lowest level is the preference, but the right of direct access to the master and to external authorities is preserved and is not conditional on exhausting the on-board route. Every seafarer gets a copy of the procedure with contacts and the name of an impartial adviser on board, and may be accompanied or represented.

Then the sentence that must not be omitted: victimization for filing a complaint is prohibited and must be penalized. Without it the procedure is worthless, because the person best placed to complain is the person most easily dismissed at the next port.

Onshore (5.2.2): complaint to an authorized officer in the port; initial investigation; resolution sought on board first; if unresolved, the flag State is notified and asked for a corrective plan of action; if still unresolved, a report goes to the Director-General of the ILO; and the source is kept confidential throughout.

Then make the link that shows the system understood: a complaint is one of the four triggers for a more detailed inspection, so the complaint procedure is not just a remedy - it is how the enforcement machinery of limb c) starts.

Limb c) - resist the urge to recite a list

The Convention publishes no list of detainable deficiencies. It gives two grounds: conditions clearly hazardous to the safety, health or security of seafarers; or a serious or repeated breach of the requirements, including seafarers' rights.

The second ground is the one worth dwelling on. It does not require danger. A ship may be detained under this Convention because seafarers have not been paid, with nothing at all wrong with the ship - and that is genuinely unusual in maritime enforcement. Say it.

Then give examples - unpaid wages, no valid certificate or DMLC, no employment agreements, under-age or medically uncertificated seafarers, manufactured rest-hour records, insufficient food or drinking water, unsanitary accommodation, failure to repatriate, no financial security certificate - but introduce them as illustrations of the two grounds, not as the Convention's list.

Finish the limb properly: detention runs until rectification or an accepted plan of action; the flag State is notified forthwith; and compensation is payable for undue detention or delay. Giving the power without the check gives half the answer.

Limb d) - the insight that makes it answerable

Answer in three layers. First, the Convention's own route, which every Indian seafarer already has - on board, direct to the master, direct to the flag State, to an authorized officer in any port, up to the Director-General of the ILO, and direct to the financial security provider in an abandonment.

Second, and this is the point that lifts the limb: for most Indian seafarers India is NOT the flag State. They serve on foreign-flag ships. So India's operative head is labour-supplying responsibility under Regulation 5.3, together with Regulation 1.4 - the licensing and regulation of recruitment and placement services, the requirement that they verify employment agreements, the prohibition on charging fees to the seafarer, and the required system of protection to compensate monetary loss. This is also the practically useful answer: the agent is in India and can be reached; the foreign owner usually cannot.

Third, the domestic architecture: the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958; the Directorate General of Shipping as the maritime administration and competent authority; the shipping master as the statutory officer for engagement, discharge and wage disputes; the ordinary courts, and the admiralty jurisdiction of the High Courts for an action in rem against the ship, which is often the only effective remedy against a foreign owner. Then the practical actors that are not law - the seafarers' unions and the ITF inspectorate.

Do not invent Indian detail. No circular number, no named grievance cell, no named tribunal or forum, no section number of the 1958 Act. An architecture stated accurately is worth far more than a citation that is wrong, and the examiner will know.

Get the Indian statute and the administration right for THIS sitting

The Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 governs. The Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 commenced on 15 March 2026 - thirty-seven months after this paper - and the maritime administration was renamed at that point. At this sitting the administration is the Directorate General of Shipping. Material written for a 2026 sitting uses the new name and the new Act; both are wrong here.

Common mistakes

  • Do not write the 2022 amendments in as obligations. They are adopted but not in force at this sitting.
  • Do not give a ratification count or a percentage of world tonnage. The figures in circulation are far later than this sitting and cannot be carried back.
  • Do not give any inspection, detention or deficiency statistic, and do not name a regional port State control memorandum of understanding.
  • Do not reproduce any provision of the Convention word for word. Cite the Regulation or Standard and state its effect.
  • Do not reverse DMLC Parts I and II.
  • Do not present a detainable-deficiency list as though the Convention published it.
  • Do not invent an Indian circular, cell, committee, forum or section number.
  • Do not cite the Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 or the renamed administration.
  • Do not cite case authority. None is held.
  • Above all, do not spend half the paper on limb a).

Examiner traps

  • "The paper says Compliant Procedures. What is being asked?" COMPLAINT procedures. MLC 2006 contains no such thing as a compliant procedure - it has an on-board complaint procedure (5.1.5) and an onshore complaint-handling procedure (5.2.2).
  • "Which edition applies?" As amended through the 2018 set. The 2022 amendments are adopted but not in force - 23 December 2024, twenty-two months away.
  • "Who draws up DMLC Part II?" The shipowner. Part I is the competent authority's. Reversing them is the classic error.
  • "Must a seafarer exhaust the on-board procedure first?" No. Lowest-level resolution is the design preference; direct access to the master and to external authorities is expressly preserved.
  • "On what grounds may a ship be detained?" Two - conditions clearly hazardous to safety, health or security, or a serious or repeated breach including seafarers' rights. The Convention publishes no list.
  • "Can a ship be detained with nothing wrong with the ship?" Yes - unpaid wages will do it, under the second ground.
  • "What protects the seafarer who complains?" Victimization is prohibited and must be penalized, and the source of an onshore complaint is kept confidential.
  • "In what capacity does India engage for most Indian seafarers?" As a labour-supplying State under Regulation 5.3, not as the flag State - most serve under foreign flags.
  • "What is the Indian maritime administration at this sitting?" The Directorate General of Shipping. The rename followed the Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 in 2026, thirty-seven months later.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Where is the on-board complaint procedure kept on your ship, and who is the impartial adviser named in it?
  • A rating tells you he has not been paid for two months. What do you do, in order?
  • Your ship is in a foreign port and an inspector asks for the DMLC. Which part do you produce, and who wrote it?
  • What would you expect a port State inspector to look at first under the MLC?
  • What is the financial security certificate on your notice board for?
  • An Indian seafarer on a foreign-flag ship has a wage claim against an agent in India. Where can he go?

Memory framework

Edition first, then the four printed limbs. EDITION (1): through the 2018 set; 2022 amendments adopted but NOT in force; Articles · Regulations · Part A mandatory · Part B guidance; Title 5 = flag / port / labour-supplying. LIMB a) (2): flag builds and certifies - DMLC I authority, II shipowner, five years, never more than three; port checks - prima facie evidence, Part A only, four triggers. LIMB b) (3): on-board 5.1.5 - lowest level but direct access preserved, victimization prohibited; onshore 5.2.2 - port officer → flag State → Director-General of the ILO. LIMB c) (4): two grounds, not a list - clearly hazardous, or serious or repeated breach. LIMB d) (5): Convention route · labour-supplying, reg 5.3 and 1.4 · MS Act 1958, shipping master, DG Shipping. CLOSE (6): the complaint is what closes the gap.

Uncertainty, the wrong-edition hazard, and the Indian evidence gap

Temporal risk is HIGH here for a physical reason rather than a theoretical one. The corpus holds two consolidated texts of this Convention in the same folder - one through the 2018 amendments and one carrying the amendments approved in 2022. The second is present, readable and inapplicable at this sitting, and that is precisely the circumstance in which a wrong-edition answer is written without anyone noticing. The operative text is MLC 2006 as amended through the 2018 set, and the twenty-two month margin to 23 December 2024 makes the exclusion robust.

Limb d) has no donor anywhere in the corpus and no held Indian source, and the answer says so. No administrative circular is cited by number, no grievance cell, committee or office is named, no tribunal or forum is named, and no section of the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 is cited by number. The limb is answered to statutory and institutional architecture, and the organising insight - that for most Indian seafarers India is not the flag State but the labour-supplying State - is Convention architecture rather than a claim about India.

No ratification count and no tonnage percentage is asserted. The corpus figure is dated thirty-one months after this sitting; it is a live example of a fact that is true for the present and would be a false assertion here. No inspection, detention or deficiency statistic is given, and no regional port State control memorandum is named, so the practical examples in limb c) are expressly identified as illustrations of the two grounds rather than as any published list.

No provision of the Convention is reproduced verbatim - Regulations and Standards are cited by number and their effect stated - and no case authority is cited, because none is held. The Title 5 material is INTERNAL REUSE VERIFIED rather than P1: the Convention text was not re-read at source this session.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 6 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________framing Which edition applies, and how the Convention is built
  2. __________a) Limb a) - Flag State and Port State responsibilities
  3. __________b) Limb b) - the on-board and onshore COMPLAINT procedures
  4. __________c) Limb c) - detainable deficiencies
  5. __________d) Limb d) - grievance redressal mechanisms for Indian seafarers
  6. __________closing Closing - the thread that joins limbs b) and d)
Flashcards 17

0 fix the edition. 1 limb a) compressed - flag State builds and certifies, port State checks and may detain. 2 limb b) resolve the misprint, then the on-board procedure and the onshore procedure as two distinct mechanisms. 3 limb c) the two detention grounds, with illustrations identified as illustrations. 4 limb d) the Convention's route, India's capacity, the domestic architecture.

Four limbs of very unequal natural size; the failure mode is a superb limb a) and three unwritten ones.

COMPLAINT procedures. MLC 2006 has no concept called a compliant procedure. It provides an on-board complaint procedure at Regulation 5.1.5 and an onshore seafarer complaint-handling procedure at Regulation 5.2.2, and the printed on-board / on-shore pairing maps onto that pair exactly.

The single most consequential misprint on this paper; answering the printed word answers nothing.

MLC 2006 as amended through the 2018 amendments. The 2014, 2016 and 2018 sets are in force. The 2022 amendments are ADOPTED BUT NOT IN FORCE - they enter into force on 23 December 2024, twenty-two months after this sitting.

A consolidated text carrying the 2022 set is in circulation and reads as current; using it is how this question is failed.

Articles, Regulations, and the Code in two parts - Part A Standards mandatory, Part B Guidelines guidance. Five Titles. Title 5 divides into flag State, port State and LABOUR-SUPPLYING responsibilities. Substantial equivalence under Article VI does not apply to Part A within Title 5.

The third leg is the one candidates forget, and limb d) depends on it.

Part I is drawn up by the COMPETENT AUTHORITY and lists the national requirements. Part II is drawn up by the SHIPOWNER and sets out the measures adopted to ensure ongoing compliance.

The classic examination trap on this Convention, and it is routinely reversed.

Certificate and DMLC for ships of 500 GT and over on international voyages or operating from or between foreign ports. Valid five years, with an intermediate inspection between the second and third anniversary dates; interim certificate six months. Inspection interval shall in no case exceed three years.

Precise numbers are cheap marks and are the part most often approximated.

Because the Maritime Labour Certificate and the DMLC must be ACCEPTED as prima facie evidence of compliance. Inspection is limited to the Articles, the Regulations and Part A only of the Code, and goes further only on one of the four triggers.

It is what makes the scheme workable across world shipping, and it explains why the triggers matter.

Required documents not produced, not maintained or falsely maintained; clear grounds for believing conditions do not conform; grounds to believe the ship changed flag to avoid compliance; or a complaint. It is mandatory where conditions could be clearly hazardous or where there is a serious or repeated breach.

They connect limb b) to limb c) - a complaint is one of them.

The prohibition on victimization. Any victimization of a seafarer for having filed a complaint is forbidden and must be penalized under national law. Without it the procedure is worthless, because the person best placed to complain is the one most easily dismissed at the next port.

Omitting it misses the entire point of the mechanism.

No. Resolution at the lowest level is the design PREFERENCE, but the right to complain directly to the master, and directly to the appropriate external authorities including the flag State and an authority in the port, is expressly preserved.

Stating it as a precondition rather than a preference is the error on this limb.

Complaint to an authorized officer in the port of call; initial investigation; resolution sought on board first; if unresolved, the flag State is notified and asked for advice and a corrective plan of action; if still unresolved, a report goes to the Director-General of the ILO. The source of the complaint is kept confidential throughout.

A five-step escalation with a named endpoint is worth far more than 'he can complain ashore'.

Two. Conditions on board are clearly hazardous to the safety, health or security of seafarers; OR there is a serious or repeated breach of the requirements, including seafarers' rights. The Convention publishes no list of detainable deficiencies - the lists in circulation are regional PSC and industry material.

An answer built on the two grounds is correct and durable; a recited list is neither.

It requires no danger. A ship may be detained under this Convention because seafarers have not been paid, with nothing at all wrong with the ship. Gravity, or repetition, is what justifies detention, and 'including seafarers' rights' makes the purely contractual wrong sufficient.

It is unusual in maritime enforcement and it shows the Convention's subject is the worker, not the ship.

Detention until the deficiencies are rectified or an accepted plan of action is in place; the flag State notified forthwith and invited to be present; seafarers' and shipowners' organisations informed; and compensation payable for any loss or damage from undue detention or delay.

Giving the power without the check on it gives half the answer.

As a LABOUR-SUPPLYING State, not as the flag State - most Indian seafarers serve on foreign-flag ships. That engages Regulation 5.3 labour-supplying responsibilities and Regulation 1.4 on recruitment and placement services: licensing, verification of employment agreements, the prohibition on charging fees to the seafarer, and a system of protection to compensate monetary loss.

The structural insight that makes limb d) answerable without inventing Indian sources, and the practically useful one - the agent is reachable, the foreign owner is not.

The Merchant Shipping Act, 1958; the Directorate General of Shipping as maritime administration and competent authority; the shipping master as the statutory officer for engagement, discharge and wage disputes; the ordinary courts, and the admiralty jurisdiction of the High Courts for an action in rem against the ship. Unions and the ITF inspectorate are practical actors, not law.

An accurate architecture beats an invented citation, and the in rem route is often the only effective remedy against a foreign owner.

The Merchant Shipping Act, 1958, and the Directorate General of Shipping. The Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 commenced 15 March 2026 - thirty-seven months later - and the administration was renamed at that point. Neither the 2025 Act nor the renamed body may appear.

The standing statute trap for the whole 2023 batch, and the corpus register records the later position.

Quick revision
15-second recall
FOUR LIMBS ≈ 4 MARKS EACH. COMPRESS LIMB a) - IT IS A WHOLE 16-MARK QUESTION ELSEWHERE. PRINTED 'Compliant' = COMPLAINT. EDITION: MLC 2006, ILO, in force 20 Aug 2013, as amended through the 2018 set; 2014/2016/2018 in force; 2022 amendments ADOPTED BUT NOT IN FORCE - 23 Dec 2024, +22 months. Build: Articles · Regulations · Part A Standards MANDATORY · Part B Guidelines guidance; five Titles; Title 5 = flag / port / LABOUR-SUPPLYING; substantial equivalence does NOT apply to Part A within Title 5. a) FLAG (build + certify): effective inspection and certification system (5.1.1); ROs may be authorised but flag remains FULLY responsible (5.1.2); Maritime Labour Certificate + DMLC at 500 GT international (5.1.3), 5 years, intermediate inspection between 2nd and 3rd anniversary, interim 6 months; DMLC Part I = competent authority, Part II = SHIPOWNER; inspection interval never exceeds 3 years (5.1.4), inspectors may prohibit sailing; 5.1.6 casualty inquiry. PORT (check): certificate + DMLC = PRIMA FACIE EVIDENCE → ordinary inspection is a document review; limited to Articles, Regulations, Part A ONLY; Art V(7) NO MORE FAVOURABLE TREATMENT. b) COMPLAINT PROCEDURES - TWO, NOT ONE. ON-BOARD 5.1.5: fair, effective, expeditious; lowest level is a PREFERENCE not a precondition - direct access to the master and to external authorities is preserved; copy to every seafarer + contacts + impartial adviser on board; may be accompanied; VICTIMIZATION PROHIBITED AND PENALIZED. ONSHORE 5.2.2: authorized officer in port → initial investigation → resolve on board first → notify FLAG STATE for a corrective plan → Director-General of the ILO; source confidential. Link: a complaint is one of the four triggers for a more detailed inspection. c) DETAINABLE - NO LIST IN THE CONVENTION, TWO GROUNDS: (1) conditions CLEARLY HAZARDOUS to safety, health or security; (2) SERIOUS OR REPEATED BREACH incl. seafarers' rights - no danger needed; unpaid wages can detain a perfectly sound ship. Reached via more detailed inspection on four triggers: documents bad/missing · clear grounds · flag changed to avoid compliance · complaint. After: rectify or accepted plan of action; flag State notified forthwith; compensation for undue detention. d) INDIA - THREE LAYERS: (1) the Convention's route above + direct access to the financial security provider (2014 amendments); (2) India is usually NOT the flag State → the head is reg 5.3 LABOUR-SUPPLYING + reg 1.4 licensed recruitment and placement, NO FEES to the seafarer, system of protection for monetary loss; (3) MS Act 1958 · Directorate General of Shipping · shipping master · courts and High Court admiralty in rem; unions and ITF = practical not law. NO circular, NO named cell, NO named forum, NO section number, NO ratification figure, NO detention statistic. DG Shipping, NOT DGMA - the rename is 37 months future.

Critical regulation
MLC 2006 Title 5, compliance and enforcement, as amended through the 2018 amendments. Regulation 5.1.1 general principles and the flag State's system of inspection and certification; 5.1.2 authorization of recognized organizations with the flag State remaining fully responsible; 5.1.3 the Maritime Labour Certificate and the Declaration of Maritime Labour Compliance, Part I by the competent authority and Part II by the shipowner; 5.1.4 inspection and enforcement, with an interval that shall in no case exceed three years and the power to prohibit sailing; 5.1.5 on-board complaint procedures with the prohibition on victimization; 5.1.6 marine casualties. Regulation 5.2.1 inspections in port, with the certificate and DMLC accepted as prima facie evidence, the four triggers for a more detailed inspection, and the two grounds for detention - conditions clearly hazardous to the safety, health or security of seafarers, or a serious or repeated breach of the requirements including seafarers' rights. Regulation 5.2.2 onshore seafarer complaint-handling procedures escalating to the flag State and to the Director-General of the ILO. Regulation 5.3 labour-supplying responsibilities, with Regulation 1.4 on recruitment and placement services. Article V(4) as the basis for inspecting a foreign ship, Article V(7) no more favourable treatment, and Article VI, whose substantial equivalence does NOT extend to Part A of the Code within Title 5.
Major trap
Two traps, and the first is fatal to the limb. Reading limb b) as printed. The paper prints 'Compliant Procedures'; the word is COMPLAINT, and MLC 2006 contains no such thing as a compliant procedure. Answering the printed word is answering nothing; the limb is Regulations 5.1.5 and 5.2.2. The second is temporal: writing the answer from the 2022 amendments. They are adopted but not in force at this sitting - 23 December 2024, twenty-two months away - and consolidated texts carrying them are in circulation and read as current. Appropriately sized PPE, drinking water as an amendment obligation, social connectivity, and repatriation of the remains of a deceased seafarer are all from that set and none is an obligation here. Two smaller traps: reversing DMLC Parts I and II (Part I is the authority's, Part II is the shipowner's), and spending half the paper on limb a), which is a complete sixteen-mark question elsewhere but is worth about four here.

Also on the platform: QP2302 Q2 - the III Code, where the same flag State and port State division governs a different Convention · QP2302 Q5 - the ISM Code, whose reporting channel is the safety-side analogue of the on-board complaint procedure · QP2302 Q6 - the bill of lading, where the same Indian statutory frame of 1958 governs the shipping side

First in set First set here. The same task returns at July 2024 Q6.

Understand this first

When a ship spills oil, somebody has to clean it up, and the cost is enormous. The question the law has to answer is not really who was careless - it is who pays, how quickly, and out of what money. Ordinary negligence law is hopeless at this. It requires the victim to prove fault, which may take years; it offers no assurance that the person at fault has any money; and by the time it produces an answer the coastline has been oiled for a decade. So the international response abandoned fault altogether and built something different. The shipowner is made liable whether or not he did anything wrong - that is what strict liability means - with only a handful of narrow escapes. In exchange for accepting liability he did not earn, he is allowed to cap what he pays. And because a cap on a company that may own one ship through a shell in another country is worthless, he is compelled to insure, must carry a certificate proving it, and the victim is allowed to sue the insurer directly. Strict liability, limitation, compulsory insurance and direct action: those four ideas are the whole design, and both conventions in this question are built from them. The difference between the two is a matter of history. The first scheme, in 1969 and then in the 1992 version this question names, was written after tanker disasters, so it was aimed at tankers - and it reaches only ships that are actually carrying persistent oil in bulk as cargo. That was a deliberate choice, and it left a hole that grew steadily wider as ships grew larger. A modern container ship carries no oil cargo at all, but it carries several thousand tonnes of heavy fuel oil in its own tanks, which is enough to devastate a coastline. Until 2001 there was no strict liability for that fuel, no compulsory insurance for it, and no certificate. The Bunker Convention was written to close that hole, and it was deliberately built to the same pattern so that the existing insurance machinery could simply be extended to it. That is why so much of this comparison comes out identical - the definition of pollution damage, the geographical reach, the defences, the certificate. But one difference runs through everything. Behind the tanker scheme sit further funds paid for not by shipowners but by the oil companies that receive the cargo, because in a tanker spill the cargo interest is a natural contributor. There is no equivalent for bunkers - nobody 'receives' a ship's fuel - so there is no second tier at all. The Bunker Convention does not even set a limit of its own; it points at the general limitation regime that applies to every other maritime claim, and the pollution claimant has to queue in the same fund as everyone else. That single structural asymmetry explains most of what this question is asking about.

Knowledge map
CLC 1992 Against the Bunker Convention 2001 - Ships, Oil and Damage, Limits of Liability, and Geographical Coverage
  1. framing
    1. 1Background - the gap the Bunker Convention was written to close
      • CLC reaches only ships carrying persistent oil in bulk as cargo
      • But a large container ship or bulk carrier burns and carries thousands of tonnes of fuel, and fell outside it
      • Before 2001: no strict liability, no compulsory insurance, no certificate for bunkers
  2. head 1
    1. 2Head 1 - the type of ships
      • CLC: any sea-going vessel ACTUALLY CARRYING persistent oil in bulk as CARGO - so a tanker in ballast is outside it
      • Combination carriers: inside only while carrying such cargo, and on the following voyage unless proved no residues remain
      • Bunkers: any seagoing vessel and seaborne craft of ANY TYPE WHATSOEVER - no cargo test at all
  3. head 2
    1. 3Head 2 - the oil covered, and the nature of the pollution damage
      • CLC oil = PERSISTENT hydrocarbon MINERAL oil - both requirements, kept distinct. Crude, fuel oil, heavy diesel, lubricating oil. Petrol, kerosene and light diesel are OUT
      • Bunker oil = ANY hydrocarbon mineral oil, INCLUDING LUBRICATING OIL, used or intended for the operation or propulsion of the ship, and any residues. Persistence is NOT required
      • The DAMAGE definition is essentially IDENTICAL in both - and saying so is the answer
  4. head 3
    1. 4Head 3 - limits of liability
      • Both: strict liability, the same three defences, and the right to limit lost on personal intent or recklessness with knowledge that such damage would probably result
      • CLC has its OWN limit - tonnage-banded, with an overall ceiling; the fund is constituted with the court and reserved to pollution claimants alone
      • And two further tiers behind it - the 1992 Fund and the Supplementary Fund, financed by cargo receivers, so ship and cargo share the burden
  5. head 4
    1. 5Head 4 - geographical coverage
      • Territory, including the territorial sea, of a State Party
      • The exclusive economic zone, or an equivalent area up to 200 nautical miles where none is declared
      • Preventive measures WHEREVER TAKEN - including on the high seas
  6. head 3
    1. 6The machinery under head 3 - channelling, insurance and direct action
      • CLC channels INWARD - liability on the registered owner ALONE, with claims barred against crew, pilot, charterer, manager, operator and salvor
      • Bunkers spreads OUTWARD - 'shipowner' includes registered owner, bareboat charterer, manager and operator, JOINTLY AND SEVERALLY
      • CLC insurance threshold is the CARGO - carrying more than 2,000 tons of persistent oil in bulk
  7. closing
    1. 7Conclusion - the one asymmetry that explains the rest
      • They agree on far more than they differ - by design, the later Convention being drafted on the earlier
      • Four differences: the ships · the oil · the channelling - and the structural one
      • CLC has two cargo-financed tiers behind it; the Bunker Convention has nothing
