Memory cue. ROUTE: (1) Background - the gap the Bunker Convention was written to close · (2) Head 1 - the type of ships · (3) Head 2 - the oil covered, and the nature of the pollution damage · (4) Head 3 - limits of liability · (5) Head 4 - geographical coverage · (6) The machinery under head 3 - channelling, insurance and direct action · (7) Conclusion - the one asymmetry that explains the rest

FOUR HEADS, NO LETTERING - COUNT THEM: SHIPS · OIL AND DAMAGE · LIMITS · GEOGRAPHY. BACKGROUND: CLC reaches only persistent oil in bulk as CARGO, so non-tankers' thousands of tonnes of fuel fell outside. Bunkers adopted 23 Mar 2001, in force 21 Nov 2008, deliberately on CLC's architecture. Map: CLC = tanker cargo oil · BUNKERS = every ship's fuel · HNS = other cargoes. 1) SHIPS. CLC = vessel ACTUALLY CARRYING persistent oil in bulk as cargo - tanker in ballast is OUT; combination carrier in only while carrying, and next voyage unless no residues proved. Bunkers = ANY seagoing vessel of any type whatsoever. Warships/State non-commercial out of both. NO OVERLAP - Bunkers excludes CLC pollution damage WHETHER OR NOT compensation is payable under CLC. 2) OIL. CLC = PERSISTENT AND HYDROCARBON MINERAL oil - both, don't collapse them - crude, fuel oil, heavy diesel, lube oil; petrol, kerosene, light diesel OUT. Bunkers = ANY hydrocarbon mineral oil INCLUDING LUBRICATING OIL used for operation or propulsion, plus residues - persistence NOT required. DAMAGE DEFINITION IS THE SAME IN BOTH: loss/damage outside the ship by contamination; environment limited to reasonable reinstatement actually undertaken or to be undertaken; plus preventive measures and loss caused by them. What differs is the OIL and the SHIP, not the DEFINITION. 3) LIMITS - STRUCTURE, NO NUMBERS. Both: strict liability, same three defences (war/exceptional natural phenomenon · third party acting with intent · negligence of the navigational-aid authority), and limit LOST on personal intent or recklessness with knowledge. CLC has its OWN tonnage-banded limit + overall ceiling, fund constituted with the court, reserved to pollution claimants, and TWO further tiers - 1992 Fund + Supplementary Fund, paid by CARGO RECEIVERS. BUNKERS SETS NO LIMIT - refers out to the applicable regime (in practice LLMC) → the bunker claim shares ONE general fund with every other claim, and there is NO SECOND TIER. 4) GEOGRAPHY - IDENTICAL. Territory incl. territorial sea + EEZ (or equivalent area to 200 n.m.) + preventive measures WHEREVER taken. The place of the DAMAGE decides - not the incident, not the flag. CHANNELLING: CLC funnels IN to the registered owner ALONE (crew, pilot, charterer, manager, operator, salvor protected); BUNKERS spreads OUT - registered owner + bareboat charterer + manager + operator, jointly and severally. INSURANCE: CLC threshold = the CARGO (over 2,000 tons persistent oil in bulk); BUNKERS threshold = the SHIP (over 1,000 GT). Both: certificate on board, P&I blue card, DIRECT ACTION against the insurer, enforced by port States on any flag.

Remember7 route headings
Cover34 core points beneath them

Memorise the 7 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 34 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. Background - the gap the Bunker Convention was written to close
  • CLC reaches only ships carrying persistent oil in bulk as cargo
  • But a large container ship or bulk carrier burns and carries thousands of tonnes of fuel, and fell outside it
  • Before 2001: no strict liability, no compulsory insurance, no certificate for bunkers
  • Adopted 23 March 2001, in force 21 November 2008; it deliberately borrows CLC's architecture
  • The map: CLC for tanker cargo oil · Bunkers for every ship's fuel · HNS for other cargoes
2. Head 1 - the type of ships
  • CLC: any sea-going vessel ACTUALLY CARRYING persistent oil in bulk as CARGO - so a tanker in ballast is outside it
  • Combination carriers: inside only while carrying such cargo, and on the following voyage unless proved no residues remain
  • Bunkers: any seagoing vessel and seaborne craft of ANY TYPE WHATSOEVER - no cargo test at all
  • Warships and State non-commercial ships excluded from both
  • No overlap: Bunkers excludes CLC pollution damage, whether or not compensation is payable under CLC
3. Head 2 - the oil covered, and the nature of the pollution damage
  • CLC oil = PERSISTENT hydrocarbon MINERAL oil - both requirements, kept distinct. Crude, fuel oil, heavy diesel, lubricating oil. Petrol, kerosene and light diesel are OUT
  • Bunker oil = ANY hydrocarbon mineral oil, INCLUDING LUBRICATING OIL, used or intended for the operation or propulsion of the ship, and any residues. Persistence is NOT required
  • The DAMAGE definition is essentially IDENTICAL in both - and saying so is the answer
  • Loss or damage outside the ship by contamination; environmental impairment limited to reasonable reinstatement actually undertaken or to be undertaken; plus preventive measures and loss caused by them
4. Head 3 - limits of liability
  • Both: strict liability, the same three defences, and the right to limit lost on personal intent or recklessness with knowledge that such damage would probably result
  • CLC has its OWN limit - tonnage-banded, with an overall ceiling; the fund is constituted with the court and reserved to pollution claimants alone
  • And two further tiers behind it - the 1992 Fund and the Supplementary Fund, financed by cargo receivers, so ship and cargo share the burden
  • Bunkers sets NO limit of its own - it refers out to the applicable national or international limitation regime, in practice the LLMC
  • Consequence: the bunker claim competes in ONE general limitation fund with every other claim from the incident, and there is NO second tier at all
  • No figure is given here, and the answer says why
5. Head 4 - geographical coverage
  • Territory, including the territorial sea, of a State Party
  • The exclusive economic zone, or an equivalent area up to 200 nautical miles where none is declared
  • Preventive measures WHEREVER TAKEN - including on the high seas
  • It is where the DAMAGE occurs that decides - not where the incident happened, and not the flag of the ship
6. The machinery under head 3 - channelling, insurance and direct action
  • CLC channels INWARD - liability on the registered owner ALONE, with claims barred against crew, pilot, charterer, manager, operator and salvor
  • Bunkers spreads OUTWARD - 'shipowner' includes registered owner, bareboat charterer, manager and operator, JOINTLY AND SEVERALLY
  • CLC insurance threshold is the CARGO - carrying more than 2,000 tons of persistent oil in bulk
  • Bunkers insurance threshold is the SHIP - over 1,000 gross tonnage
  • Both: a certificate carried on board, a P&I blue card behind it, and DIRECT ACTION against the insurer
  • Port States enforce the certificate on ships of ANY flag
7. Conclusion - the one asymmetry that explains the rest
  • They agree on far more than they differ - by design, the later Convention being drafted on the earlier
  • Four differences: the ships · the oil · the channelling - and the structural one
  • CLC has two cargo-financed tiers behind it; the Bunker Convention has nothing
  • Because a ship’s own fuel has no receiver to finance a tier. Every practical difference traces back to that
Model written answer

1. Background - the gap the Bunker Convention was written to close

The comparison cannot be explained without the history, so it comes first and briefly.

The International Convention on Civil Liability for Oil Pollution Damage, 1992 - CLC'92 - descends from the response to the tanker casualties of the 1960s. It was aimed at tankers, and it says so in its scope: it reaches ships actually carrying persistent oil in bulk as cargo.

That left a hole, and the hole grew. A large container ship or bulk carrier carries no oil cargo whatever, but she carries several thousand tonnes of heavy fuel oil in her own tanks - enough to devastate a coastline. Until 2001 there was no strict liability for that fuel, no compulsory insurance covering it and no certificate. A coastal State faced with a bunker spill from a non-tanker was thrown back on ordinary domestic law, with all its difficulties of proof and its uncertainty about whether the defendant had any money.

The International Convention on Civil Liability for Bunker Oil Pollution Damage, 2001 was adopted on 23 March 2001 and entered into force on 21 November 2008. It was written deliberately on CLC's architecture - strict liability, limitation, compulsory insurance, a certificate and direct action against the insurer - so that the existing P&I machinery could simply be extended to it rather than rebuilt.

The two instruments therefore complete a map, and stating the map at the outset frames the whole answer:

  • CLC'92 - persistent oil carried as cargo by a tanker.
  • Bunker Convention 2001 - the fuel of every other ship.
  • The HNS Convention - other hazardous and noxious cargoes, completing the scheme.

One asymmetry, introduced here and returned to at the end, explains most of the differences that follow: behind CLC there are further funds financed by the OIL CARGO INTERESTS. Behind the Bunker Convention there is nothing, because nobody 'receives' a ship's own fuel.

2. Head 1 - the type of ships

CLC'92Bunker Convention 2001
The testAny sea-going vessel or seaborne craft of any type ACTUALLY CARRYING persistent oil in bulk as CARGOAny seagoing vessel and seaborne craft of ANY TYPE WHATSOEVER - there is no cargo test at all
Tanker in ballastOUTSIDE the Convention - she is not carrying oil as cargoInside, like every other ship
Combination carrierInside only while carrying oil in bulk as cargo, and on any voyage following such carriage unless it is proved that no residues remainInside, always
Container ship, bulker, cruise ship, ferryOutsideInside - these are precisely the ships it was written for
Warships and State non-commercial shipsExcludedExcluded

The point that shows the test is understood, rather than the vessel class memorised: CLC does not apply to 'tankers'. It applies to a ship actually carrying the relevant cargo. A VLCC steaming in ballast is outside CLC entirely, and if she spills her own bunkers on that voyage, the claim lies under the Bunker Convention.

2.1 How the two are prevented from overlapping

The seam between the instruments is closed deliberately, and how it is closed is examinable:

The Bunker Convention expressly excludes pollution damage as defined in CLC - and it does so WHETHER OR NOT compensation is payable in respect of it under CLC.

  • So a laden tanker's bunker spill is a CLC matter, not a Bunker Convention matter: CLC covers oil escaping from such a ship whether it came from her cargo tanks or her fuel tanks.
  • And the exclusion bites even where nothing is actually recoverable under CLC on the facts. A claimant cannot fall back on the Bunker Convention because a CLC claim has failed.
  • The result is complementarity without double cover. Each incident falls under one regime, decided by the ship and her cargo, not by which tank the oil came out of.

3. Head 2 - the oil covered, and the nature of the pollution damage

This head has two halves and they give opposite answers. The oil is where the two conventions differ most sharply. The damage is where they are essentially identical. Saying both is the answer.

3.1 The oil - a sharp difference

CLC'92Bunker Convention 2001
DefinitionPERSISTENT hydrocarbon MINERAL oil - carried on board as cargo, or in the bunkers of such a shipANY hydrocarbon mineral oil, INCLUDING LUBRICATING OIL, used or intended to be used for the operation or propulsion of the ship, and any residues of it
Persistence required?YES - and it is the decisive limbNO
Typical examples inCrude oil, fuel oil, heavy diesel oil, lubricating oilHeavy fuel oil, marine diesel and gas oil, lubricating oil, and their residues and sludges
Typical examples outPetrol, kerosene, light diesel and other non-persistent oils; and any non-mineral oil such as a vegetable or animal oilNon-mineral oils; and oil carried as cargo, which is not bunker oil

Two requirements, kept distinct. CLC oil must be persistent AND it must be hydrocarbon mineral oil. These are separate tests and collapsing them is a standing error: a persistent vegetable oil is not CLC oil because it is not mineral, and petrol is not CLC oil because, although mineral, it is not persistent. Persistence means the oil does not readily evaporate or disperse and therefore remains to foul coastlines and require clean-up, which is exactly the harm the Convention was written for.

The Bunker Convention drops the persistence requirement altogether, and expressly includes lubricating oil. Its test is not what the oil is like but what it is for - the operation or propulsion of the ship.

3.2 The nature of the pollution damage - essentially identical

Here the comparison produces a sameness rather than a difference, and identifying that is a positive finding, not an omission. In both conventions, pollution damage means:

  1. Loss or damage caused OUTSIDE the ship by CONTAMINATION resulting from the escape or discharge of the oil, wherever the escape or discharge may occur. Damage to the ship herself is not pollution damage.
  2. Subject to an important restriction on environmental claims: compensation for impairment of the environment, other than loss of profit from such impairment, is limited to the costs of reasonable measures of reinstatement actually undertaken or to be undertaken. This is what stops an abstract claim for 'damage to the marine environment' assessed on some theoretical valuation; it must be tied to real remedial work.
  3. The costs of preventive measures - reasonable measures taken by any person after an incident to prevent or minimise pollution damage - and further loss or damage caused by those measures.

The conclusion for this head, and it is the sentence that earns the mark: the definition of pollution damage is common to the two conventions. What differs is the OIL that can trigger it and the SHIP that must be carrying it - not the damage itself. That is a direct consequence of the Bunker Convention having been deliberately drafted on CLC's model.

Two consequences worth noting, because they are common to both and are often assumed to differ: economic loss consequential on contamination - a fisherman's or hotelier's loss - is recoverable in principle under both, and pure economic loss unconnected with contamination is recoverable under neither; and in both, the reasonableness of preventive measures is the controlling test rather than their success.

4. Head 3 - limits of liability

A statement about what is not given, made positively so that the reader does not supply it. No monetary limit, no tonnage band, no unit-of-account figure and no currency conversion appears anywhere in this answer. The unit of account under both conventions is the Special Drawing Right, whose exchange value moves daily, and the figures themselves have been raised by amendment more than once. The comparison the stem asks for is structural, and the structure is where the real difference lies - the two conventions limit liability in fundamentally different ways, and that difference matters far more than either set of numbers.

4.1 What the two share

  • Strict liability. The shipowner is liable for pollution damage caused by the oil without proof of fault.
  • The same three defences, and they are narrow: damage resulting from an act of war, hostilities, civil war or insurrection, or an exceptional, inevitable and irresistible natural phenomenon; damage wholly caused by the act or omission of a third party done with intent to cause damage; and damage wholly caused by the negligence of a government or authority responsible for maintaining lights or other navigational aids. There is also a reduction where the claimant's own fault contributed.
  • A right to limit that can be lost. Under both, the shipowner loses the right to limit if the damage resulted from his personal act or omission, committed with intent to cause the damage, or recklessly and with knowledge that such damage would probably result. That is a very high threshold and it is rarely crossed - which is the point of it.

4.2 Where they differ - and this is the head's real content

CLC'92Bunker Convention 2001
Does the Convention set its own limit?YES. It contains its own limitation regime, banded by the ship's tonnage with an overall ceilingNO. It sets no figure at all
Where the limit comes fromThe Convention itselfIt refers out to the limitation regime applicable under national or international law - in practice the LLMC as amended
The fundA dedicated fund constituted with the court of a State Party, reserved exclusively to pollution claimantsThe general limitation fund for the incident, if one is constituted
Who competes for itPollution claimants onlyEvery other claimant from the same incident - cargo, property and other claims share it
What stands behind itTWO further tiers - the 1992 Fund and, for States that have joined it, the 2003 Supplementary FundNOTHING. There is no bunker equivalent of the IOPC Fund
Who pays the further tiersThe oil cargo receivers, by contribution - so the burden is shared between ship and cargoNot applicable

Three consequences follow, and they are the marks on this head:

  1. The bunker claimant may recover proportionately less from the same size of casualty, not because the Bunker Convention's limit is lower in itself, but because he is sharing one general fund with everybody else's claims while the CLC claimant has a fund to himself.
  2. The CLC claimant has two further layers of protection that the bunker claimant simply does not have. Where a tanker spill exceeds the shipowner's limit, the 1992 Fund and then the Supplementary Fund respond; where a bunker spill exceeds the general limit, the claimant's recovery stops.
  3. The reason for the asymmetry is not oversight but structure. The tanker tiers are financed by the interests that receive the oil cargo, and there is no equivalent interest behind a ship's own fuel. A cargo-financed fund for bunkers would have had no contributors.

5. Head 4 - geographical coverage

This head is short, and it is the one most often dropped because it comes last. The answer is that the geographical coverage of the two conventions is the same, and the reason is again that the later Convention was drafted on the earlier one's model. Both apply to:

  1. Pollution damage caused in the territory, including the territorial sea, of a State Party;
  2. Pollution damage caused in the exclusive economic zone of a State Party, or - where that State has not established an EEZ - in an equivalent area beyond and adjacent to its territorial sea, determined in accordance with international law and extending not more than 200 nautical miles from the baselines; and
  3. Preventive measures, WHEREVER TAKEN, to prevent or minimise such damage.

Three consequences, and each is a scoring point:

  • It is the place where the DAMAGE occurs that decides, not the place of the incident. A casualty on the high seas is within the Conventions so far as the oil reaches a State Party's territory, territorial sea or EEZ.
  • The flag of the ship is irrelevant to coverage. Neither Convention limits itself to ships of States Parties for the purpose of liability, and the compulsory insurance requirement is enforced by port States against ships of any flag - which is what stops the scheme being escaped by choice of registry.
  • Preventive measures are the deliberate exception to the geographical rule, and it is a rational one: the costs of measures taken on the high seas to stop oil reaching a coast are recoverable, because the whole object of the regime is to encourage those measures to be taken early rather than to wait until damage has occurred within the zone.

6. The machinery under head 3 - channelling, insurance and direct action

Two further contrasts sit under the liability head and neither should be omitted, because between them they are the most examinable difference on the whole question.

6.1 Channelling - CLC funnels liability IN, the Bunker Convention spreads it OUT

CLC'92Bunker Convention 2001
Who is liableThe REGISTERED OWNER ALONE'Shipowner' means the registered owner, the bareboat charterer, the manager AND the operator
OthersClaims are barred against the servants and agents, the crew, the pilot, the charterer, the manager, the operator, the salvor and those taking preventive measures - unless they acted with intent or recklessly with knowledgeAll of the named persons are JOINTLY AND SEVERALLY LIABLE
The effectThe claimant has one certain defendant and no need to investigate the commercial structure behind the shipThe claimant has more defendants to choose from, and may reach a solvent manager or operator behind an impecunious owner

The sentence that captures it: CLC channels liability inward to a single party so that the claimant always knows whom to sue; the Bunker Convention spreads it outward across several so that the claimant is more likely to find someone worth suing. Both are rational, and they solve the same problem from opposite ends.

6.2 Compulsory insurance, the certificate and direct action

  • The thresholds are set on different things, and this is a clean comparison point. The CLC insurance duty attaches by reference to the CARGO - a ship carrying more than 2,000 tons of persistent oil in bulk as cargo. The Bunker Convention's duty attaches by reference to the SHIP - a ship of more than 1,000 gross tonnage registered in a State Party.
  • Under the Bunker Convention the duty to insure falls on the REGISTERED OWNER alone, even though liability is spread across four categories of person. Liability is wide; the obligation to buy the cover is narrow.
  • Both require a certificate attesting that insurance or other financial security is in force, in the model form annexed to each Convention, carried on board and issued or certified by the flag State - behind which, in practice, stands a P&I club blue card.
  • Both give a DIRECT ACTION against the insurer. This is what makes the regime work: the claimant does not have to sue an offshore single-ship company and then try to enforce. The insurer may invoke the defences the shipowner could have invoked and may in addition limit even where the shipowner has lost the right to limit, but may not rely on defences arising from the owner's conduct towards him under the policy.
  • Enforcement is by port State control on ships of ANY flag, so a ship registered in a non-party State must still carry a certificate to trade to a State Party's ports. Without that rule the scheme could be avoided by choice of registry.

7. Conclusion - the one asymmetry that explains the rest

Set side by side, the two conventions agree far more than they differ. The same strict liability, the same three defences, the same test for losing the right to limit, the same definition of pollution damage, the same restriction of environmental claims to reasonable reinstatement actually undertaken, the same geographical reach, the same certificate and the same direct action against the insurer. That is by design: the Bunker Convention was drafted on CLC's model precisely so that the existing machinery could be extended rather than reinvented.

They differ at four identifiable points, and three of the four follow from the fourth:

  1. The ships - CLC reaches only the laden oil-carrying ship; the Bunker Convention reaches every ship.
  2. The oil - CLC requires persistence; the Bunker Convention does not, and expressly includes lubricating oil.
  3. The channelling - inward to one party against outward to four.
  4. And the structural one: CLC carries its own dedicated limit and has two cargo-financed tiers behind it, while the Bunker Convention sets no limit of its own, sends the claimant to the general limitation regime, and has nothing behind it at all.

The last of those is the important one. The tanker regime could be built in tiers because there is an identifiable cargo interest that receives the oil and can be made to contribute. A ship's own fuel has no receiver, so there was nobody to finance a second tier and none was created. Every practical difference in what a claimant actually recovers traces back to that single fact - and a comparison that identifies it has answered the question rather than listed the two instruments.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks and four required heads named in one sentence with no lettering: the type of ships, the oil and nature of the pollution damage, the limits of liability, and the geographical coverage. Number them in the margin before writing - the fourth is habitually dropped because it comes last and its answer is short, and it is a full quarter of the marks.

The word is COMPARE. Two consecutive descriptions of two conventions will not score like a comparison. Build each head as a two-column contrast - and be willing to report a sameness, because on two of the four heads the conventions are essentially identical, and saying so with the reason is the correct answer.

Count the heads before you write - there are four and no lettering

The stem is one sentence, but it names four required heads: the type of ships; the oil and nature of pollution damages covered; the limits of liability; and the geographical coverage. Write them in the margin before you start.

Geographical coverage is the one that gets dropped, because it is last and because the answer to it is short. It is a full quarter of the marks and it can be written in five lines. Write it.

Compare - do not describe twice

The word is compare. Two consecutive descriptions of two conventions is not a comparison and will not score like one. Use a table or a running two-column structure under each head, so that on every point the reader can see both positions at once.

And note that a comparison can properly produce a sameness. On two of the four heads - the nature of pollution damage, and the geographical coverage - the two conventions are essentially identical, and saying so with the reason is the correct answer, not an evasion.

Open with the gap - one paragraph, and it pays for itself

CLC reaches only ships actually carrying persistent oil in bulk as cargo. A container ship or bulker carries no oil cargo but carries thousands of tonnes of fuel, and until 2001 there was no strict liability, no compulsory insurance and no certificate for it. The Bunker Convention was adopted 23 March 2001, in force 21 November 2008, and was deliberately built on CLC's architecture so the existing P&I machinery could be extended.

Then give the map in one line: CLC for tanker cargo oil, Bunkers for every ship's fuel, HNS for other cargoes.

Head 1 - state the legal test, not the vessel type

CLC does not apply to 'tankers'. It applies to a ship actually carrying persistent oil in bulk as cargo - so a tanker in ballast is outside it, and a combination carrier is inside only while carrying such cargo and on the next voyage unless it is proved no residues remain. The Bunker Convention applies to any seagoing vessel of any type whatsoever, with no cargo test at all.

Then give the anti-overlap rule, which is where the mark is: the Bunker Convention excludes pollution damage as defined in CLC, whether or not compensation is actually payable under CLC. So a laden tanker's bunker spill is a CLC matter, and a claimant whose CLC claim fails cannot fall back on the Bunker Convention. Complementary, with no double cover and no gap.

Head 2 - keep CLC's two requirements apart

CLC oil is PERSISTENT and HYDROCARBON MINERAL oil. Two separate tests. A persistent vegetable oil fails the second; petrol fails the first. Collapsing them into 'persistent oil' is a standing error that costs marks.

Bunker oil is any hydrocarbon mineral oil, including lubricating oil, used or intended for the operation or propulsion of the ship, plus residues. No persistence requirement. Its test is what the oil is for, not what it is like.

Then switch and say that the damage definition is the same in both: loss or damage outside the ship by contamination; environmental impairment limited to the costs of reasonable measures of reinstatement actually undertaken or to be undertaken; plus preventive measures and loss caused by them. The line to write: 'What differs is the oil and the ship, not the definition of the damage.'

Head 3 - answer the structure, and say why you give no figures

Do not give any monetary limit, tonnage band, SDR figure or currency conversion. They move, they have been amended, and they are not what a comparison needs. Say so in one sentence - it reads as discipline, not as ignorance - and then give the structure, which is where the real difference is.

Shared: strict liability; the same three narrow defences (act of war or exceptional, inevitable and irresistible natural phenomenon; a third party acting with intent to cause damage; negligence of the authority responsible for navigational aids); and the right to limit lost on personal intent or recklessness with knowledge that such damage would probably result.

Different, and this is the heart of the question: CLC has its own tonnage-banded limit, forms a dedicated fund with the court reserved to pollution claimants, and has two further tiers behind it - the 1992 Fund and the Supplementary Fund, financed by cargo receivers. The Bunker Convention sets no limit of its own, refers out to the applicable limitation regime (in practice the LLMC), so the bunker claim shares one general fund with every other claim from the incident, and there is no second tier at all.

Head 4 - short, and identical in both

Territory including the territorial sea of a State Party; the EEZ, or an equivalent area not more than 200 nautical miles from the baselines where no EEZ has been declared; and preventive measures wherever taken.

Then the three consequences: it is the place of the damage that decides, not the place of the incident; the flag is irrelevant, and port States enforce the certificate on ships of any flag; and preventive measures are covered anywhere, including the high seas, because the regime wants them taken early.

Do not omit the channelling contrast

It sits under the liability head and it is the sharpest single difference between the two instruments. CLC channels liability INWARD to the registered owner alone, barring claims against crew, pilot, charterer, manager, operator and salvor. The Bunker Convention spreads it OUTWARD - registered owner, bareboat charterer, manager and operator, jointly and severally.

One sentence captures both: CLC gives the claimant one certain defendant; the Bunker Convention gives him more defendants to choose from.

And the insurance thresholds, which are a clean comparison point

CLC's threshold is the CARGO - more than 2,000 tons of persistent oil in bulk. The Bunker Convention's is the SHIP - over 1,000 gross tonnage. Both require a certificate carried on board with a P&I blue card behind it, and both give direct action against the insurer, who may limit even where the shipowner cannot.

Close on the asymmetry - it is what turns a list into an answer

The two conventions agree on almost everything because the later was drafted on the earlier. The one structural difference is that CLC has two cargo-financed tiers behind it and the Bunker Convention has nothing. And the reason is not oversight: there is an identifiable interest that receives an oil cargo and can be made to contribute, and there is no equivalent interest behind a ship's own fuel. A closing paragraph that makes that point has compared the instruments rather than listed them.

Common mistakes

  • Do not give any SDR figure, tonnage band, limitation amount, fund ceiling or currency conversion. Neither treaty text is held and the figures move.
  • Do not quote any Article of either Convention word for word.
  • Do not cite case authority or name a reported casualty. None is held.
  • Do not state how many States are party to either Convention, or assert India's party status - the stem sets no Indian limb.
  • Do not collapse CLC's two oil requirements into one.
  • Do not say the two conventions can both apply to one incident. The exclusion is express and absolute.
  • Do not import the time limits for claims. They are a good topic and they are not one of this paper's four heads.
  • Do not drop the geographical head because it is short.

Examiner traps

  • "Does CLC apply to tankers?" It applies to a ship actually carrying persistent oil in bulk as cargo - so a tanker in ballast is outside it.
  • "Can both conventions apply to one spill?" No. The Bunker Convention excludes CLC pollution damage whether or not compensation is payable under CLC.
  • "What oil does CLC cover?" Persistent AND hydrocarbon mineral - two separate tests. Petrol fails the first; a vegetable oil fails the second.
  • "Is lubricating oil covered by the Bunker Convention?" Yes, expressly - and persistence is not required.
  • "How do the pollution damage definitions differ?" They do not, in substance. What differs is the oil and the ship.
  • "What is the Bunker Convention's limit of liability?" It has none of its own. It refers out to the applicable regime, in practice the LLMC - so the claim shares one general fund with every other claim from the incident.
  • "What stands behind each?" CLC has two further tiers financed by cargo receivers. The Bunker Convention has nothing.
  • "How does channelling differ?" CLC funnels inward to the registered owner alone; the Bunker Convention spreads outward across registered owner, bareboat charterer, manager and operator, jointly and severally.
  • "What decides whether a spill is covered?" The place where the damage occurs - not the place of the incident, and not the flag of the ship.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Which certificates on your ship relate to pollution liability, and who issued them?
  • What is a blue card, and who gives it?
  • Your bulk carrier grounds and loses fuel oil. Which convention applies, and who pays?
  • The same happens to a laden tanker. Does the answer change, and why?
  • What is the practical difference to a claimant between a dedicated fund and a general limitation fund?
  • Why is there no bunker equivalent of the IOPC Fund?

Memory framework

Background, then the four printed heads, then the machinery, then the asymmetry. BACKGROUND (1): CLC reaches only persistent oil carried as cargo, so every other ship's fuel had no regime until 2001, in force 2008. HEAD 1 SHIPS (2): actually carrying against any vessel whatsoever, and the exclusion that stops overlap. HEAD 2 OIL AND DAMAGE (3): persistent + mineral against any mineral oil including lube oil - and the damage definition is the same. HEAD 3 LIMITS (4): CLC has its own limit, its own fund and two cargo-financed tiers; Bunkers has none of the three. HEAD 4 GEOGRAPHY (5): identical - territory, territorial sea, EEZ, and preventive measures wherever taken. MACHINERY (6): channelling in against out; insurance threshold cargo against ship; direct action. CLOSE (7): a ship's own fuel has no receiver, so there is no second tier.

Uncertainty, and why no figure appears under a head that names limits

Neither treaty text is held by the corpus, which classifies this whole family as citation and index only, with verified status facts and no text. Every article-level statement in the answer is INTERNAL REUSE VERIFIED - reuse of an MIW record of a full-text reading made in another session - and none is P1. No article is quoted verbatim and the content is given by substance throughout.

No SDR figure, tonnage band, limitation amount, fund ceiling or currency conversion appears anywhere, even though the printed stem names 'limits of liability' as a required head. That head is answered to the structure of limitation, which is what a comparison requires in any event and is more discriminating than a pair of numbers. The answer says so positively, so that a candidate does not supply a figure of their own.

This is an evidence decision and not a temporal one, and the distinction is recorded so that a later reader does not mistake it. The CLC figures the donor states derive from amendments in force since 2003, twenty years before this sitting, and are temporally perfectly safe. They are excluded because the treaty texts are not held, not because they are out of date.

Temporal risk is otherwise LOW and structurally so. CLC 1992 entered into force on 30 May 1996 and the Bunker Convention on 21 November 2008 - twenty-seven and fourteen years before this sitting - and no amendment, protocol or entry into force falls in the two-month window to the donor. No case authority is cited and no reported casualty is named; India's party status is not asserted, the stem setting no Indian limb; and the number of States party to either convention is not stated, because it dates.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________framing Background - the gap the Bunker Convention was written to close
  2. __________head 1 Head 1 - the type of ships
  3. __________head 2 Head 2 - the oil covered, and the nature of the pollution damage
  4. __________head 3 Head 3 - limits of liability
  5. __________head 4 Head 4 - geographical coverage
  6. __________head 3 The machinery under head 3 - channelling, insurance and direct action
  7. __________closing Conclusion - the one asymmetry that explains the rest
Flashcards 15

0 the background - the gap the Bunker Convention closed. 1 type of ships, plus the anti-overlap rule. 2 the oil (sharply different) and the nature of the damage (identical). 3 limits of liability, answered structurally. 4 geographical coverage, identical. 5 channelling and compulsory insurance. 6 close on the missing second tier.

Four heads are printed in continuous prose with no lettering, and the last one is habitually dropped.

CLC reaches only ships actually carrying persistent oil in bulk as cargo. A container ship or bulk carrier carries no oil cargo but several thousand tonnes of fuel, and until 2001 there was no strict liability for it, no compulsory insurance and no certificate. Adopted 23 March 2001, in force 21 November 2008, deliberately on CLC's architecture.

Nothing in the comparison is intelligible without it.

CLC - any sea-going vessel ACTUALLY CARRYING persistent oil in bulk as cargo, so a tanker in ballast is outside it, and a combination carrier is inside only while carrying such cargo and on the following voyage unless no residues are proved. Bunkers - any seagoing vessel and seaborne craft of ANY TYPE WHATSOEVER, with no cargo test.

CLC applies by cargo carried, not by vessel class; stating it as 'tankers' loses the head.

The Bunker Convention expressly excludes pollution damage as defined in CLC, WHETHER OR NOT compensation is payable under CLC. So a laden tanker's bunker spill is a CLC matter, and a claimant whose CLC claim fails cannot fall back on the Bunker Convention. Complementary, with no double cover and no gap.

The 'whether or not payable' words are the whole of the mechanism and are usually missed.

CLC - PERSISTENT hydrocarbon MINERAL oil; both requirements, kept distinct. Crude, fuel oil, heavy diesel, lubricating oil in; petrol, kerosene and light diesel out for lack of persistence; vegetable and animal oils out for lack of mineral origin. Bunkers - ANY hydrocarbon mineral oil INCLUDING lubricating oil, used or intended for the operation or propulsion of the ship, and residues; persistence not required.

Collapsing CLC's two requirements into one is a standing error.

It does not, in substance. Both cover loss or damage caused outside the ship by contamination; both limit compensation for impairment of the environment to the costs of reasonable measures of reinstatement actually undertaken or to be undertaken; and both cover preventive measures and further loss caused by them. What differs is the oil and the ship, not the definition.

A comparison can properly produce a sameness, and identifying it is the answer to half of head 2.

Because otherwise a claim for 'damage to the marine environment' could be advanced on a theoretical valuation with no remedial work behind it. Both conventions tie recovery to the costs of reasonable measures of reinstatement actually undertaken or to be undertaken - loss of profit from the impairment remains recoverable separately.

It is the same restriction in both and it is the substantive content of the damage definition.

Damage resulting from an act of war, hostilities, civil war or insurrection, or an exceptional, inevitable and irresistible natural phenomenon; damage wholly caused by an act or omission of a third party done WITH INTENT to cause damage; and damage wholly caused by the negligence of a government or authority responsible for maintaining navigational aids. Contributory fault of the claimant reduces liability.

They are identical in both instruments and the 'wholly' and 'with intent' qualifications are what make them narrow.

CLC has its OWN tonnage-banded limit with an overall ceiling, forming a dedicated fund constituted with the court and reserved exclusively to pollution claimants, with the 1992 Fund and the Supplementary Fund behind it. The Bunker Convention sets NO limit of its own; it refers out to the applicable limitation regime, in practice the LLMC, so the claim shares one general fund with every other claim from the incident - and there is no second tier at all.

This is the heart of the question and it is answerable, and better answered, without any figure.

Because the tanker tiers are financed by the interests that RECEIVE the oil cargo, and a ship's own fuel has no receiver. A cargo-financed fund for bunkers would have had no contributors. It is a structural consequence, not an oversight.

It explains most of the practical differences and turns a list into a comparison.

CLC channels liability INWARD to the registered owner alone, barring claims against servants and agents, crew, pilot, charterer, manager, operator and salvor unless they acted with intent or recklessly with knowledge. The Bunker Convention spreads it OUTWARD - 'shipowner' includes the registered owner, bareboat charterer, manager and operator, jointly and severally liable.

The sharpest single difference: CLC gives one certain defendant, Bunkers gives more defendants to choose from.

CLC's threshold is the CARGO - a ship carrying more than 2,000 tons of persistent oil in bulk as cargo. The Bunker Convention's is the SHIP - more than 1,000 gross tonnage. Under Bunkers the duty to insure falls on the registered owner alone, even though liability is spread across four categories of person.

A clean comparison point, and the liability-wide-but-duty-narrow observation shows the scheme understood.

Compulsory insurance evidenced by a certificate carried on board, with a P&I blue card behind it, and DIRECT ACTION against the insurer - who may invoke the shipowner's defences and may limit even where the shipowner has lost the right to limit. Port States enforce the certificate on ships of any flag.

Without direct action the claimant would be suing an offshore single-ship company and enforcing against nothing.

Identical: damage in the territory including the territorial sea of a State Party; damage in the exclusive economic zone, or an equivalent area not more than 200 nautical miles from the baselines where no EEZ is declared; and preventive measures WHEREVER taken, including on the high seas.

A short head worth a full quarter of the marks, and the one most often dropped.

The place where the DAMAGE occurs decides, not the place of the incident. A high-seas casualty is covered so far as the oil reaches a State Party's territory, territorial sea or EEZ. The flag of the ship is irrelevant, and port States enforce the certificate on ships of any flag, so the scheme cannot be escaped by choice of registry.

It is the examinable consequence of the geographical head.

Quick revision
15-second recall
FOUR HEADS, NO LETTERING: SHIPS · OIL AND DAMAGE · LIMITS · GEOGRAPHY. BACKGROUND: CLC reaches only ships actually carrying persistent oil in bulk as CARGO, so non-tankers' thousands of tonnes of fuel had no strict liability, no compulsory insurance, no certificate. Bunkers adopted 23 Mar 2001, in force 21 Nov 2008, built on CLC's architecture. Map: CLC = tanker cargo oil · BUNKERS = every ship's fuel · HNS = other cargoes. 1) SHIPS. CLC = vessel ACTUALLY CARRYING persistent oil in bulk as cargo → tanker in ballast is OUT; combination carrier in only while carrying + next voyage unless no residues proved. BUNKERS = any seagoing vessel of ANY TYPE WHATSOEVER, no cargo test. Warships / State non-commercial out of both. ANTI-OVERLAP: Bunkers excludes CLC pollution damage WHETHER OR NOT compensation is payable under CLC → laden tanker's bunker spill = CLC; failed CLC claim cannot fall back on Bunkers. 2a) OIL. CLC = PERSISTENT AND HYDROCARBON MINERAL - two separate tests, do not collapse. In: crude, fuel oil, heavy diesel, lube oil. Out: petrol, kerosene, light diesel (not persistent) and vegetable/animal oils (not mineral). BUNKERS = ANY hydrocarbon mineral oil INCLUDING LUBRICATING OIL used or intended for the operation or propulsion of the ship, plus residues. No persistence needed. 2b) DAMAGE - THE SAME IN BOTH. Loss or damage OUTSIDE the ship by CONTAMINATION; environmental impairment limited to costs of reasonable reinstatement ACTUALLY UNDERTAKEN OR TO BE UNDERTAKEN; plus preventive measures and further loss caused by them. What differs is the OIL and the SHIP, not the DEFINITION. 3) LIMITS - STRUCTURE ONLY, NO FIGURES. BOTH: strict liability; three defences - war/hostilities/insurrection or exceptional, inevitable and irresistible natural phenomenon · third party act wholly with INTENT · negligence of the navigational-aid authority; limit LOST on personal intent or recklessness with knowledge that such damage would probably result. CLC: its OWN tonnage-banded limit + overall ceiling; fund constituted with the court, RESERVED TO POLLUTION CLAIMANTS; TWO TIERS BEHIND - 1992 Fund + 2003 Supplementary Fund, financed by CARGO RECEIVERS. BUNKERS: NO LIMIT OF ITS OWN - refers out to the applicable regime, in practice LLMC → shares ONE GENERAL FUND with every other claim from the incident → NO SECOND TIER AT ALL. 4) GEOGRAPHY - IDENTICAL. Territory incl. territorial sea + EEZ (or equivalent area ≤ 200 n.m. from baselines where none declared) + PREVENTIVE MEASURES WHEREVER TAKEN. The place of the DAMAGE decides - not the incident, not the flag. CHANNELLING: CLC funnels IN → registered owner ALONE, claims barred against crew, pilot, charterer, manager, operator, salvor (unless intent/recklessness). BUNKERS spreads OUT → registered owner + bareboat charterer + manager + operator, JOINTLY AND SEVERALLY. INSURANCE: CLC threshold = CARGO (> 2,000 tons persistent oil in bulk); Bunkers threshold = SHIP (> 1,000 GT), duty on the registered owner alone. Both: certificate on board, P&I blue card, DIRECT ACTION against the insurer (who may limit even where the owner cannot), enforced by port States on ANY flag. CLOSING: the one asymmetry is the missing second tier - a ship's fuel has no 'receiver' to finance one.

Critical numbers

  • Bunker Convention - ADOPTED 23 MARCH 2001, IN FORCE 21 NOVEMBER 2008, fourteen years before this sitting
  • FOUR heads printed with no lettering - type of ships; oil and nature of pollution damage; limits of liability; geographical coverage
  • CLC compulsory insurance threshold is the CARGO - more than 2,000 tons of persistent oil in bulk as cargo
  • Bunker Convention compulsory insurance threshold is the SHIP - more than 1,000 gross tonnage
  • Geographical reach in BOTH - territory including territorial sea, the EEZ or an equivalent area not exceeding 200 NAUTICAL MILES from the baselines, and preventive measures WHEREVER taken
  • THREE defences in both; TWO further compensation tiers behind CLC; NONE behind the Bunker Convention

Critical regulation
International Convention on Civil Liability for Oil Pollution Damage, 1992 (CLC'92), in force 30 May 1996 - strict liability of the registered owner for pollution damage from persistent hydrocarbon mineral oil carried in bulk as cargo, with liability channelled to that owner alone, a tonnage-banded limitation regime of its own constituting a fund reserved to pollution claimants, compulsory insurance for ships carrying more than 2,000 tons of such oil in bulk, a certificate and direct action against the insurer; backed by the 1992 Fund Convention and the 2003 Supplementary Fund Protocol, financed by oil cargo receivers. International Convention on Civil Liability for Bunker Oil Pollution Damage, 2001, adopted 23 March 2001 and in force 21 November 2008 - the same architecture applied to the bunker oil of any seagoing vessel of any type whatsoever, with 'bunker oil' defined as any hydrocarbon mineral oil including lubricating oil used or intended for the operation or propulsion of the ship, liability spread jointly and severally across the registered owner, bareboat charterer, manager and operator, express exclusion of CLC pollution damage whether or not compensation is payable under CLC, NO limitation regime of its own but a reference out to the applicable regime in practice the LLMC, compulsory insurance for ships over 1,000 gross tonnage, a certificate and direct action; and NO second-tier fund.
Major trap
Two traps. Describing the two conventions one after the other instead of comparing them, and in particular missing that on two of the four printed heads - the nature of the pollution damage, and the geographical coverage - they are essentially identical, which is the correct answer and not an omission. And collapsing CLC's two oil requirements: the oil must be persistent AND hydrocarbon mineral, so a persistent vegetable oil is out and petrol is out, for different reasons. Two smaller ones: supplying a monetary limit or SDR figure for either convention - the comparison is structural, and the decisive difference is that CLC has its own dedicated fund and two cargo-financed tiers behind it while the Bunker Convention has neither; and dropping the geographical head because it is printed last and is short.

Also on the platform: QP2302 Q4 - salvage, where the salvor's environmental role and the Article 14 payer sit alongside this liability regime · QP2302 Q2 - the III Code, where the flag State's certification duty appears in the form of these compulsory insurance certificates · QP2302 Q6 - the bill of lading, where the cargo interests behind a CLC spill hold their title to the oil

First in set First set here. The same task returns at April 2023 Q7, February 2024 Q2, September 2024 Q6, September 2025 Q7.

The same boundary makes PSC procedures A.1155(32), not A.1185(33). The second trap is answering the coastal State limb with UNCLOS maritime zones instead of the services the Code actually requires.
Q3 EEXI Design Compliance Measures, and the CII Rating with AER and EEOI
16 marks		 Where EEXI sits in law - and what the printed Phase 2 framing refers to → Hull and propulsive design measures → Machinery and energy-recovery measures → Power limitation - what most existing ships actually did → Limb b) - the concept of the CII rating → AER and EEOI critically compared - the denominator is the whole argument → Measures to improve the rating - operational → Measures to improve the rating - technical, and the SEEMP as the instrument MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 as revised by MEPC.328(76): regulation 23 attained EEXI, regulation 24 and Table 1 required EEDI with the PHASED structure, regulation 25 and Table 3 required EEXI banded by type and size, regulation 26 SEEMP, regulation 27 fuel oil consumption data collection, regulation 28 operational carbon intensity and the rating.
MEPC.328(76) IN FORCE 1 NOVEMBER 2022 - read from the resolution's own operative paragraph 3. The corpus register's 2023-11-01 is WRONG and is carried as TSCR-3		 Adopting the stem's 'Phase 2 (20% - 30% reduction)' framing and then describing the EEDI table. The phased structure is regulation 24 Table 1 for NEW ships; EEXI takes its factor from regulation 25 Table 3, banded by type and size and NOT phased. The second trap is temporal and comes from the twenty-month-later donor: describing ships as having been rated A to E. At February 2023 the first rating year was six weeks old and no ship held a rating. The third is saying power limitation improves the CII - it does not, unless it actually forces a lower operating speed.
Q4 Maritime Against Contractual Salvage, Liability for the Award, and Lloyd's Open Form
16 marks		 The three preconditions - what makes any claim a salvage claim → Demand 1 - the difference between maritime salvage and contractual salvage → Demand 2 - simple examples → Demand 3 - who is responsible for payment of the award → Demand 4 - the form popularly used → Demand 5 - how the amount of the award is calculated under the form → The environmental exception - which qualifies the answers above International Convention on Salvage, 1989. The three preconditions of danger, voluntariness and success; Article 8, the duties of the salvor and of the owner and master; Article 13, the criteria for fixing the reward and the proportionate liability of the salved interests according to salved value; Article 14, special compensation where the environment was threatened, payable by the shipowner.
International Convention on Salvage, 1989 - IN FORCE 14 July 1996, twenty-seven years before this sitting		 Answering demand 3 with 'the shipowner'. The award is payable by the owners of ALL the property saved, each in proportion to the saved value of its own interest - so on a laden ship the cargo interests pay their share, which may exceed the ship's. Saying 'the insurers' is also wrong: they indemnify behind the liability, they are not the party responsible. The second trap is distinguishing pure from contractual salvage by whether money was paid rather than by whether there was a prior agreement.
Q5 Effective Communication under the ISM Code, Engine Resource Management, and Communication Failure
16 marks		 What the ISM Code actually says about communication → The concept those provisions create → The principles of Engine Resource Management → Applying the principles on board → Applying the principles with outsiders → Two examples of occurrences caused mainly by communication failure → The assessment and monitoring machinery that closes the loop The ISM Code, as amended through MSC.353(92). Its communication provisions taken together: the working language requirement and the ability of personnel to communicate effectively in the execution of their duties; familiarisation of new and transferred personnel; the reporting, investigation and analysis of non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences with corrective action; the master's responsibility to issue orders in a clear and simple manner and to review the system and report deficiencies ashore; and the designated person ashore having direct access to the highest level of management.
ISM Code as amended through MSC.353(92) - operative at this sitting and unchanged between this paper and its donor		 Writing general communication theory - sender, channel, receiver, feedback, noise - when the stem says 'as mentioned in the ISM Code'. The second trap is running out of answer before the two examples, which the unlettered layout makes easy and which costs roughly a third of the marks. The third is dropping 'and with outsiders', which is half of the second demand. And do not invent a Code definition of effective communication: there is none, and saying so scores.
Q6 Bill of Lading - Main Function, Why It Is Made Out 'To Order', and When It Becomes the Contract
16 marks		 Limb (a), first demand - the functions of a bill of lading, and which is the main one → Limb (a), second demand - what an order bill is, and how it operates → Limb (a), second demand - the reasons for making a bill out 'to order' → Limb (b) - why the bill is normally only EVIDENCE of the contract → Limb (b) - the circumstances in which the bill BECOMES the actual contract → The statutory frame operative at this sitting The Hague-Visby Rules as given effect in India by the Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993 - the carrier's duty of due diligence before and at the beginning of the voyage, the duty to care properly for the cargo, the required contents of the bill as receipt including the apparent order and condition of the goods, the escalation of that statement from prima facie to conclusive on transfer to a good faith third party, and the nullity of any clause relieving or lessening the carrier's liability. With the Indian Bills of Lading Act, 1856, whose first section vests in the consignee or endorsee all rights of suit as if the contract contained in the bill of lading had been made with himself, and the Indian Sale of Goods Act, 1930, which includes the bill within documents of title to goods.
THREE functions - receipt, evidence of the contract, document of title. The MAIN one is document of title		 Writing the answer on the Bills of Lading Act 2025 and the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act 2025. Both commenced 10 September 2025 - thirty-one months after this sitting - and the corpus's own October 2025 bill of lading answer is written entirely on them, with renumbered Schedule articles. At February 2023 the operative statutes are the Indian Bills of Lading Act, 1856 and the Indian Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993. The second trap is answering limb (b) by repeating the three functions: the question asks when the bill stops being evidence and becomes the contract, and the answer is on transfer to a third party who was never part of the original bargain. The third is treating the printed proposition in limb (b) as a statement to be refuted rather than a general rule whose exceptions are wanted.
Q7 High-Efficiency Propellers - Ducted, Kappel, Contra-Rotating and Azimuth, with Merits and Demerits
16 marks		 Why propeller efficiency is being pursued - the framing the stem supplies → Where the losses occur - the map the rest of the answer hangs on → (a) Ducted propellers - an attack on the axial loss → (b) Kappel propellers - an attack on the tip vortex loss → (c) Contra-rotating propellers - an attack on the rotational loss → (d) Azimuth propulsion - an attack on hull-side and appendage losses → Comparative judgement, and the instruction printed in the stem MARPOL Annex VI as revised by resolution MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022. Chapter 4 applies from 1 January 2023: regulations 23 and 25 require an existing ship's attained EEXI not to exceed the required EEXI, and regulation 28 requires the annual operational carbon intensity indicator to be determined and rated A to E, with a corrective action plan where a ship is rated D for three consecutive years or E in any year. The enhanced SEEMP, including the Part III carbon intensity plan, is required on board from the same date. The operative policy instrument is the Initial IMO Strategy on reduction of GHG emissions from ships, 2018 - NOT the 2023 Strategy, which is five months future at this sitting. No instrument prescribes any propeller type or design concept; the regulations create the driver, not the design.
Revised MARPOL Annex VI, resolution MEPC.328(76) - ENTERED INTO FORCE 1 NOVEMBER 2022, three months before this sitting		 Answering all four devices when the stem asks for any three, and giving merits only when it asks for merits and demerits. On the engineering, the trap is presenting the ducted propeller as an unqualified improvement: its benefit rises with thrust loading and on a fast, lightly loaded ship the duct's own drag makes the ship worse. On the framing, the trap is importing later law - the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy (five months future), the EU ETS extension to shipping (eleven months future) and any Net-Zero Framework material (thirty-two months future) - and, subtler, writing that ships have been rated A to E when at this sitting the first rating year was six weeks old and no ship held a rating at all.
Q8 MLC 2006 - Flag and Port State Responsibilities, Complaint Procedures, Detainable Deficiencies, and Indian Grievance Redressal
16 marks		 Which edition applies, and how the Convention is built → Limb a) - Flag State and Port State responsibilities → Limb b) - the on-board and onshore COMPLAINT procedures → Limb c) - detainable deficiencies → Limb d) - grievance redressal mechanisms for Indian seafarers → Closing - the thread that joins limbs b) and d) MLC 2006 Title 5, compliance and enforcement, as amended through the 2018 amendments. Regulation 5.1.1 general principles and the flag State's system of inspection and certification; 5.1.2 authorization of recognized organizations with the flag State remaining fully responsible; 5.1.3 the Maritime Labour Certificate and the Declaration of Maritime Labour Compliance, Part I by the competent authority and Part II by the shipowner; 5.1.4 inspection and enforcement, with an interval that shall in no case exceed three years and the power to prohibit sailing; 5.1.5 on-board complaint procedures with the prohibition on victimization; 5.1.6 marine casualties. Regulation 5.2.1 inspections in port, with the certificate and DMLC accepted as prima facie evidence, the four triggers for a more detailed inspection, and the two grounds for detention - conditions clearly hazardous to the safety, health or security of seafarers, or a serious or repeated breach of the requirements including seafarers' rights. Regulation 5.2.2 onshore seafarer complaint-handling procedures escalating to the flag State and to the Director-General of the ILO. Regulation 5.3 labour-supplying responsibilities, with Regulation 1.4 on recruitment and placement services. Article V(4) as the basis for inspecting a foreign ship, Article V(7) no more favourable treatment, and Article VI, whose substantial equivalence does NOT extend to Part A of the Code within Title 5.
MLC 2006 - IN FORCE 20 AUGUST 2013		 Two traps, and the first is fatal to the limb. Reading limb b) as printed. The paper prints 'Compliant Procedures'; the word is COMPLAINT, and MLC 2006 contains no such thing as a compliant procedure. Answering the printed word is answering nothing; the limb is Regulations 5.1.5 and 5.2.2. The second is temporal: writing the answer from the 2022 amendments. They are adopted but not in force at this sitting - 23 December 2024, twenty-two months away - and consolidated texts carrying them are in circulation and read as current. Appropriately sized PPE, drinking water as an amendment obligation, social connectivity, and repatriation of the remains of a deceased seafarer are all from that set and none is an obligation here. Two smaller traps: reversing DMLC Parts I and II (Part I is the authority's, Part II is the shipowner's), and spending half the paper on limb a), which is a complete sixteen-mark question elsewhere but is worth about four here.
Q9 CLC 1992 Against the Bunker Convention 2001 - Ships, Oil and Damage, Limits of Liability, and Geographical Coverage
16 marks		 Background - the gap the Bunker Convention was written to close → Head 1 - the type of ships → Head 2 - the oil covered, and the nature of the pollution damage → Head 3 - limits of liability → Head 4 - geographical coverage → The machinery under head 3 - channelling, insurance and direct action → Conclusion - the one asymmetry that explains the rest International Convention on Civil Liability for Oil Pollution Damage, 1992 (CLC'92), in force 30 May 1996 - strict liability of the registered owner for pollution damage from persistent hydrocarbon mineral oil carried in bulk as cargo, with liability channelled to that owner alone, a tonnage-banded limitation regime of its own constituting a fund reserved to pollution claimants, compulsory insurance for ships carrying more than 2,000 tons of such oil in bulk, a certificate and direct action against the insurer; backed by the 1992 Fund Convention and the 2003 Supplementary Fund Protocol, financed by oil cargo receivers. International Convention on Civil Liability for Bunker Oil Pollution Damage, 2001, adopted 23 March 2001 and in force 21 November 2008 - the same architecture applied to the bunker oil of any seagoing vessel of any type whatsoever, with 'bunker oil' defined as any hydrocarbon mineral oil including lubricating oil used or intended for the operation or propulsion of the ship, liability spread jointly and severally across the registered owner, bareboat charterer, manager and operator, express exclusion of CLC pollution damage whether or not compensation is payable under CLC, NO limitation regime of its own but a reference out to the applicable regime in practice the LLMC, compulsory insurance for ships over 1,000 gross tonnage, a certificate and direct action; and NO second-tier fund.
CLC 1992 - IN FORCE 30 MAY 1996, twenty-seven years before this sitting		 Two traps. Describing the two conventions one after the other instead of comparing them, and in particular missing that on two of the four printed heads - the nature of the pollution damage, and the geographical coverage - they are essentially identical, which is the correct answer and not an omission. And collapsing CLC's two oil requirements: the oil must be persistent AND hydrocarbon mineral, so a persistent vegetable oil is out and petrol is out, for different reasons. Two smaller ones: supplying a monetary limit or SDR figure for either convention - the comparison is structural, and the decisive difference is that CLC has its own dedicated fund and two cargo-financed tiers behind it while the Bunker Convention has neither; and dropping the geographical head because it is printed last and is short.
